APTECH • CREATING SERVICES FOR THE WEB • AUGUST 2024 MANUAL

Session 1. Start with your first line of Java.

Learn what each line means, run it, change it, and then build something yourself. You can start here even if you have never written Java. This session connects your first program to web services and explains how an application stores data.

UnderstandJava basics, web services, SOAP, REST & JPA
Practise5 guided Java examples + independent challenges
Prove itBuild, test, explain, and submit your own work
Start Session 1 →
SET UP ONCE

Prepare your computer, one step at a time

A program is a set of instructions a computer follows. Java is the programming language we use to write those instructions. The text you write is source code. A text editor lets you type and save that text in a file. A terminal is a window where you type commands to ask your computer to do something, such as compile or run a program.

01

Install the Java Development Kit

JDK means Java Development Kit: tools for writing and running Java programs. It includes javac, the compiler. To compile means to check Java source against the language rules and translate it into bytecode, instructions stored in .class files.

java is the launcher that starts the JVM (Java Virtual Machine), the software that executes Java bytecode. To run or execute means to carry out a program’s instructions. A runtime supplies the software needed to run a program; it may lack tools for compiling your own source.

Use JDK 17 or newer. A version identifies a release of the software. If needed, download the Windows JDK from Eclipse Temurin, a distribution (packaged release) of the JDK. Match your computer’s processor architecture, the kind of processor it uses: commonly x64, though some computers use ARM64. Check Windows Settings → System → About → System type if unsure.

In the installer, enable adding Java to PATH. PATH is the list of folders Windows searches for executable programs when you type a command such as javac. Reopen the terminal after installation so it reads the updated list.

02

Open PowerShell and check both tools

PowerShell is a shell: software that reads your commands and acts on them. On Windows, open Start, type PowerShell, and open it. The prompt, usually starting with PS, shows that it is ready for your next command. Type each command below, pressing Enter after each. -version is an option, an extra instruction asking the tool to report its version.

java -version
javac -version

Both commands must display a version of 17 or above. Your exact version text may differ. If either says “not recognized,” install a JDK with PATH enabled and reopen PowerShell. Ask your instructor if this still fails.

03

Use an editor and the right folder

Use VS Code (Visual Studio Code), a text editor, or an editor your instructor provides. A project is the collection of files for the work you are building. A folder groups files. Open the project folder, then find session1-intro/beginner. That path means the beginner folder inside session1-intro. The examples are references; create your own files for independent exercises.

In File Explorer, enable View → Show → File name extensions. A file extension is the ending after a dot that identifies a file’s kind: .java for Java source, .class for compiled bytecode and .txt for ordinary text. Save Java files as .java, never .java.txt. If using Notepad, choose “All files” when saving.

Put PowerShell in the beginner folder.

For this project's current location, type:

Set-Location "C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service\session1-intro\beginner"
Get-Location
Get-ChildItem

Set-Location changes the current folder; quotation marks keep a path containing spaces together. Get-Location should end with session1-intro\beginner. Get-ChildItem lists the files there. If you move the project, use its new path. Every beginner command below assumes you are in this folder.

Type Java into the editor. Type commands into PowerShell. The code and command boxes are labelled separately. Do not type the terminal prompt (such as PS C:\...>) as part of a command. All five beginner examples use the JDK; no Gradle, database, or internet connection is needed after setup.

Two different kinds of path

Your current folder (also called the working directory) is where PowerShell looks for a file named in a command without a full location. A file path is a file or folder’s location on disk, such as C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service. A full path starting with a drive is absolute; HelloJava.java is relative to the current folder.

Later, /greeting is a URL path: part of a web address selecting what the server should provide. It need not be a file on disk. Gradle, mentioned above, is a build tool that can automate compilation and obtain libraries; this first lab uses javac directly.

If a term is unfamiliar, use the Session 1 term reference. Each group includes meanings and examples.

SESSION 1

Java foundations, web services & persistence

1.0 How to work through this lesson

We will use one story: an Aptech student needs course information. First, a program prints a message. Next, it chooses a reply. Then a browser requests that reply from a running Java service. Finally, we look at how student records can be kept in a database.

What the supplied manual actually requires

The presenter's teaching Session 1 (WEBSRV–TL1) covers book Session 1: Introduction to Web Services and book Session 2: Introduction to Jakarta Persistence API (JPA). Those two book chapters belong in this one teaching session. API means Application Programming Interface: the agreed way for one piece of software to use another. JPA is the familiar abbreviation for the former Java Persistence API, now Jakarta Persistence, used to work with stored data. JAX-RS, originally Java API for RESTful Web Services and now Jakarta RESTful Web Services, supports resource-based web services. We define persistence, resources and REST in the lesson below.

Teaching Session 2 now covers JAX-RS. XML (eXtensible Markup Language) is a tagged data format; parsing means reading text according to its rules. XML parsing is kept as an optional supplement. Session 3 covers JAX-WS, originally Java API for XML Web Services and now Jakarta XML Web Services, commonly used for SOAP services. Session 3 now includes guided JAX-WS/SOAP services and clients. Session 4 is the later practice session for book Sessions 1–4. Finish this Session 1 before continuing to Session 2.

StageWhat you will doEvidence that you understand
PrepareInstall/check the JDK; learn sections 1.1–1.2.Write, compile, and explain a short Java program.
Read and predictExplain requests, responses, SOAP, REST, and JPA.Describe each in your own words with an example.
Follow and changeRun the guided programs in 1.5 and 1.6.Predict a change before running it; compare the result.
Work aloneComplete 1.9, the assignment in 1.11, and the exit checks.Write your own code, test it, and explain why it works.

Practice is scheduled: use the meeting-by-meeting plan for Java preparation, core teaching, supported database labs, assignments and next-day recall. The manual’s two-hour teaching slot is one part of this plan. Complete the practical checkpoint before progressing, even if another work period is needed.

Suggested two-hour class plan for the instructor

After checking Java preparation: 10 minutes for a first-program recap; 20 for web services and SOAP/REST; 20 for request logic; 30 for building and testing the HTTP service; 25 for objects, mapping, JPA and JPQL; 15 for independent prediction, questions and the exit check. Assign unfinished practice as supported follow-up. If students cannot explain variables and if yet, spend the class on the foundation and schedule the remaining work separately.

  1. Read: say what the example will do before touching the keyboard.
  2. Type: type the guided example yourself; copying is available for recovery.
  3. Run: compare actual output with the expected output.
  4. Change: change one thing, predict the result, compile, and run again.
  5. Recall: hide the example and solve the similar task. For independent tasks, use your notes and compiler errors, without AI or a pasted solution.

Keep a small “prediction → result → explanation” log. When you get stuck, reread the error, check the matching explanation, and make one small correction. Being able to repair an error is part of learning. On a phone, swipe wide code boxes and tables sideways to see the rest of each line.

Read a term → point to an example → explain its job. A definition should help you use the idea, not just repeat a sentence. New words are explained beside the examples. Section 1.13: Session 1 term reference groups the terms for quick revision, including words used in the optional demo.

How we establish that you can work independently

Reading is the preparation. Before progressing, demonstrate the following with completed solutions closed. Your own notes, supplied build configuration, editor, compiler and normal code-generation tools are allowed; AI-generated answers and another student’s completed solution are not. You are learning to write and explain application code, rather than recreate Jersey or Metro’s internal runtime.

Let the student predict, implement, save/build/restart when needed, test a success and a failure, and explain the result. Use these outcomes: ready independently; needs a small hint; needs the guided example again. Give feedback on the specific missing skill, then set a similar retest. Do not use a quiz score or checkbox count as the sole decision.

Recall check: repeat a small variation after a day’s break. Independent skill means the student can transfer and explain the steps again, not merely remember where to paste a solution.

Scope of persistence: Complete 1.8B for a real provider/database and transactions, then 2.13B for a persistent REST service. The original course Map and SOAP calculation examples retain their separate purposes. This local lab demonstrates persistence and rollback; production deployment is a later engineering topic.

1.1 Your first Java program: print two lines

Syntax means the rules for writing valid Java, including spelling and punctuation. A keyword, such as class or public, has a meaning built into the language. An identifier is a name you choose, such as HelloJava. A statement is an instruction; a block groups instructions between braces. Indentation is the space at the start of a line showing how blocks fit inside each other.

Goal: run a program and explain where execution starts. In your editor, open HelloJava.java. Read the code, then type it yourself. If you use a separate practice folder to preserve the reference, put your PowerShell terminal in that folder before compiling; the file and terminal must refer to the same location.

session1-intro/beginner/HelloJava.java
public class HelloJava {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Hello, Java!");
        System.out.println("I can write my own program.");
    }
}
Part of the codeMeaning in ordinary language
public class HelloJavaA class groups related Java code. This class is named HelloJava. In this example the filename must be HelloJava.java, including capitals. public makes the class accessible.
{ and }Braces mark the beginning and end of a block. The outer pair belongs to the class; the inner pair belongs to main. Indentation helps you see which lines belong together.
public static void main(String[] args)This is the entry method Java calls when you run this class. A method is a named group of instructions. static lets Java call it without creating an object; void means it returns no value.
String[] argsString is Java’s text type. An array holds an ordered group of values; [] marks the array type here. args is the parameter name that receives command-line arguments (text supplied when launching the program). We do not use them yet. Keep this exact main declaration for these examples.
System.out.println("Hello, Java!");System is a Java library class. Its out field supplies the standard output destination, usually your terminal. A dot selects a member (a field or method). println is a method that prints text and moves to the next line. Parentheses contain the argument you supply: here, "Hello, Java!".
"..." and ;Straight double quotes surround a text value. The semicolon ends this instruction. Class and method headers with opening braces do not get a semicolon here.

Guided steps: compile, then run

Save the file. In PowerShell in the beginner folder, type:

javac HelloJava.java

Expected: no message if compilation succeeds; the prompt returns and HelloJava.class appears. This bytecode file is what the JVM runs. The compiler reports a problem if the source is invalid.

java HelloJava
Hello, Java!
I can write my own program.

Use .java when compiling. Use the class name HelloJava when running this compiled example; do not add .class. Run it again: it prints the same two lines and exits. This is a local program, not yet a server.

WE DO → SAY WHAT THE WORD MEANS

Point to HelloJava.java: it is the source file you can edit. Point to HelloJava.class: it contains bytecode produced by the compiler. java HelloJava starts a process, one running instance of the program; this instance prints its output and then ends.

You explain: which file changes when you edit a message? Which command updates the compiled file? Why must you compile again before expecting the new message? Explain before testing.

WE DO → YOU CHANGE

Make it yours

Change the first message to Hello, Amina! and add a third print instruction saying I am ready to practise.. Predict the three lines, save, compile again, and run. If the old text still appears, check that you saved and recompiled the file you are actually running.

YOU DO • NO MODEL CODE

Challenge A: an introduction card

Create MyIntroduction.java with a matching public class. Print your name, your centre, and your learning goal on separate lines. Write the structure from memory, using the explanation table only if needed. Compile and run it. Success means three lines appear in the correct order and you can point to the entry method and both brace pairs.

Stuck? Use a small hint

Your class name and filename must match. Start with the class block, put the main block inside it, then add one print instruction at a time. Do not write all three lines before the first one compiles.

1.2 Variables, choices, methods, and objects

A value is a piece of data, such as 65 or "Amina". Declare means introduce a variable’s type and name. Initialise means give it its first value. Assign means store a value in it, initially or later. A literal is a value written directly in the code: "Amina" is a String literal and 1 is an integer literal.

A variable is a named place to keep a value while your program runs. A type tells Java what kind of value it can hold. We use String for text, int for whole numbers, and boolean for true or false. Java is case-sensitive: String and string are different.

session1-intro/beginner/GreetingPractice.java
public class GreetingPractice {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String studentName = "Amina";
        int lessonsCompleted = 1;
        System.out.println("Hello, " + studentName + "!");
        System.out.println("Lessons completed: " + lessonsCompleted);
    }
}

String studentName = "Amina"; declares a text variable called studentName and gives it its initial value. The = stores a value; it is not a question about equality. int lessonsCompleted = 1; keeps the whole number 1. Numbers do not need quotation marks.

The + joins text when one side is a String. "Hello, " + studentName + "!" builds Hello, Amina!. Spaces inside quotes are printed; spaces used to indent code are not. With numbers alone, 2 + 3 calculates 5. "Total: " + (2 + 3) prints Total: 5; without the parentheses, the text would be joined from left to right as Total: 23.

javac GreetingPractice.java
java GreetingPractice
Hello, Amina!
Lessons completed: 1
GUIDED CHANGE

Change the name to Tunde and the number to 2. Predict both output lines, save, compile, and run. Then introduce String course = "Web Services"; and print Course: Web Services using the variable.

A choice: run one branch

A condition is an expression that produces true or false. An expression is code evaluated to obtain a value; 2 + 3 gives 5. An operator is a symbol that performs work: + adds numbers or joins text and >= compares numbers. A branch is one possible path through a decision; if runs its block when the condition is true, and else runs its block when that condition is false. Joining Strings with + is called concatenation.

int score = 65;
if (score >= 50) {
    System.out.println("Pass");
} else {
    System.out.println("Practise again");
}

This is a fragment, not a complete file: type it inside a main method, for example after the prints in GreetingPractice. score >= 50 asks whether the number is at least 50 and produces a boolean. If true, the first block runs; otherwise the else block runs. With 65 it prints Pass. Predict the result for 49 and for 50 before testing. An else if adds another test after an earlier test fails.

For text values, use "GET_GREETING".equals(request) to compare their contents. It asks “does this text match exactly?” Do not use == for String contents: it checks whether two references identify the same object. A leading ! reverses a boolean: !"GET".equals(method) means “the method is not GET.”

A method: name a task and return a result

A method declaration defines a method’s name, inputs, return type and code. A method call asks it to run. A parameter is the named input in the declaration; an argument is the actual value supplied in a call. The caller is the code making that call. The return type says what kind of result the caller receives, or void when there is no result value.

public static String greet(String name) {
    return "Hello, " + name + "!";
}

Place this fragment inside the class, outside main. It declares a method named greet. Its parameter name receives text from the caller. The String before greet says it returns text; return sends that value back and ends this method call. Inside main, String message = greet("Amina"); calls it with the argument "Amina". System.out.println(message); prints Hello, Amina!.

Printing and returning are different. return gives a value to the calling code; it does not display it. A println displays text; it does not return that message as a String. This matters when a server must send a result to a client instead of only printing it in its own terminal.

A parameter is the slot; an argument is what you put in it

In greet(String name), name is the parameter. In greet("Amina"), "Amina" is the argument. The call supplies that text to name, and the method returns the greeting to its caller. A local variable, such as message inside main, belongs to that method call. Scope is the region of code in which a name can be used. A variable declared inside a block is not available after that block ends.

You explain: for greet("Tunde"), identify the parameter, argument and returned value. Explain why the word name is not the text displayed.

A class and an object: a template and one student

session1-intro/beginner/StudentCardDemo.java
public class StudentCardDemo {
    static class Student {
        int id;
        String name;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Student student = new Student();
        student.id = 101;
        student.name = "Amina";
        System.out.println(student.id + ": " + student.name);
    }
}

The nested Student class is a template with two fields, values belonging to each Student object: id and name. We put the template inside the demo to keep one file. static class here lets main use this nested template without an outer demo object.

An object is one instance made from a class, like one filled student card made from a card template. new Student() creates an instance in memory (RAM, Random Access Memory), the temporary working storage used while programs run. Student student declares a variable holding a reference to that object: a way to reach it, rather than a second copy of the card. A dot selects a field: student.id = 101; sets this object’s id.

A constructor sets up a new object. It has the class’s name and no return type. The parentheses in new Student() call a constructor with no arguments. Java supplies one here because we declared no constructor. Another new Student() creates a separate object with its own field values. Nested class means a class declared inside another class; our Student template is nested inside StudentCardDemo.

javac StudentCardDemo.java
java StudentCardDemo
101: Amina

Prediction: if you stop and run this program again, it creates a new object and assigns those values again. It has not saved a student in a file or database. We will connect this fact to persistence in 1.8.

YOU DO

Create LearningCard.java. Store a name and a whole number of completed exercises; print both. Add an if/else that prints Keep practising below 3 exercises and Ready for a service at 3 or above. Test with 2, 3, and 4. Then create a second Student in your object demo with id 102 and another name, and print both students. Explain which values belong to which object.

1.3 What is a web service?

🏫

The school reception desk

You ask reception, “What course runs today?” Reception checks the timetable and replies, “Creating Services for the Web.” You do not need to enter the records room yourself.

In software, a student app sends a request to a course service. The service processes it and sends a response. The app and service agree on an address, message format, and rules so they can understand each other.

A network connects devices so they can exchange data. The internet connects many networks worldwide; a private network can connect devices in one centre. A web service is software another program can call across a network using agreed web communication rules. It can also run on your own computer for practice.

HTTP means Hypertext Transfer Protocol. A protocol is an agreed set of rules for exchanging messages: how to ask, how to reply and how to interpret the reply. A browser is an application that requests and displays web content; here it acts as our client. Software means programs; an application (app) is software used to carry out a task.

WordMeaningOur example
Client / requesterThe program that makes the request.Your browser or a student app.
Server / providerA running program that receives requests and provides replies.The Java program you will leave running.
APIApplication Programming Interface: the agreed way to use software. A web API describes available requests and replies.Read a greeting with GET /greeting. An API can also exist without a network.
EndpointAn address at which a service is available; a method and path select what to do.http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting for GET.
Request / responseThe message asking for work / the message sent back.GET /greeting → 200 and Welcome to Aptech!
InteroperabilityDifferent systems communicate because they follow the same agreed rules.A Java server can reply to a browser or a Python client.

HTML (HyperText Markup Language) describes webpage content using tags. JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) is a text format for structured data, such as {"name":"Amina"}; Java can produce and read it even though its name mentions JavaScript. XML (eXtensible Markup Language) labels structured data with tags, such as <name>Amina</name>. Plain text is text with no required HTML, JSON or XML structure, such as Welcome to Aptech!.

A webpage often sends HTML for a person to read. A web API often returns data for software to use. A client app may turn API data into a page for its user. Data format means the rules for arranging that data; it is different from a communication protocol such as HTTP.

Read the address instead of memorising it

URL (Uniform Resource Locator) means a web address. A host identifies the computer receiving the request, using a name or IP (Internet Protocol) address. The scheme, such as http, tells the client which kind of access to use. The URL path, such as /greeting, selects a resource. A resource is something the API makes available, such as a greeting or student record. A representation is the data sent to describe it: one student resource could be represented as JSON or XML.

http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting
│      │         │    └─ path: which resource?
│      │         └────── port: which listening program?
│      └──────────────── host: which computer?
└─────────────────────── scheme: which communication rules?

127.0.0.1 is a loopback address: this computer. localhost usually resolves to a loopback address too. A port is a numbered network entry point. Our server listens on 8081. Your browser must use the same host, port, and path. We use HTTP for local practice; deployed services commonly use HTTPS to protect communication. Visiting this loopback address on your phone refers to the phone, not your laptop.

Resolve here means translate a host name into an IP address. Listen means wait for connections at a host and port. Local means on your current computer; remote means reached on another computer. To deploy means place and configure an application where its intended users can access it. HTTPS is HTTP protected by TLS (Transport Layer Security), which encrypts traffic and helps verify the server’s identity. Encryption encodes data so someone without the required key cannot read it.

EXPLAIN IT WITHOUT CODE

A mobile app asks a weather service for Lagos's temperature. Identify the client, provider, request, and response. Explain why the app does not need to be written in the same language as the service. Then describe what changes when only the port in the address is wrong.

1.4 SOA, SOAP, and REST: three ideas to separate

Architecture means how a system’s parts are arranged and work together. An interface describes how to use a part; its implementation is the code doing the work. In SOA, an interface might say “ask this service for a course by id”; clients can use that agreement without knowing how the provider stores courses.

Service-Oriented Architecture (SOA) organises capabilities as services with clear interfaces so different applications can reuse them. For example, a student portal and a receptionist's app might both call the same Course Service. They depend on the agreed interface rather than directly reading the service's internal Java variables.

Provider

Builds and runs the course service.

Registry

An optional directory describing available services and how to find them.

Requester

Finds the service's address and calls it.

The traditional model is publish → find → bind: the provider publishes a description, the requester finds it, then “binds” by using the documented address and rules to call the provider. UDDI means Universal Description, Discovery and Integration, a standard used for service directories in older SOAP systems. A registry is not required for every call; our practice client knows the address already. An API gateway receives client requests and forwards them to appropriate services. Routing means choosing where a request goes or which code handles it; a gateway is not automatically a service registry.

Two common approaches to web services

SOAP, historically expanded as Simple Object Access Protocol, is a message protocol using a defined XML envelope. REST (Representational State Transfer) is an architectural style: rules for organising a system around resources and their representations. Its uniform interface means using consistent interaction rules, including resource identifiers and representations. In HTTP APIs this commonly means resource URLs and standard methods such as GET and POST. A constraint is a rule a design must satisfy. JSON is a data format, not another name for REST.

SOAP example: ask the Student Service for student 101

An XML tag is a label in angle brackets. An element includes an opening tag, its contents and a closing tag: <s:id>101</s:id> is one element. An attribute is extra information written in an opening tag, such as xmlns:s="urn:aptech:students". The root element is the outermost element enclosing the document.

A SOAP Envelope is that outer message wrapper. Its Body contains the operation request or result; a SOAP Header, when present, carries extra message information. An operation is a named service task, such as getStudent. Its input here is id 101. A namespace identifies which vocabulary an XML name belongs to, so two vocabularies can both use a name such as id without confusion. A prefix is the short label before the colon: soap or s. xmlns declares the prefix’s namespace.

<soap:Envelope xmlns:soap="http://schemas.xmlsoap.org/soap/envelope/"
               xmlns:s="urn:aptech:students">
    <soap:Body>
        <s:getStudent>
            <s:id>101</s:id>
        </s:getStudent>
    </soap:Body>
</soap:Envelope>

This is an illustrative SOAP 1.1 request body, not a Java program to run. A SOAP response would also have an Envelope and Body; errors can use a SOAP Fault. SOAP commonly travels over HTTP POST. A WSDL (Web Services Description Language) document can describe the service's operations, input/output messages, and address. We will build SOAP code in a later session.

A SOAP Fault is a structured error inside a SOAP Body. A service contract is the agreement about available operations, accepted inputs and returned results; WSDL can express it in a document. The namespace identifiers in this XML label vocabularies; they are not addresses you need to open. URI (Uniform Resource Identifier) is the broader term for an identifier. A URL locates something; a URN (Uniform Resource Name), such as urn:aptech:students, names something without giving its location.

REST example: read the student resource

GET /students/101 HTTP/1.1
Host: school.example
Accept: application/json

Example response body:
{"id":101,"name":"Amina"}

This is an illustrative HTTP request and a separate example response body; school.example is not a working lab address. JSON groups named values with braces. In "id":101, the key is id and the value is the number 101. "name":"Amina" has a text value. A REST representation could also be XML or plain text.

Read the HTTP message words

An HTTP method says what kind of action the client asks for, such as GET to read. It is separate from a Java method such as greet(). A header supplies information about an HTTP message: Host names the destination host and Accept: application/json asks for a JSON representation. A media type labels a data format: application/json means JSON and text/plain means plain text.

The body carries the main message data, such as the returned student JSON. An HTTP status code is the three-digit result signal in a response: 200 means OK, 404 means Not Found and 405 means Method Not Allowed. The next lab shows these in real responses. HTTP/1.1 names the protocol version. A JSON object here is a set of key/value pairs between braces; it is a text representation, not the live Java object in memory.

You explain: identify the resource, representation, method and header in the student example. Explain which would change if the same student were returned as XML.

QuestionSOAPREST over HTTP
What is it?A protocol with a specified message structure.An architectural style with constraints, including a uniform interface and stateless requests.
How does this example ask?Operation getStudent inside an XML Body.GET /students/101 asks for a resource representation.
Which data format?SOAP messages use XML.JSON, XML, plain text, and other representations.
How can the interface be described?Often WSDL.Often OpenAPI or other API documentation; OpenAPI is not required for REST.
When might it fit?Systems needing SOAP contracts or WS-* standards.Resource APIs for browser, mobile, and other clients. Requirements determine the choice.

Stateless means each request carries the information needed to understand it; the server does not rely on a hidden previous client conversation to interpret it. It does not mean the server cannot have a database. SOAP and REST both need deliberate authentication, validation, and error handling; neither makes a system secure by itself.

Authentication checks who a caller is; authorisation checks what that caller may do. Validation checks whether input meets the rules, such as a required name being present. Error handling is how the program responds when a task fails. OpenAPI is a format for describing an HTTP API’s paths, inputs and responses. WS-* is a collective label for web-service standards such as WS-Security, used to secure SOAP messages; it is not one Java command or a guarantee that security is configured.

HTTP methodTypical resource operationStudent example
GETRead; should not change the resource.GET /students/101
POSTSubmit data for processing; often create a new resource.POST /students with new student data
PUTCreate or replace the representation at the target address.PUT /students/101 with the complete replacement record
PATCHApply a partial change.PATCH /students/101 with a changed name
DELETERemove the target resource.DELETE /students/101

We implement GET only in today's HTTP lab. The table introduces the other methods; it does not claim our demo supports them.

CHECK YOUR REASONING

A browser wants to read a course; choose a method and path. A client wants to create a student; choose a method and path. Explain why seeing JSON in a message is not enough to conclude that an API follows REST. Draw provider → request → response → client and decide whether your drawing needs a registry.

1.5 Guided lab: choose a reply before adding a network

Goal: practise the decision a service makes without network setup. This program runs entirely in one process, one running instance of a program. Logic means the rules and steps that choose what to do. This is a model of service logic; it is not yet a web service. Fallback means the answer used when no known case matches, here Unknown request.

session1-intro/beginner/RequestPractice.java
public class RequestPractice {
    public static String replyTo(String request) {
        if ("GET_GREETING".equals(request)) {
            return "Welcome to Aptech!";
        } else if ("GET_COURSE".equals(request)) {
            return "Creating Services for the Web";
        } else {
            return "Unknown request";
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String request = "GET_GREETING";
        String response = replyTo(request);
        System.out.println("Request: " + request);
        System.out.println("Response: " + response);
    }
}
  1. Java begins in main and stores "GET_GREETING" in request.
  2. replyTo(request) calls the method. Its parameter, also named request, receives that text for this call. Variables inside methods have their own scope; these two names are not one shared variable.
  3. The first equals test is true, so return "Welcome to Aptech!"; sends back the result. The remaining branches are skipped.
  4. Main keeps that returned text in response, then prints the request and response. Printing happens here, after the method returns.
javac RequestPractice.java
java RequestPractice
Request: GET_GREETING
Response: Welcome to Aptech!
WE DO • TRACE THE BRANCHES

Change only the request in main to "GET_COURSE". Predict why the first test fails and the second succeeds. Compile and run: the response should be Creating Services for the Web. Now use "GET_PRICE"; both tests fail, so the response is Unknown request. The text "get_greeting" also fails because equals matches case exactly.

WE DO • ADD ONE BEHAVIOUR

Add another else if immediately before the final else. Match "GET_CENTRE" and return "Aptech Lagos". Save and compile. Test GET_GREETING, GET_COURSE, GET_CENTRE, and an unknown request one at a time. All old branches must still work.

Reveal this guided change only after trying it
} else if ("GET_CENTRE".equals(request)) {
    return "Aptech Lagos";
} else {
    return "Unknown request";
}

This replaces the final closing part of the decision chain; do not paste an extra final else beside the existing one.

YOU DO • SIMILAR TASK

Challenge B: library information

Create LibraryReply.java. Write a method replyTo that returns Library opens at 9 AM for GET_HOURS, Borrow up to 2 books for GET_LIMIT, and Unknown request for anything else. Main must call the method and print its returned result. Test all three branches. Write the code yourself; no full solution is supplied.

Hint 1: organise the task

Write the inputs and expected outputs on paper. Create main and the method inside one matching class. First make one known input work, then add the second branch and the fallback.

Hint 2: check a common mistake

Your method's return type is String. Every possible route through it must return text. Printing inside the method is not a substitute for returning its answer.

1.6 Guided lab: your first HTTP web service

Goal: let a browser request a greeting from Java. This is a minimal HTTP resource example using the JDK's built-in HTTP server, without a framework. It introduces the request/response part of REST; one endpoint alone does not demonstrate every REST constraint. JAX-RS and Jersey come later.

Before starting

You should be able to explain the variables, text comparisons, and method returns in 1.5. We now borrow a small amount of Java library code for network handling. Read the explanation table carefully; you are not expected to invent the library API from memory.

Words used by the server code

A library is reusable code supplied for other programs to use. A package groups related Java types under a name, such as java.net. An import lets a source file use a type’s short name: HttpServer instead of its full package name. Importing does not download or start anything. A framework supplies more of an application’s structure and calls your code at defined points; Jersey is the JAX-RS framework/runtime used in Session 2.

A handler is code called to deal with an event, here an incoming request. To register it means tell the server which handler to call. A lambda, written with ->, supplies a function’s behaviour; here exchange -> { ... } supplies the handler code, and exchange is its input representing one request and response. The server’s context connects a path prefix to a handler.

To bind a server means reserve its local address and port so it can listen there. A connection is a communication link between client and server. A queue is a waiting line; the pending-connection queue holds connections awaiting acceptance. Exception means a Java object reporting a problem during execution, such as an address already being in use. throws Exception declares that a problem may be passed to the caller.

session1-intro/beginner/FirstWebService.java
import com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer;
import java.net.InetSocketAddress;
import java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;

public class FirstWebService {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        HttpServer server = HttpServer.create(
            new InetSocketAddress("127.0.0.1", 8081), 0);

        server.createContext("/", exchange -> {
            String method = exchange.getRequestMethod();
            String path = exchange.getRequestURI().getPath();
            int status;
            String response;

            if (!"GET".equals(method)) {
                status = 405;
                response = "Use GET for this service.";
                exchange.getResponseHeaders().set("Allow", "GET");
            } else if ("/greeting".equals(path)) {
                status = 200;
                response = "Welcome to Aptech!";
            } else {
                status = 404;
                response = "Resource not found.";
            }

            byte[] body = response.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);
            exchange.getResponseHeaders().set(
                "Content-Type", "text/plain; charset=utf-8");
            exchange.sendResponseHeaders(status, body.length);
            try (var output = exchange.getResponseBody()) {
                output.write(body);
            }
            exchange.close();
        });

        server.start();
        System.out.println("Open http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting");
        System.out.println("Keep this terminal open. Press Ctrl+C to stop.");
    }
}
Code, in execution orderWhat it does and why it is there
The three import linesLet us use classes from Java libraries by short names. HttpServer handles HTTP; InetSocketAddress combines an address and port; StandardCharsets supplies the UTF-8 text encoding.
main(...) throws ExceptionStarts the program. throws Exception lets a startup problem be reported to the caller, here the Java launcher. It does not prevent errors. We keep error handling simple for this learning demo.
HttpServer.create(..., 0)Creates a server bound to loopback address 127.0.0.1 and port 8081. The final 0 requests the default pending-connection queue size. The variable server refers to the created object.
server.createContext("/", exchange -> { ... });Registers the code to run for incoming paths under /. The -> is a lambda: a block supplied as a request handler. The server calls it later for each request and provides exchange, an object representing that request and its response. The body does not run merely because we register it.
getRequestMethod() and getRequestURI().getPath()Read the incoming method (such as GET) and path (such as /greeting). These are ordinary method calls on the library objects.
int status; and String response;Declare local variables before choosing their values. Each branch sets both variables so they are ready to use after the if/else.
First branch: method is not GETChoose 405 (Method Not Allowed), a helpful message, and an Allow header advertising GET. The exclamation mark means “not.”
Second branch: path is /greetingFor a GET with this exact path, choose 200 (OK) and the greeting. These are our service decisions.
Final elseFor an unknown GET path, choose 404 (Not Found). Registering / lets our own code choose this readable fallback.
byte[] body = response.getBytes(...)Convert the text to a list of bytes for transmission, using UTF-8. HTTP needs the number of bytes, which can differ from the number of text characters.
Content-Type and sendResponseHeadersTell the client the reply is plain text in UTF-8, then send the chosen status and byte length. A header is extra information about the message.
try (var output = ...) and output.write(body)Open the response output stream and write the bytes to the client. var asks Java to infer the local variable type. This try-with-resources block closes the stream automatically, even if writing fails.
exchange.close()Finishes this request/response exchange. It does not stop the entire server.
server.start() and the two println callsBegin listening, then print instructions in the server terminal. The server stays alive for later requests; the greeting itself is sent in the handler, not by these println calls.

Step 1 — save and compile in terminal A

Text has to become bytes before it travels

A byte is eight bits of data; a bit is a 0 or 1. byte[] body is an array of bytes. Character encoding is the rule for turning text characters into bytes and back. UTF-8 (Unicode Transformation Format, 8-bit) is the encoding used here: the character A needs one byte, while é needs two. That is why body.length counts bytes instead of relying on the number of visible characters.

A stream is a way to read or write a sequence of data. The output stream from getResponseBody() sends our bytes towards the client. write(body) writes them; close() releases the stream’s resources. Try-with-resources, try (...) { ... }, closes a resource automatically when the block finishes, even if a problem occurs. Here the word resource means a stream that needs closing; in REST it means something exposed by an API.

var asks the compiler to infer (work out) the local variable’s type from the value assigned. The type is still fixed. body.length reads the array’s number of elements. A module groups related Java packages; jdk.httpserver is the JDK module supplying these HTTP-server classes.

javac --add-modules jdk.httpserver FirstWebService.java

The --add-modules jdk.httpserver option explicitly makes the JDK HTTP-server module available. Successful compilation is silent. Fix reported errors before running.

Step 2 — start the server in terminal A

java --add-modules jdk.httpserver FirstWebService
Open http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting
Keep this terminal open. Press Ctrl+C to stop.

Leave terminal A running. It will not return to a normal command prompt until you stop it. That is expected: a server waits for requests. Do not try to type the next command into that running program.

Step 3 — open the address in your browser

Type http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting into the address bar and press Enter. The browser sends a GET request. You should see Welcome to Aptech!. Reload the page to make another request. Then visit http://127.0.0.1:8081/missing; you should see Resource not found.

Step 4 — inspect the status in terminal B

Open a second PowerShell window. These HTTP commands can run from any folder. Use curl.exe explicitly on Windows; curl alone may be a PowerShell alias with different options. If curl.exe is unavailable, use the browser's developer tools → Network tab and reload the page to inspect method, status and response.

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-type: text/plain; charset=utf-8
...other headers...

Welcome to Aptech!

-i includes response headers. This is a shortened example: header order, capitalisation, and a Date header can vary. The important checks are 200, the content type, and the body. The blank line separates headers from the body.

curl is a command-line tool for making network requests; curl.exe selects its Windows executable. An alias is a shortcut name for another command; in some PowerShell versions curl points to a different tool. -X POST asks curl to use the HTTP method POST. With no method option in these examples, curl makes a GET. Developer tools are the browser’s inspection tools; their Network tab records requests and responses.

Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8 is a response header telling the client the body is plain text encoded as UTF-8. Charset here labels the text encoding. Allow: GET advertises the method supported by our handler. A default is what a tool uses when you have not chosen another setting. A restart means stop the old program instance and start a new one. Ctrl+C sends an interruption to the running console program so this server can stop.

Test in terminal BExpected statusExpected body
curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting200Welcome to Aptech!
curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8081/missing404Resource not found.
curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting405, with Allow: GETUse GET for this service.

Status and message have different jobs. The status is a standard signal software can inspect. The body supplies the actual data or a readable explanation. A successful-looking message with the wrong status would be misleading.

Step 5 — make one change, then restart

Change the greeting to You built your first service!. Save. In terminal A press Ctrl+C, compile again, and start the server again using the same commands. Reload the browser. You should see the new message. Editing a file does not change a Java process that is already running.

1.7 Follow a request, one step at a time

This is a browser teaching simulation of the unchanged Java example, not a Java compiler or a live call to your server. Choose a request, predict the reply, then reveal each step. For actual proof, run the Java lab above.

1.8 JPA: how Java objects become stored records

A database is an organised collection of stored data, managed by database software. A record groups the values describing one item, such as student 101 and the name Amina. A table groups related records in rows and columns, like a class register. An identifier distinguishes one item; a database primary key is a particular kind of identifier.

Persistence means retaining data beyond the lifetime of a program. The Student object from 1.2 lives in memory while that program runs. A database can keep a student record after it stops. JPA, now called Jakarta Persistence, is a specification for mapping Java objects to relational database records and working with them.

A specification needs an implementation

JPA defines interfaces, annotations and behaviour. An implementation, such as Hibernate ORM or EclipseLink, performs the work. A relational database, such as PostgreSQL, stores the rows. Spring Data JPA adds repository conveniences on top of JPA; it is neither the database nor the JPA implementation.

Who defines the rules, and who does the work?

A specification is a written definition of rules and required behaviour. An implementation is software that follows those rules. A JPA provider is the implementation used by your application, such as Hibernate ORM or EclipseLink. Here provider means persistence software; in SOA, provider meant the service offering a capability.

A Java interface declares operations other code can implement. JPA’s EntityManager interface declares operations for working with entities; the provider supplies their behaviour. A repository groups operations for finding and saving a particular kind of data. Spring Data JPA is a helper framework for building those repositories. PostgreSQL is database software that actually stores and retrieves tables.

A dependency is software your project needs in order to work. Configuration is the settings telling it how to work, such as which database to connect to. Compatible versions are releases that can work together. Jakarta EE means Jakarta Enterprise Edition, a family of specifications for Java application services; Jakarta Persistence is one part.

A relational database stores data in tables. A row is one record; a column is one property. A primary key uniquely identifies a row. In a students table, id 101 identifies Amina and id 102 identifies Tunde.

id (primary key)full_name
101Amina
102Tunde

Object-relational mapping (ORM) connects the object view (Student, id, name) to the table view (students, id, full_name). Object-relational metadata is the mapping information: which class belongs to which table, which field belongs to which column, and which field is the identifier.

Metadata means information describing other data or its organisation. A JPA annotation is metadata written with @ in Java source. A persistent entity is a Java type whose instances the provider can manage and map to database records. Attribute in this example means an entity’s Java field, such as name; an XML attribute instead is information in an opening tag.

Read a mapping example; do not run it with today's plain javac setup

import jakarta.persistence.Entity;
import jakarta.persistence.Table;
import jakarta.persistence.Id;
import jakarta.persistence.Column;

@Entity
@Table(name = "students")
public class Student {
    @Id
    private int id;

    @Column(name = "full_name")
    private String name;

    public Student() {
    }
}

This separate example would live in Student.java in a JPA project. It needs the Jakarta Persistence API, a provider, database connection and configuration; those are not installed by the beginner lab. Copying it into a plain JDK-only project will cause package jakarta.persistence does not exist. This section teaches the mapping first; 1.8B supplies the provider, file database, configuration and actual transaction exercises.

Line / annotationWhat it tells Java or JPA
@EntityThis class is a persistent entity: an object type the provider can map to stored records. The default entity name is Student.
@Table(name = "students")Use the database table named students. Without this explicit name the defaults and provider naming strategy apply.
@IdThe following id field is the primary-key identifier. Here the application assigns ids; automatic id generation would require additional configuration.
@Column(name = "full_name")Map the Java field name to the database column full_name. The two names need not match.
private fieldsThe fields are hidden from ordinary direct access outside the class. Because @Id is on a field, this mapping uses field access; the provider accesses the fields.
public Student() { }An explicit constructor with no arguments. JPA entities need a public or protected no-argument constructor so the provider can create instances. public permits access wherever the class is accessible. protected permits access from the same package and, under Java’s access rules, subclasses (classes based on this class). This constructor’s block performs no extra setup.

Annotations start with @ and supply metadata. The text name = "students" inside @Table supplies an annotation value; it does not declare a Java variable. Metadata may also be expressed in XML mapping files. A relationship connects entities: many students might belong to one centre. @ManyToOne describes that kind of association; we study its coding in a later database lesson. A naming strategy is the provider’s rule for choosing table and column names when an explicit mapping is absent. Field access means the provider reads/writes mapped fields rather than using accessor methods.

JPQL: ask about entities and their fields

A query asks for data, often matching a condition. SQL (Structured Query Language) is used to work with relational database data. JPQL, originally Java Persistence Query Language and now Jakarta Persistence Query Language, is JPA’s query language for entities. SQL typically names database tables and columns; JPQL names entity types and Java attributes. This difference matters when names differ.

JPQL: SELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.id = :studentId
SQL:  SELECT * FROM students WHERE id = 101;

In JPQL, SELECT s asks for Student entities; FROM Student s uses s as a short alias for each entity; WHERE filters matches. :studentId is a named parameter whose value is supplied separately, such as 101. The provider translates the JPQL into SQL suitable for the configured database. JPQL is not typed into PowerShell and not the same as a Java if condition.

SELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.name = :studentName

This second query uses s.name, the Java field. It does not use s.full_name, the database column. Bind studentName to "Amina" in application code. Parameters separate data from query text; do not build queries by joining untrusted input into the query string.

An alias in a query is a short name such as s for Student, different from a shell command alias. A named parameter, such as :studentName, is a placeholder for a value supplied separately. To bind a query parameter means assign that placeholder a value. It is a different use of bind from reserving a server port. A parameterised query keeps the query structure separate from its input values.

SELECT chooses what to return; FROM identifies which entities or table to read; WHERE gives a filtering condition. In the SQL example * means all columns. Filter means keep only matching items. Untrusted input means outside data, such as user text, which must not be treated as executable query instructions.

Where JPA fits in a web service

  1. A client sends GET /students/101.
  2. The web layer reads the requested id.
  3. Application logic asks JPA for the Student with id 101.
  4. The provider queries the database and builds or retrieves a Student object.
  5. The web layer converts the needed data into a response for the client.

JPA does not create the HTTP endpoint. It handles the persistence part. An EntityManager is the JPA interface used to manage entities. Common actions include persist to make a new entity managed, find to retrieve by id, and remove to delete a managed entity. Changes normally occur within a transaction: a unit of work that commits together or rolls back on failure. Constructing an object with new, or adding @Entity alone, does not save it. Provider work, transaction handling, and a successful commit are required for a durable change.

Managed does not mean already saved

A managed entity is an entity instance the EntityManager is currently tracking in its persistence context, its collection of tracked instances. persist makes a new entity managed; it does not itself guarantee that a database transaction has successfully committed. find looks up an entity by its primary key. remove marks a managed entity for deletion; a successful transaction is needed to keep that database change.

To commit means successfully complete the transaction so its changes are kept. To roll back means cancel its database changes. Durable means the committed change is retained beyond the current program run. For example, saving a student and their registration can belong to one transaction so a failure does not leave only half the intended database changes.

The web layer receives and sends HTTP messages. Application logic decides what the request means and what to do. Persistence handles stored data. You explain: which part receives GET, which part decides which student is needed, and which part retrieves the stored record?

Recognise the version names in older and newer material

VersionName / package family
1.0Java Persistence API 1.0; javax.persistence.
2.0, 2.1, 2.2Java Persistence API releases; javax.persistence.
Jakarta Persistence 2.2Jakarta branding, still the javax.persistence namespace.
3.0Switch to jakarta.persistence.
3.1Jakarta EE 10 generation; jakarta.persistence.
3.2Jakarta EE 11 generation; jakarta.persistence.

These version families are for recognition, not a request to mix dependencies. The API and provider versions must be compatible. This lesson's mapping uses jakarta.persistence. For specification reference, see Jakarta Persistence specifications.

WE DO • DESIGN THE MAPPING

Suppose Java has Course with fields id and title; the database has table courses with columns id and course_title. Class → table: Course → courses. Identifier: id. Field → column: title → course_title. A JPQL filter should use c.title, not c.course_title.

YOU DO • MAPPING AND QUERY

Design a Book entity with id and title. The table is library_books; columns are book_id and book_title. On paper, map the class and both fields, choose the primary key, write a JPQL query selecting books by title with a named parameter, and identify which tool is the API, provider, database and repository helper. This is a design task, not a claim that you ran a JPA application.

Hint: keep the two naming systems separate

@Table names the physical table. @Column names a physical column, including the id field if its column name differs. JPQL uses the entity name Book and its Java field title. Put @Id on the identifier field.

1.8B Save real records, one small step at a time

Goal: connect the JPA ideas in 1.8 to a real database. You will save a student, close the program, read the saved row in a new program, query it, change it, cancel a change and delete your practice row. Complete this after Java objects in 1.2 and the mapping explanation in 1.8. The Maven setup is explained in 2.3; you may read that setup now without starting Session 2's web service.

Two passes through the database work

First use the console lab below: Java → JPA → Hibernate → H2 database file. After learning JAX-RS in Session 2, complete 2.13B: HTTP → Jersey → the same StudentStore → the same database. Start with console work so HTTP problems do not obscure the database idea.

Use made-up names in your own project copy. The default file is data/students.mv.db inside the terminal's current folder. Run every command here from session1-jpa-lab so each program opens the same file. The database starts empty; the code does not reseed it or clear it on startup.

Step 1 — know the job of each piece

PieceIts jobConcrete example
Student.javaDescribes one entity and its mapping.Java name → database column full_name.
Jakarta Persistence APIDefines interfaces such as EntityManager.Your code calls persist and find.
HibernateImplements those interfaces and translates object operations/JPQL into SQL.Turns a new Student into an INSERT.
H2 + its JDBC driverH2 stores the rows in a file; the driver lets Java communicate with it.data/students.mv.db
persistence.xml + Database.javaConfigure the provider, entity and database connection.The persistence unit is named school-lab.
StudentStoreOur own helper containing persistence operations.add, find, search, rename, delete.
PersistenceLabA supplied command launcher for the console exercises.add "Amina" selects StudentStore.add.

Embedded database means H2 runs inside the Java process; it stores committed data in a separate file. You do not install a separate database server or open a database website. Maven downloads the Java libraries. A JDBC URL is a database connection string: jdbc:h2:file:... selects H2 file storage. It is different from an HTTP URL.

Step 2 — prepare the folder and build

The folder should contain pom.xml and src. Use JDK 17+ and Maven as in 2.3. Wait for BUILD SUCCESS. The first build needs internet access; later offline builds work only after all required libraries and plugins are cached. A dependency is a library the project needs. The supplied pom pins Jakarta Persistence 3.1.0, Hibernate 6.6.5.Final and H2 2.3.232; it also supplies Jersey 3.1.5 for the later REST bridge. Maven packages the libraries and our code in one JAR.
