Read the dependency names first. The compiler plugin targets Java 17; the shade plugin bundles dependencies. Its service-file transformer keeps provider discovery information. You may reuse this setup for exercises; the learning task is to write and explain the entity and persistence operations.

Step 3 — read and type the entity

session1-jpa-lab/src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/Student.java
package com.aptech.persistence;

import jakarta.persistence.Column;
import jakarta.persistence.Entity;
import jakarta.persistence.GeneratedValue;
import jakarta.persistence.GenerationType;
import jakarta.persistence.Id;
import jakarta.persistence.Table;

@Entity
@Table(name = "students")
public class Student {
    @Id
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)
    private Long id;

    @Column(name = "full_name", nullable = false, length = 60)
    private String name;

    public Student() {
    }

    public Student(String name) {
        setName(name);
    }

    public Long getId() {
        return id;
    }

    public String getName() {
        return name;
    }

    public void setName(String name) {
        if (name == null || name.isBlank() || name.trim().length() > 60) {
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("Name must contain 1 to 60 characters after trimming.");
        }
        this.name = name.trim();
    }
}
PartMeaning
@Entity / @TableStudent is a JPA entity; its rows go in students.
Long id / @GeneratedValue(IDENTITY)H2 assigns the identifier during insertion. Long can be null before an id exists. Do not set ids manually in this lab.
@Column(name="full_name", nullable=false, length=60)Map name to full_name, require a stored value and allocate up to 60 characters. The annotation is a database constraint; our Java check also rejects blank text.
Student()No-argument constructor the provider can use to load a row.
Student(String name)Constructor your code uses for a new student; it calls our setter to validate.
getId / getNameGetter methods return values. They do not query the database themselves.
setNameChecks before assigning this.name; this selects the current object’s field. || means “or” and short-circuits, so null is checked before calling a String method.
throw new IllegalArgumentException(...)Report an invalid value instead of storing it. An exception interrupts the normal path; a catch block can handle it.

You predict: what happens for " Amina ", " " and a name of 61 characters? The trimmed name is saved in the first case; the other two are rejected before insert. Name length is counted using Java String length, as in earlier lessons.

Step 4 — connect the provider to the database file

session1-jpa-lab/src/main/resources/META-INF/persistence.xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<persistence xmlns="https://jakarta.ee/xml/ns/persistence" version="3.1">
    <persistence-unit name="school-lab" transaction-type="RESOURCE_LOCAL">
        <provider>org.hibernate.jpa.HibernatePersistenceProvider</provider>
        <class>com.aptech.persistence.Student</class>
        <exclude-unlisted-classes>true</exclude-unlisted-classes>
        <properties>
            <property name="jakarta.persistence.jdbc.driver" value="org.h2.Driver"/>
            <property name="jakarta.persistence.jdbc.user" value="sa"/>
            <property name="jakarta.persistence.jdbc.password" value=""/>
            <property name="hibernate.hbm2ddl.auto" value="update"/>
            <property name="hibernate.show_sql" value="false"/>
        </properties>
    </persistence-unit>
</persistence>
session1-jpa-lab/src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/Database.java
package com.aptech.persistence;

import java.io.IOException;
import java.nio.file.Files;
import java.nio.file.Path;
import java.util.Map;
import jakarta.persistence.EntityManagerFactory;
import jakarta.persistence.Persistence;

public class Database {
    public static EntityManagerFactory open() throws IOException {
        Path base = Path.of(System.getProperty("lab.db", "./data/students"))
            .toAbsolutePath().normalize();
        Files.createDirectories(base.getParent());
        String url = "jdbc:h2:file:" + base.toString().replace('\\', '/');
        System.out.println("Database file: " + base + ".mv.db");
        return Persistence.createEntityManagerFactory("school-lab",
            Map.of("jakarta.persistence.jdbc.url", url));
    }
}

A persistence unit is a named set of entities and settings. school-lab in XML must match the name in createEntityManagerFactory. RESOURCE_LOCAL means our Java code controls transactions on this database connection rather than an application server doing it.

Path.of turns text into a file location. toAbsolutePath().normalize() resolves it against the current folder and tidies it. Files.createDirectories creates the parent folder if needed. Map.of supplies a setting by name; here it overrides the JDBC URL in the unit. EntityManagerFactory prepares the provider and can create working EntityManagers. Open one factory per program, then close it when the program ends.

The lab uses H2's local sa account with an empty password and Hibernate's built-in pool. hibernate.hbm2ddl.auto=update creates missing tables and applies supported schema changes for this exercise. It keeps existing rows. These settings are for this local teaching lab; production database access, pooling and controlled schema migrations need their own design. Maven's clean removes build output, not this data folder.

Step 5 — save one row and prove it survives

Read the add method inside StudentStore.java. Type it inside that class, replacing its existing add method if working in your own copy. Do not put it inside another method or leave two identical method declarations.

src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentStore.java — add method
    public Student add(String name) {
        Student student = new Student(name);
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        EntityTransaction tx = em.getTransaction();
        try {
            tx.begin();
            em.persist(student);
            tx.commit();
            return student;
        } catch (RuntimeException problem) {
            if (tx.isActive()) tx.rollback();
            throw problem;
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }
InstructionWhat happens
new Student(name)Create and validate an ordinary Java object. No committed database row yet.
factory.createEntityManager()Open a persistence context, a set of entity objects tracked for this work.
em.getTransaction() / tx.begin()Get the transaction object and start a unit of work.
em.persist(student)Make the entity managed. With this identity strategy an INSERT may occur now to obtain its id; it is still uncommitted.
tx.commit()Successfully finish the transaction and keep the insert. Only after it succeeds does add return saved data.
catch (RuntimeException problem)If an operation fails, roll back the active transaction and rethrow the same failure for the caller to handle.
finally / em.close()Run cleanup whether the method succeeds, returns or throws. Release this EntityManager; the factory stays available for other operations.
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar list
No students.
Rows: 0

If your personal database already has rows, list them and record that starting state. Empty output above is for a fresh project copy.

java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar add "Amina"
Saved 1: Amina

Hibernate also prints startup information; the first provider start on this computer took tens of seconds. Wait for the final application lines and check the printed database file. If it appears stuck, record the last log line before starting another process. Your actual generated id may differ; record the id returned by add. Generated ids are identifiers, not row counts, and need not be consecutive after failed or deleted work.

java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar list
1: Amina
Rows: 1

Each command starts and finishes a separate Java process. The second list finds the committed row in the file after the add program has closed. This is the evidence of persistence. Do not edit the .mv.db file in a text editor. On Windows you can see the file with:

Get-ChildItem data
YOU CHANGE • SAVE A SECOND ROW

Add " Tunde ". Predict the stored name, record the returned id, then list again. You should have both students, with no surrounding spaces in Tunde’s name. Explain why stopping the program did not erase Amina.

Step 6 — find one row or query by name

src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentStore.java — find method
    public Student find(long id) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        try {
            return em.find(Student.class, id);
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }
src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentStore.java — search method
    public List<Student> search(String name) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        try {
            return em.createQuery("SELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.name = :name ORDER BY s.id", Student.class)
                .setParameter("name", name).getResultList();
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

em.find(Student.class, id) uses the primary key and returns a Student or null. Student.class identifies the entity type. The JPQL query uses Student and s.name, not table students or column full_name. :name is a placeholder; setParameter("name", name) supplies its value. The placeholder omits its colon when binding. This query matches the exact stored text; it does not trim or ignore case.

getResultList() returns a List, possibly empty. ORDER BY s.id makes displayed order predictable. After em.close, returned entities are detached: that context no longer tracks them. Reading their simple id/name fields is fine here; changing one afterward does not automatically update the database.

java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar find 1
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar search "Amina"
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar search "amina"
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar find 999999

Use your actual Amina id in place of 1. Expected: Amina found; one exact-name match; zero lowercase matches; “Student not found.” for an id absent from your list. Missing data is a normal result, different from a database connection failure.

Step 7 — update a managed entity, then cancel a different update

src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentStore.java — rename method
    public boolean rename(long id, String name, boolean cancel) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        EntityTransaction tx = em.getTransaction();
        try {
            tx.begin();
            Student student = em.find(Student.class, id);
            if (student == null) {
                tx.rollback();
                return false;
            }
            student.setName(name);
            if (cancel) {
                em.flush();
                tx.rollback();
            } else {
                tx.commit();
            }
            return true;
        } catch (RuntimeException problem) {
            if (tx.isActive()) tx.rollback();
            throw problem;
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

Find inside the transaction to get a managed object, then call its setter. Hibernate tracks the change and writes it when the transaction commits: this is dirty checking. No second persist call is needed for that already managed object. If find returns null, roll back and return false.

The boolean cancel selects our teaching experiment. The normal path commits. The cancel path calls flush and then rolls back. Flush sends pending changes to the database within the transaction; it is not commit. Rollback cancels those database changes. The object in memory might still contain the edited text after rollback, so we close that context and read again in a new one to check what was actually saved.

java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar rename 1 "Amina Bello"
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar find 1
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar rollback 1 "Temporary Name"
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar find 1

Replace 1 with the recorded id. Expected: “Change committed.” → Amina Bello; “Change rolled back.” → still Amina Bello. Predict each read before running it. Both reads use a fresh process and context.

WE DO • REJECTION ALSO PRESERVES OLD DATA

Try rename with spaces only, then find the same id. The launcher should print “Rejected: Name must contain 1 to 60 characters after trimming.” The original name remains. Read the setter and catch/finally path to explain why.

Step 8 — delete only your own practice row

src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentStore.java — delete method
    public boolean delete(long id) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        EntityTransaction tx = em.getTransaction();
        try {
            tx.begin();
            Student student = em.find(Student.class, id);
            if (student == null) {
                tx.rollback();
                return false;
            }
            em.remove(student);
            tx.commit();
            return true;
        } catch (RuntimeException problem) {
            if (tx.isActive()) tx.rollback();
            throw problem;
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

em.remove marks the managed entity for deletion; commit keeps that deletion. Use the id of the fictional Tunde row you created, keeping Amina for the REST bridge. Substitute the actual id below; 2 is an example, not a guaranteed id.

java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar delete 2
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar find 2
java -jar target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar list

Expected: “Student deleted.”; “Student not found.”; a list without that row. Run delete on that same id again: it reports not found. Do not delete an entire database folder to practise one-row removal.

Full helper and launcher — locate the methods you just practised
session1-jpa-lab/src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentStore.java
package com.aptech.persistence;

import java.util.List;
import jakarta.persistence.EntityManager;
import jakarta.persistence.EntityManagerFactory;
import jakarta.persistence.EntityTransaction;

public class StudentStore {
    private final EntityManagerFactory factory;

    public StudentStore(EntityManagerFactory factory) {
        this.factory = factory;
    }

    public List<Student> list() {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        try {
            return em.createQuery("SELECT s FROM Student s ORDER BY s.id", Student.class)
                .getResultList();
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

    public Student find(long id) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        try {
            return em.find(Student.class, id);
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

    public List<Student> search(String name) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        try {
            return em.createQuery("SELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.name = :name ORDER BY s.id", Student.class)
                .setParameter("name", name).getResultList();
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

    public Student add(String name) {
        Student student = new Student(name);
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        EntityTransaction tx = em.getTransaction();
        try {
            tx.begin();
            em.persist(student);
            tx.commit();
            return student;
        } catch (RuntimeException problem) {
            if (tx.isActive()) tx.rollback();
            throw problem;
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

    public boolean rename(long id, String name, boolean cancel) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        EntityTransaction tx = em.getTransaction();
        try {
            tx.begin();
            Student student = em.find(Student.class, id);
            if (student == null) {
                tx.rollback();
                return false;
            }
            student.setName(name);
            if (cancel) {
                em.flush();
                tx.rollback();
            } else {
                tx.commit();
            }
            return true;
        } catch (RuntimeException problem) {
            if (tx.isActive()) tx.rollback();
            throw problem;
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }

    public boolean delete(long id) {
        EntityManager em = factory.createEntityManager();
        EntityTransaction tx = em.getTransaction();
        try {
            tx.begin();
            Student student = em.find(Student.class, id);
            if (student == null) {
                tx.rollback();
                return false;
            }
            em.remove(student);
            tx.commit();
            return true;
        } catch (RuntimeException problem) {
            if (tx.isActive()) tx.rollback();
            throw problem;
        } finally {
            em.close();
        }
    }
}
session1-jpa-lab/src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/PersistenceLab.java
package com.aptech.persistence;

import java.util.List;
import jakarta.persistence.EntityManagerFactory;

public class PersistenceLab {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        if (args.length == 0 || !validCommand(args)) {
            System.err.println("Use: list | add \"name\" | find id | search \"name\" | rename id \"name\" | rollback id \"name\" | delete id");
            System.exit(2);
        }
        EntityManagerFactory factory = Database.open();
        int exitCode = 0;
        try {
            StudentStore store = new StudentStore(factory);
            String command = args[0];
            switch (command) {
                case "list" -> print(store.list());
                case "add" -> {
                    Student saved = store.add(args[1]);
                    System.out.println("Saved " + saved.getId() + ": " + saved.getName());
                }
                case "find" -> {
                    Student found = store.find(id(args[1]));
                    System.out.println(found == null ? "Student not found." : found.getId() + ": " + found.getName());
                }
                case "search" -> print(store.search(args[1]));
                case "rename", "rollback" -> {
                    boolean cancel = "rollback".equals(command);
                    boolean found = store.rename(id(args[1]), args[2], cancel);
                    System.out.println(!found ? "Student not found." : cancel ? "Change rolled back." : "Change committed.");
                }
                case "delete" -> System.out.println(store.delete(id(args[1])) ? "Student deleted." : "Student not found.");
                default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException("Unknown command.");
            }
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException problem) {
            System.err.println("Rejected: " + problem.getMessage());
            exitCode = 2;
        } finally {
            factory.close();
        }
        if (exitCode != 0) System.exit(exitCode);
    }

    private static boolean validCommand(String[] args) {
        return switch (args[0]) {
            case "list" -> args.length == 1;
            case "add", "find", "search", "delete" -> args.length == 2;
            case "rename", "rollback" -> args.length == 3;
            default -> false;
        };
    }

    private static long id(String text) {
        long value = Long.parseLong(text);
        if (value < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("Id must be positive.");
        return value;
    }

    private static void print(List<Student> students) {
        if (students.isEmpty()) System.out.println("No students.");
        for (Student student : students) {
            System.out.println(student.getId() + ": " + student.getName());
        }
        System.out.println("Rows: " + students.size());
    }
}

The launcher checks the command and number of arguments before opening a database. Its switch selects a helper method; -> in this switch selects one case, rather than the request-handler lambda used earlier. Long.parseLong converts an id argument to a number. System.exit(2) reports rejected input after cleanup. This launcher is supplied scaffolding; explain and write the entity/store decisions before attempting to reproduce its command parser.

Step 9 — use the first useful clue to repair a problem

ObservationCheckAction
jakarta.persistence does not existDid you use bare javac?Use mvn package in the JPA project containing this pom.
No persistence provider / no unit school-labIs META-INF/persistence.xml included? Do names match?Use the supplied Maven layout, provider dependency and services transformer. Rebuild before rerunning.
Database may be already in useIs PersistentServer or another lab process using the same file?Press Enter in that server terminal to stop it before running console commands. Use one program at a time for this file.
List unexpectedly emptyDoes the printed database path match the earlier one?Return to the same project folder. Do not add duplicate rows while looking at another database.
Query cannot resolve full_nameDid you use a column name in JPQL?Use s.name; keep full_name in @Column.
Name rejected / invalid numeric idRead the validation reason.Use 1–60 nonblank characters or an actual positive numeric id. The CLI exits with code 2 for rejected values.
Access denied / file cannot openIs your project copy writable?Use a writable personal copy and keep the printed database path in your error log.
Old behaviour after a source editWas source saved and rebuilt?Stop the server if running; save, mvn package, then rerun/restart.

Step 10 — complete a real persistence assignment

YOU DO • BOOKS THAT SURVIVE A RESTART

In your personal project copy create Book.java, BookStore.java and BookLab.java under the same package. Map Book to library_books, id to book_id with identity generation and title to book_title. Validate trimmed titles of 1–80 characters. Add Book’s fully qualified class name beside Student's class entry in persistence.xml.

  1. Write Book getters, setter, no-argument and title constructors.
  2. Write store methods to add, list, find and search exact title using a bound parameter.
  3. Write a title update that commits and a separate attempt that rolls back after flush. Add deletion of a found managed book.
  4. In BookLab main, open Database.open, create your store, call one operation, print results and close the factory in finally. Begin with a hardcoded add call; after it works, change main to a list call and rebuild. A full command parser is not required.
  5. Build using mvn package; run the new class using the command below. Later, extend main to select operations or keep one operation per run while checking each result.
java -cp target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar com.aptech.persistence.BookLab
Required evidenceAcceptance
Create two books, then close and run a list-only program.Both titles and generated ids remain.
Find an existing id and an absent id.The correct book or an explicit not-found result.
Exact-title query and a nonmatching title.Only expected rows; query values supplied with setParameter.
Title lengths 1, 80, 81 and spaces only.Accept 1 and 80; reject 81 and blank without adding/changing a row.
Commit an update, then try a different update with rollback.A fresh read retains only the committed title.
Delete one of your books, close and read again.Only that book is absent; the other remains.
Explain + new instructor variation.Identify the table/field names, transaction boundaries, and why an edited detached object is not enough.

Submit your source, exact folder/build/run commands, starting database path, generated ids, prediction/result table and one repaired error. Do not submit only a database file or copy the Student implementation unchanged with no explanation. The instructor should run your commands in your personal copy and ask for one small new requirement.

Small hints after an attempt

Change entity types and JPQL entity/field names together. Keep physical names in @Table/@Column. Register Book in persistence.xml before running it. The constructor can validate through its setter. The factory can be reused, but each store operation needs its own EntityManager. Always close it in finally and roll back an active failed transaction.

1.9 Independent practice: make the ideas stick

Close the guided solutions. Work from these requirements and your own notes. Use compiler errors as feedback; ask your instructor to explain a term if needed. Before each run, write what you expect. Passing once by accident is not enough: explain the result and test a boundary or failure case.

TaskDo it yourselfHow to check
D1 — Java recallFrom a blank file, create CampusCard.java. Keep a student name and completed-task count in variables. Print both and choose a readiness message at a threshold of 4.Test counts 3, 4, and 5. Predict which branch runs at exactly 4.
D2 — method recallCreate CourseReply.java with a method returning a title for GET_TITLE, a level for GET_LEVEL, and an unknown-request message otherwise.Test both known requests, an unknown request, and a lower-case spelling. Explain your exact-match rule.
D3 — HTTP transferUsing the library setup from 1.6 as reference, add GET /centre to your service with your centre name. Design the branch yourself.Check status and body for /centre, /greeting, /missing, and POST /centre. Retest old routes after adding the new one.

After your attempt: self-review questions

Did your class name match the filename? Did you compile after saving? Does every reply branch return text? Did you change the old server process or only the source file? Did you preserve 404 and 405? Did you use the Java attribute in JPQL? These questions identify mistakes without giving you a full submission to copy.

Next-day recall: after a break of at least one day, write a two-branch reply method again without opening its original code. Then explain SOAP versus REST and JPA's job aloud. If you cannot, revisit that topic and try another small example.

1.10 When something goes wrong

First identify the stage: compile (javac), start/run (java), or request (browser/curl). Read the first useful error. Check one suspected cause, make one change, and try again. Do not replace the entire program because of a one-character mistake.

Debugging means finding a problem’s cause and correcting it. A compile-time error is reported while checking source, such as a missing semicolon. A runtime error happens during execution, such as failure to bind a port. A logic error means valid code runs but chooses the wrong result. A test compares a predicted result with what actually happens. A test log records the input, expectation, observation and any difference. A trace follows execution step by step; a simulation demonstrates a model of it.

What you seeLikely causeTry this
javac is not recognisedNo JDK compiler on PATH, or terminal opened before installation.Check javac -version in a new terminal; install the JDK with PATH support.
file not found: HelloJava.javaWrong current folder or wrong extension.Run Get-Location and Get-ChildItem. Check for HelloJava.java.txt.
class ... is public, should be declared in a file named ...Public class name and filename differ.Make both exactly match, including capitals.
';' expected / reached end of file while parsingA missing semicolon, quote, or brace.Inspect the reported line and preceding line. Count matching braces from the outside inward.
cannot find symbolA misspelled name or use outside the variable's scope.Compare capitals and declare the variable in a block where it is accessible.
variable ... might not have been initializedA possible route through the method never assigns the variable.In the HTTP handler, every branch must set status and response.
Could not find or load main classWrong folder/class name or no compiled class.Return to the beginner folder, compile successfully, then run the matching class name without .class.
BindException / Address already in useAnother process is already listening on port 8081, often your previous server.Stop your own previous server with Ctrl+C. If another application owns the port, use 8082 in your code and all request URLs, then recompile.
Connection refused / browser cannot connectServer stopped, failed to start, or wrong host/port.Check terminal A for successful startup. Use the printed http://127.0.0.1:8081 address.
404 with Resource not found.You reached the server, but the exact path did not match.Use /greeting with the same case; /Greeting and /greeting/ differ in our explicit checks.
405 with Use GET for this service.You reached the server using an unsupported method.Use GET. A 405 is the intended behaviour for the POST test.
Old greeting after editingUnsaved source, missing compilation, or old process still running.Save → Ctrl+C → javac → java → reload.
package jakarta.persistence does not existThe conceptual JPA example was treated as a JDK-only runnable file.Keep it as a mapping study. Real JPA execution requires a provider and configured database project.
Optional: understand the original socket demo in this project

The original SimpleServiceServer and SimpleServiceClient exchange lines over a raw TCP socket on port 5000. That is a client/server networking demonstration, not an HTTP, SOAP, or REST service. A JSON-shaped reply does not change the transport into HTTP. A browser expects HTTP and cannot use that custom protocol by visiting localhost:5000. Use the FirstWebService lab for today's web-service exercise.

If your instructor demonstrates the older files, both terminals must start in the project root. Compile both with:

javac -d session1-intro/out session1-intro/src/main/java/com/aptech/session1/SimpleServiceServer.java session1-intro/src/main/java/com/aptech/session1/SimpleServiceClient.java

Terminal A:

java -cp session1-intro/out com.aptech.session1.SimpleServiceServer

Terminal B, also at the project root:

java -cp session1-intro/out com.aptech.session1.SimpleServiceClient

TCP (Transmission Control Protocol) provides a reliable, ordered stream of bytes between connected programs. A socket is a program’s communication endpoint. The optional demo sends lines using its own message rules over TCP; HTTP defines additional rules for request and response messages. Transport refers to how data is carried between programs. A custom protocol uses rules chosen by the application’s authors.

The project root is the top folder containing this project’s session folders. Package com.aptech.session1 groups its Java types; com.aptech.session1.SimpleServiceServer is a fully qualified class name, including the package. -d selects the compiler’s output directory; -cp selects the classpath, where the launcher looks for compiled classes. Stop terminal A with Ctrl+C when finished. This is optional enrichment after the beginner HTTP lab.

1.11 Assignment: build a small campus information service

Scenario: your centre wants a service students can use to find campus information. Build your own CampusInfoService.java from the library structure you studied. You may refer to the JDK setup and your notes, but write the routing decisions and messages yourself without AI or a copied completed solution.

  1. The matching public class must run an HTTP server on 127.0.0.1:8082. Use a different port from the guided example.
  2. GET /welcome returns a welcome message you wrote with status 200.
  3. GET /course returns the course title with status 200.
  4. GET /hours returns your stated opening hours with status 200.
  5. A GET to any unknown path returns status 404 and a clear message. A non-GET request returns status 405, a clear message and an Allow: GET header.
  6. Stop, compile, and restart after making one change. Prove the new value appears and the other routes still work.
  7. On paper or in a text document, design a Student entity mapped to campus_students with fields id and name mapped to student_id and student_name. Identify the key and write a parameterised JPQL query by name. This part is a design exercise, not a database implementation.

What to submit

  • Your CampusInfoService.java source file and the exact compile/run commands you used. A .class file alone is insufficient.
  • A test log covering all three successful routes, one unknown path and one non-GET request: method, URL, expected status/body and actual status/body. Include evidence of the edited response after restart.
  • Your mapping and JPQL design, plus a short explanation of JPA versus Hibernate versus Spring Data JPA.
  • Five plain-language explanations: where the program starts, when the handler runs, how a branch is chosen, how the reply reaches the browser, and why this program does not yet save records.
  • One error you encountered, what the message meant, and how you fixed it. If none occurred, reproduce and repair the intentional semicolon error from D4.
How to write one row of a test log

For the guided example, a row could read: method: GET; URL: http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting; expected: 200 and Welcome to Aptech!; actual: record the status and body you personally observed; result: match or mismatch. Make a separate row for each assignment test using port 8082 and your own messages. Never fill in an actual result before running the test.

A copied working file alone does not demonstrate understanding. Correct misunderstandings and resubmit if the explanation or tests are incomplete.

1.13 Session 1 term reference: meanings you can use

Use this as a reference while reading and practising, rather than trying to memorise every word at once. Open the group you need. Each entry gives a meaning and an example; the links return you to the relevant lesson. There are 96 entries grouping related terms, including punctuation and the optional socket demo.

Same word, different context: a Java method is program code; an HTTP method describes a network action. A REST resource is exposed by an API; a stream resource needs closing. A JPA provider implements persistence; an SOA provider offers a service. An XML attribute is in a tag; a JPA attribute is an entity property. Read the surrounding example to decide which meaning is intended.

Computer setup and running a program

Read this group in the lesson →

Program, software and application

A program is instructions a computer can execute. Software is programs; an application is software carrying out a task for a user.

Example: Our Java program prints a greeting; a browser is an application that displays web content.

Java, source code and source file

Java is the programming language used here. Source code is the text you write in it. A source file stores that text.

Example: You edit the instructions in HelloJava.java.

JDK, JVM and runtime

The Java Development Kit contains development tools. The Java Virtual Machine executes Java bytecode. A runtime provides the software for execution and may lack a compiler.

Example: Install a JDK so both javac and java are available.

Compiler, compile, bytecode and execute

A compiler checks source against language rules and translates it. Java compilation produces bytecode; execution runs the instructions using the JVM.

Example: javac HelloJava.java creates HelloJava.class; java HelloJava runs that compiled class.

Editor, terminal, shell and PowerShell

An editor changes file text. A terminal is the command window. A shell interprets commands entered there; PowerShell is the shell we use on Windows.

Example: Type Java into the editor and javac HelloJava.java into PowerShell.

Command, option and prompt

A command asks a tool to act. An option adds a setting. A prompt indicates the shell is ready for input.

Example: In javac -version, javac names the tool and -version asks for its version; do not type the PS prompt.

Project, project root, folder and file extension

A project is its collection of files. Its root is the top project folder. A folder groups files. An extension labels a file’s kind.

Example: session1-intro is inside the root; .java identifies source and .class identifies compiled bytecode.

File path and working directory

A file path describes a location on disk. The working directory is the current folder used for relative locations.

Example: Set-Location changes that folder; Get-Location reports it; Get-ChildItem lists its contents.

Absolute and relative paths

An absolute file path gives the full location. A relative file path starts from the current folder.

Example: C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service is absolute; HelloJava.java is relative to the folder PowerShell is in.

PATH and executable

PATH is the list of folders the shell searches for programs. An executable is a file the operating system can run.

Example: Adding the JDK’s tools to PATH lets javac work without typing its full location.

Version, distribution and processor architecture

A version identifies a software release. A distribution is a packaged release. Processor architecture identifies the processor family the software must match.

Example: Temurin distributes JDK releases; choose x64 for an x64 computer or ARM64 for an ARM64 computer.

Process, local, remote and restart

A process is one running program instance. Local means this computer; remote means another computer. Restart means stop one instance and launch a new one.

Example: The local HTTP server is a process. After editing its source, stop, compile and start again.

Build tool, library, dependency and configuration

A build tool automates preparation such as compiling. A library supplies reusable code. A dependency is software needed by a project. Configuration supplies settings.

Example: Session 1 uses the JDK library directly; Gradle can automate later builds. A real JPA project also needs a provider and database settings.

Java words and punctuation

Read this group in the lesson →

Syntax, keyword and identifier

Syntax is the language’s writing rules. A keyword has a built-in meaning. An identifier is a name chosen for a class, variable or method.

Example: class is a keyword; HelloJava and studentName are identifiers.

Statement, block, braces and indentation

A statement is an instruction. A block groups code between braces. Indentation is the leading space showing the structure.

Example: The println instruction ends with a semicolon; the surrounding main block uses { and }.

Class, object, instance and nested class

A class defines a type and its structure. An object is one instance of a class. A nested class is declared inside another class.

Example: Student is a template; each new Student() creates a separate student object inside our demo.

Variable, value and type

A variable is a named place to hold a value. A value is data. A type identifies the kind of value allowed.

Example: String studentName = "Amina" uses a text type, a name and a text value.

Declare, initialise and assign

Declare introduces a variable’s type and name. Initialise gives its first value. Assign stores a value, whether initially or later.

Example: int score; declares. score = 65; assigns the first value. score = 70; changes it.

Literal, String, int and boolean

A literal is a value written directly in source. String holds text; int holds whole numbers within its range; boolean holds true or false.

Example: "Amina" is a String literal; 65 is an integer literal; score >= 50 produces a boolean.

Case-sensitive

Capital and lower-case letters are treated as different when names or exact text are compared.

Example: String is the type; string is a different name. Our explicit path checks distinguish /greeting from /Greeting.

Expression, operator and concatenation

An expression is evaluated to get a value. An operator performs an action. Concatenation joins text.

Example: 2 + 3 evaluates to 5; "Hello, " + studentName joins Strings.

Condition, branch, if, else if and else

A condition produces true or false. A branch is one path through a decision. if checks a condition, else if checks another if earlier ones fail, and else supplies the fallback.

Example: score >= 50 chooses the Pass branch; a smaller score chooses Practise again.

Method declaration, method call, caller and return type

A declaration defines a method. A call asks it to run; the caller makes that request. A return type says what kind of result comes back.

Example: greet declares a String return type; main calls greet("Amina") and receives text.

Parameter and argument

A parameter is the named input slot in a declaration. An argument is the actual value passed in a call.

Example: name is the parameter in greet(String name); "Amina" is the argument in greet("Amina").

Return, print, output and void

return gives control and optionally a result to the caller. Printing displays data. Output is the data produced. void means the method has no result value.

Example: return builds no visible page by itself; println displays its argument in the terminal.

main, static and public

main is the launcher’s entry method in these examples. static belongs to the class and can be used without a class instance. public permits access wherever the containing type is accessible.

Example: Java can call public static void main(String[] args) without constructing HelloJava.

Array, [] and length

An array holds an ordered group of elements with a fixed size once created. [] marks an array type. length gives its number of elements.

Example: String[] args holds launch arguments; body.length counts the bytes in byte[] body.

Local variable, field, member and scope

A local variable is declared inside a method or block. A field is a variable belonging to an object or class. Fields and methods are members. Scope is where a name can be used.

Example: Student.name is a field; message inside main is local and cannot be used by greet.

Reference, new, constructor and no-argument constructor

A reference lets code reach an object. new creates an object using a constructor, the setup code with the class’s name and no return type. No-argument means no input values are supplied.

Example: Student student = new Student(); stores a reference to the created Student; its constructor call has empty parentheses.

Memory and RAM

Random Access Memory is temporary working storage used while a program runs. Creating an object there does not save it in a database.

Example: The student object in StudentCardDemo does not remain as a stored student record after the program ends.

Package, import and fully qualified class name

A package groups Java types. import lets source use a type’s short name. A fully qualified class name includes its package.

Example: import java.net.InetSocketAddress lets code write InetSocketAddress instead of java.net.InetSocketAddress.

Parentheses (), dot ., quotes "" and semicolon ;

Parentheses contain inputs or group expressions. A dot selects a member. Quotes delimit a text literal. A semicolon ends statements such as assignments or method calls.

Example: System.out.println("Hello"); selects out and println, supplies text in parentheses and ends the instruction.

=, ==, equals, >=, < and !

= assigns. == compares primitive values or object references. String.equals compares text contents. >= means at least, < means less than and ! reverses a boolean.

Example: score = 65 stores 65; score >= 50 checks it; !"GET".equals(method) checks whether text differs from GET.

private and protected

private restricts ordinary access to the declaring class’s context. protected permits package access and certain access from subclasses. A subclass is a class based on another class.

Example: The JPA Student example hides fields with private but provides a public no-argument constructor.

Exception, throws and try-with-resources

An exception reports a problem during execution. throws declares that it may be passed to the caller. try-with-resources automatically closes declared resources when the block finishes.

Example: The server may fail to bind its port. try (var output = ...) ensures its output stream is closed.

var and type inference

var asks the compiler to infer the local variable’s type from its initial value. It does not allow the variable to switch freely between types.

Example: var output = exchange.getResponseBody() gives output the returned stream type.

Lambda, ->, handler, context and registration

A lambda supplies a function’s behaviour. A handler deals with an event. A server context connects a path prefix to a handler. Registering tells the server which handler to call.

Example: createContext("/", exchange -> { ... }) registers behaviour; it runs later when a request arrives.

Library, framework and module

A library supplies reusable code. A framework supplies application structure and calls your code at defined points. A Java module groups packages and controls their availability.

Example: HttpServer is in the jdk.httpserver module; Jersey supplies the service runtime used in Session 2.

Byte, bit, character encoding, UTF-8 and charset

A bit is 0 or 1; eight bits form a byte. Encoding maps text to bytes. UTF-8 is Unicode Transformation Format, 8-bit, a common encoding. charset labels an HTTP text encoding.

Example: A uses one byte and é uses two in UTF-8; use the byte array’s length for this HTTP body.

Stream, write and close

A stream carries a sequence of data. Writing sends data to it; closing releases the associated resources.

Example: output.write(body) sends response bytes towards the client; the try block closes that output stream.

Web addresses and HTTP messages

Read this group in the lesson →

Network, internet and browser

A network connects devices for data exchange. The internet connects networks worldwide. A browser requests and displays web content.

Example: Your browser can call the Java service on your own computer using a loopback connection.

Web service, API and endpoint

A web service is software callable using agreed web communication rules. An Application Programming Interface describes how software is used. An endpoint is an available service address.

Example: GET at http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting selects our greeting endpoint.

Client/requester and server/provider

The client makes a request. The server receives it and provides a response. These are roles; the same application can act as both in different interactions.

Example: The browser requests the greeting; the Java process serves it.

Request, response and interoperability

A request asks for work or information. A response is the reply. Interoperability means different systems can communicate using agreed rules.

Example: A browser can understand an HTTP response sent by a Java program.

HTTP, protocol and HTTP/1.1

Hypertext Transfer Protocol defines request/response communication rules. A protocol is an agreement about message exchange. HTTP/1.1 identifies one version of HTTP.

Example: The response starts with HTTP/1.1 200 OK in the shortened curl output.

URL, scheme, host, IP address, port and URL path

A Uniform Resource Locator gives a web address. Its scheme selects the access mechanism; the host identifies the computer; an Internet Protocol address can identify that host; the port selects the listener; the path selects a resource.

Example: http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting uses http, host 127.0.0.1, port 8081 and path /greeting.

Loopback, localhost and resolve

Loopback sends traffic back to the current computer. localhost is a name normally resolving to a loopback address. Resolve means translate a host name to an address.

Example: 127.0.0.1 on your phone refers to the phone, not your laptop’s Java server.

URI and URN

Uniform Resource Identifier is the broader term for an identifier. A URL locates something. A Uniform Resource Name identifies something by name without giving a location.

Example: urn:aptech:students names the example XML namespace; it is not a lab URL to open.

Listen, bind, connection and queue

A server binds (reserves) its local address and port and listens (waits) for connections. A connection links the communicating programs. A queue holds connections awaiting acceptance.

Example: HttpServer.create reserves port 8081; server.start begins listening.

Resource and representation

An API resource is something exposed to clients, such as a student or greeting. A representation is the data describing that resource.

Example: Student 101 is the resource; {"id":101,"name":"Amina"} is one JSON representation.

HTTP method versus Java method

An HTTP method identifies a requested action in a network message. A Java method is a named block of program behaviour. They are different uses of the same word.

Example: GET asks to read /greeting; getRequestMethod() is a Java call that reads that HTTP method.

GET, POST, PUT, PATCH and DELETE

GET reads without requesting a resource change. POST submits for processing, often creation. PUT creates/replaces at a target address. PATCH applies a partial change. DELETE requests removal.

Example: The Session 1 handler supports only GET; POST is intentionally rejected with 405.

Header, body, Host, Accept and Content-Type

Headers provide message information; the body carries its main data. Host names the destination, Accept requests response formats and Content-Type identifies the format of the attached body.

Example: Accept: application/json requests JSON; Content-Type: text/plain labels the plain-text reply.

Media type, text/plain, application/json and Allow

A media type labels a representation’s format. text/plain labels ordinary text; application/json labels JSON. The Allow header lists supported HTTP methods.

Example: Our 405 reply includes Allow: GET and a plain-text explanation in its body.

Status code: 200, 404 and 405

A response’s three-digit code gives a standard result signal. 200 OK means success, 404 Not Found means the requested resource was not found, and 405 Method Not Allowed means the method is unsupported there.

Example: GET /missing returns 404; POST /greeting returns 405 in this handler.

HTTPS, TLS, encryption and deployment

HTTPS uses HTTP protected by Transport Layer Security. Encryption makes traffic unreadable without the appropriate key. Deploying places and configures an application where its users can reach it.

Example: Our lab uses local HTTP; publicly deployed services commonly use HTTPS.

curl.exe, -i, -X and developer tools

curl is a network request tool; curl.exe names its Windows executable. -i includes response headers; -X sets the HTTP method. Browser developer tools let you inspect network exchanges.

Example: curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8081/greeting tests the expected 405.

Alias and default

A shell alias is a shortcut name for another command. A default is the setting used when no alternative is supplied.

Example: In some PowerShell versions curl is an alias; use curl.exe. These curl examples default to GET unless a method is selected.

Authentication, authorisation, validation and error handling

Authentication verifies who a caller is; authorisation checks permitted actions. Validation checks input rules. Error handling determines how failures are reported or recovered from.

Example: A required name check is validation; permission to edit a student is authorisation.

SOA, SOAP, REST and data formats

Read this group in the lesson →

Architecture, interface and implementation

Architecture arranges a system’s parts. An interface describes how to use one part. Its implementation does the work behind that agreement.

Example: Clients know how to ask the course service without reading its private storage code.

SOA, registry and publish/find/bind

Service-Oriented Architecture organises capabilities as reusable services. A registry is a service directory. Publish shares a description; find discovers it; bind means use its address and interface to call it.

Example: The student portal and reception app can reuse a course service; the local lab already knows its address.

UDDI, API gateway and routing

Universal Description, Discovery and Integration is a standard for service directories. An API gateway forwards requests to services. Routing chooses a destination or handler.

Example: A gateway can forward a course request; it does not automatically serve as a registry.

SOAP, Envelope, Body, Header and Fault

SOAP is a message protocol, historically Simple Object Access Protocol. Envelope is its outer XML wrapper; Body carries the task/result; an optional Header carries extra information; a Fault in the Body reports an error.

Example: getStudent and id 101 are inside the sample SOAP Body. SOAP elements are separate from HTTP headers.

Operation, contract and WSDL

An operation is a named service task. A contract specifies available tasks, inputs and results. Web Services Description Language can document a SOAP service’s contract and address.

Example: getStudent takes an id and returns student information; WSDL can describe that agreement.

REST, constraint, uniform interface and stateless

Representational State Transfer is an architectural style with constraints (design rules). Its uniform interface uses consistent interaction rules around identified resources and representations. Stateless requests supply the information needed for interpretation without relying on a hidden earlier conversation.

Example: GET /students/101 reads that student; storing student records in a database does not break statelessness.

OpenAPI and WS-*

OpenAPI describes HTTP API paths, inputs and replies. WS-* is a family of web-service standards, including WS-Security for SOAP message security.

Example: They are descriptive/standards terms in the comparison table, not commands used by today’s Java lab.

HTML, XML, JSON and plain text

HyperText Markup Language describes webpage content. eXtensible Markup Language labels structured data with tags. JavaScript Object Notation expresses structured data in text. Plain text has no required markup/data structure.

Example: A student can be JSON {"name":"Amina"}, XML <name>Amina</name>, or a plain-text name.

XML tag, element, attribute and root

A tag is a label in angle brackets. An element includes tags and contents. An attribute gives information inside an opening tag. The root is the outermost element.

Example: <s:id>101</s:id> is an element; SOAP’s Envelope is the root in this document.

XML namespace, prefix and xmlns

A namespace identifies the vocabulary of an XML name. A prefix is its local short label. xmlns declares the relationship between that prefix and a namespace identifier.

Example: xmlns:s="urn:aptech:students" makes s:id part of the student vocabulary; another prefix may identify SOAP’s vocabulary.

JSON object, key/value pair and data format

A JSON object is a text structure of named values between braces. The key names a property; the value supplies its data. A data format defines how such data is arranged.

Example: In {"id":101}, id is the key and 101 is the value. This JSON object represents data from a Java object.

JAX-RS, Jersey and JAX-WS

JAX-RS was Java API for RESTful Web Services, now Jakarta RESTful Web Services. Jersey implements that API. JAX-WS was Java API for XML Web Services, now Jakarta XML Web Services, commonly used for SOAP.

Example: Session 2 uses Jersey to handle resource routing; Session 3’s topic is JAX-WS.

Databases, JPA and queries

Read this group in the lesson →

Database, relational database, table, record, row and column

A database is organised stored data managed by software. A relational database uses related tables. A table groups rows and columns; a row is one record; a column stores one property across records.

Example: The students table has a row for Amina and columns id and full_name.

Persistence and durable data

Persistence retains data beyond the program’s lifetime. Durable committed changes remain stored beyond the current run.

Example: A database student record can survive restarting the application; new Student() alone does not save a record.

Primary key and identifier

An identifier distinguishes an item. A primary key uniquely identifies a database row; other rows in that table cannot use the same key.

Example: id 101 identifies Amina’s row in students.

JPA and Jakarta EE

JPA is the familiar abbreviation for the former Java Persistence API, now Jakarta Persistence, a specification for object/relational persistence. Jakarta Enterprise Edition groups specifications for Java application services.

Example: JPA defines persistence operations; it does not create our HTTP route.

Specification, implementation and provider

A specification defines rules. An implementation follows them. A JPA provider is the implementation your application uses.

Example: JPA supplies the agreement; Hibernate ORM or EclipseLink performs the persistence work.

Hibernate ORM, EclipseLink, PostgreSQL and Spring Data JPA

Hibernate ORM and EclipseLink implement JPA. PostgreSQL is database software. Spring Data JPA is a helper framework providing repository support on top of JPA.

Example: The provider translates entity work into database operations; the repository helper makes application data-access code easier to write.

Repository, dependency, configuration and compatibility

A repository groups data-access operations. A dependency is needed software. Configuration gives settings. Compatibility means chosen releases can work together.

Example: A JPA project needs compatible API/provider libraries and settings for its database connection.

Entity, attribute, mapping, ORM and metadata

An entity is a Java type whose instances can be managed for persistence. Here an attribute is its Java field. Mapping connects object and table structures; Object-Relational Mapping is ORM. Metadata describes those connections.

Example: Student maps to students; the Java attribute name maps to the database column full_name.

Annotation, @Entity, @Table, @Id and @Column

An annotation attaches metadata in Java source. @Entity marks an entity type; @Table names its table; @Id marks its identifier; @Column supplies column mapping.

Example: @Column(name = "full_name") explains that Java’s name field uses a differently named database column.

Field access, naming strategy and @ManyToOne

Field access means the provider uses fields directly. A naming strategy supplies default physical names. @ManyToOne describes a relationship where many instances can refer to one related instance.

Example: Many students can belong to one centre; relationship implementation is a later database topic.

EntityManager, managed entity and persistence context

EntityManager is JPA’s interface for managing entities. A managed instance is currently tracked in its persistence context, the collection of instances being tracked.

Example: Making a new entity managed is not proof that its database transaction has committed.

persist, find and remove

persist makes a new entity managed. find retrieves an entity by primary key. remove marks a managed entity for deletion. Durable changes require successful transaction handling.

Example: Finding Student id 101 retrieves its entity; removing it needs a successful transaction to keep the deletion.

Transaction, commit and rollback

A transaction groups database work for completion as a unit. Commit keeps successful changes; rollback cancels the transaction’s database changes.

Example: Student and registration updates can succeed together or be cancelled together after a failure.

Query, SQL and JPQL

A query asks for data. Structured Query Language works with relational data. Jakarta Persistence Query Language works with entities and Java attributes, retaining the familiar JPQL abbreviation from Java Persistence Query Language.

Example: SQL uses students.full_name; JPQL uses the Student entity and its name attribute.

Query alias, named parameter and parameter binding

A query alias is a short name for an entity. A named parameter is a placeholder. Binding supplies its value separately from the query structure.

Example: In SELECT s FROM Student s WHERE s.name = :studentName, s is the alias and :studentName is the placeholder.

SELECT, FROM, WHERE, *, filter and parameterised query

SELECT specifies results, FROM identifies the source, WHERE restricts matches and SQL * selects all columns. Filtering keeps matches. A parameterised query separates its structure from input values.

Example: Bind studentName to Amina rather than concatenating user text into JPQL.

Web layer, application logic and persistence layer

The web layer receives/sends HTTP messages. Application logic decides what work is needed. Persistence handles stored data.

Example: GET supplies id 101; application logic requests that student; JPA/provider retrieves the record.

javax.persistence and jakarta.persistence

These are Java package names used by different Persistence API generations. They group API types; they are not databases or commands.

Example: The displayed mapping uses jakarta.persistence imports; keep API and provider versions compatible.

Troubleshooting and the optional socket demo

Read this group in the lesson →

Compile-time, runtime and logic error; debugging

Compile-time errors occur during compilation. Runtime errors occur during execution. Logic errors produce an unintended result from code that runs. Debugging finds and corrects the cause.

Example: A missing semicolon fails compilation; an occupied port fails startup; the wrong greeting can be a logic error.

Parsing, symbol and initialisation error

Parsing reads source according to syntax rules. A compiler symbol is a name it must resolve. A variable must receive a value before code reads it.

Example: cannot find symbol may mean a misspelling or a name outside scope; not initialized means a possible branch omitted assignment.

Test, expectation, observation, test log and evidence

A test compares expected behaviour with observed behaviour. A log records inputs and results. Evidence records what was actually demonstrated.

Example: Write the expected 404 first; run GET /missing; then record the actual status and body.

Trace, simulation and fallback

A trace follows steps of execution. A simulation shows a model of those steps. A fallback is the behaviour chosen when known cases do not match.

Example: The browser trace explains the final else; the real Java server is the place to verify its 404 response.

TCP, socket, transport and custom protocol

Transmission Control Protocol carries an ordered, reliable byte stream. A socket is a program’s communication endpoint. Transport is how data is carried. A custom protocol uses application-defined message rules.

Example: The old socket demo sends lines over TCP and does not provide HTTP request/response messages.

Classpath, package tree, -cp and -d

The classpath lists locations used to find classes. A package tree arranges compiled files by package name. -cp chooses a classpath; javac -d chooses the output directory.

Example: The optional demo compiles into session1-intro/out and launches the class with its complete package name.

BindException, connection refused and HTTP error response

BindException can report failure to reserve an address/port. Connection refused means no listener accepted that connection. An HTTP error response means a server was reached and replied with an error status.

Example: An occupied 8081 can block startup; 404 with a body means the running handler replied.

WE DO → CONNECT WORDS TO AN EXAMPLE

Explain one line, rather than reciting definitions

Read String message = greet("Amina");. message is a local variable; String is its type; "Amina" is the argument to the method call. The result returned by greet is assigned to message. Now point to the declaration of greet and identify its parameter and return type.

YOU DO → EXPLAIN IN YOUR OWN WORDS

My terms, my examples

  1. Choose five words from the Java group. Write a one-sentence meaning for each and point to a matching part of your own introduction or reply program.

  3. Explain class versus object, parameter versus argument, and resource versus representation to a classmate using your own examples.
  4. Draw the Student → students mapping. Label entity, field/attribute, table, column and primary key. Explain where the provider, query and transaction fit.
  5. The next day, hide this reference and repeat three explanations. Check them afterwards and record the words you need to revisit in your learning log.

For every answer, give a meaning and an example. A copied definition alone is not enough. Use your notes and compiler messages without AI; ask your instructor to check terms you still cannot explain.

Build REST resources with JAX-RS

In Session 1 you read an HTTP method and path, chose a response, and sent it to a browser. Our story is still an Aptech centre: greet a student, list courses, find one course, and accept a new course.

This lesson covers those goals and adds practice. The previous XML page is retained as an optional supplement.
