2.1 What JAX-RS is, and when to use it

🏫

Give reception a routing guide

Imagine reception has a guide: “For course information, use the Course Desk. To read information, ask the read method. To submit a new record, ask the create method.” Reception follows the guide instead of inventing the procedure for each visitor.

In Java, annotations supply the routing guide. Jersey reads the guide, selects a matching method, supplies inputs and turns the returned result into an HTTP response.

JAX-RS is the familiar acronym for the Jakarta RESTful Web Services API and specification. It defines Java annotations, interfaces and behaviour for building REST-style web services. It is not a standalone server, a database, or an application you launch by itself. Its modern package is jakarta.ws.rs; older JAX-RS 2.x code often uses javax.ws.rs.

PieceIts job in our projectDo not confuse it with
Jakarta REST / JAX-RSDefines @Path, @GET, @POST, Response and other API rules.A running server or database.
JerseyImplements the API: matches requests and invokes your resource methods.The JAX-RS specification itself.
JDK HTTP serverAccepts HTTP connections; Jersey’s adapter connects it to the resource methods.The code deciding which course exists.
JacksonConverts Java objects to/from JSON through Jersey integration.A database or all REST rules.
MavenDownloads libraries, compiles code and packages a runnable JAR.The component that handles a browser’s request.
Your resource classContains the actions your API offers.The HTML learning page.

Useful examples include a student portal reading course data, a mobile app checking a timetable, or another service submitting an enrolment. A Java provider and a client written in another language can communicate through agreed HTTP and data-format rules. Framework annotations reduce repetitive routing work, but you still design the resources, validation, statuses, security and storage.

REST means more than using JSON: it is an architectural style with constraints such as a uniform interface and stateless requests. This small local lab practises resource URLs and HTTP semantics. It does not implement production authentication or every REST constraint. We use loopback addresses so requests stay on your own computer.

YOU EXPLAIN

For a student portal, name one resource, one GET request and one POST request. Identify which part should be handled by your Java method and which part Jersey can handle. Then explain why installing a JAX-RS API dependency alone is insufficient to run a service.

2.2 The new Java ideas, in small pieces

Read this bridge before opening the full service. The Java concepts from Session 1 still apply; these are the few additional pieces the project uses.

Packages, imports and annotations

package com.aptech.session2;

import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;

@Path("/greeting")
public class HelloResource {
    @GET
    public String greet() {
        return "Hello, Student!";
    }
}

This is a small standalone resource example for reading. The project’s complete greeting resource appears in 2.4. package gives the class a namespace, so its full name is com.aptech.session2.HelloResource. Under the source root, the matching folder is com/aptech/session2. An import lets us use a library type by its short name.

An annotation starts with @ and describes code to a tool or runtime. @Path("/greeting") attaches a URL path to a resource class. @GET says a method handles HTTP GET. These annotations do not run a server by themselves; Main must start Jersey and register the class. The ordinary Java name greet does not automatically become the URL.

Objects and constructors

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/Course.java
package com.aptech.session2;

public class Course {
    public int id;
    public String title;

    public Course() {
    }

    public Course(int id, String title) {
        this.id = id;
        this.title = title;
    }
}

Course is a plain Java class with public fields id and title. A constructor has the same name as the class and no return type. new Course(1, "Java Foundations") calls the two-argument constructor, which assigns those values to a new object.

In this.id = id;, this.id means the field of the object being created; the plain id on the right is the constructor parameter. this resolves the name overlap. The no-argument constructor creates an initially empty object. We include it to make the simple data class easy for object-binding tools to construct. Public fields keep the beginner example short; encapsulation with accessors is a useful later Java topic.

Null and safe checks

null means a reference has no object value. It is different from "", an empty String, and " ", a String containing spaces. Calling isBlank() on null would fail. A check like input == null || input.title == null || input.title.isBlank() tests from left to right. || means OR and stops once a test is true, so the later dereference is avoided when input is null.

&& means AND and stops when an earlier test is false. In args.length > 0 && "--hello-only".equals(args[0]), Java reads the first array element only if one exists. isBlank() checks empty or whitespace-only text; trim() removes leading and trailing basic whitespace.

Lists, maps and loops

List<Course> result = new ArrayList<>();
for (Course course : courses.values()) {
    result.add(course);
}

This is a fragment, not a complete program. A List<Course> is an ordered collection of Course objects; the angle brackets specify the item type. ArrayList implements that list. A Map<Integer, Course> associates an integer key with each Course object: id → course. Integer is the object form of int used by these generic collections.

The enhanced for loop means “for each Course in this collection, put it in the variable course and execute the block.” result.add(course) appends an item. courses.get(id) retrieves the mapped object or returns null if the key is absent. new LinkedHashMap<>() creates a map that preserves insertion order for this demo.

WE DO → YOU TRY

On paper, create two Course objects with different ids/titles, then draw a map with each id pointing to its object. Predict courses.get(999) when no key 999 exists. Explain why a list of two Course objects becomes an array of two JSON objects rather than one object.

Hints for the paper exercise

Use new Course(id, title) for each object. A missing map key returns null. A list contains multiple values, so its JSON representation uses outer square brackets.

WE TYPE → RUN → CHANGE • BEFORE WRITING A STORE

Practise collection code without a web server

A drawing helps you predict; this small runnable program lets you practise the Java instructions yourself. It uses the JDK only. Complete it before the larger CourseStore in 2.6. The nested Course class is the same one-file technique you used in Session 1: each new object holds its own id and title.

In your editor open session2-jaxrs/beginner/CourseCollectionPractice.java. Read and type the example. PowerShell should be inside session2-jaxrs, as in 2.3. You can run this program while the separate HTTP server is stopped.

session2-jaxrs/beginner/CourseCollectionPractice.java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.LinkedHashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;

public class CourseCollectionPractice {
    static class Course {
        int id;
        String title;

        Course(int id, String title) {
            this.id = id;
            this.title = title;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Map<Integer, Course> courses = new LinkedHashMap<>();
        int nextId = 1;

        Course first = new Course(nextId, "Java Foundations");
        courses.put(nextId, first);
        nextId++;

        Course second = new Course(nextId, "Web Services");
        courses.put(nextId, second);
        nextId++;

        for (Course course : courses.values()) {
            System.out.println(course.id + ": " + course.title);
        }

        Course found = courses.get(2);
        if (found != null) {
            System.out.println("Found: " + found.title);
        }

        Course missing = courses.get(999);
        if (missing == null) {
            System.out.println("Course not found.");
        }

        List<Course> matches = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Course course : courses.values()) {
            if (course.title.contains("Java")) {
                matches.add(course);
            }
        }
        System.out.println("Stored: " + courses.size());
        System.out.println("Java matches: " + matches.size());
        System.out.println("Next id: " + nextId);
    }
}
InstructionWhat you should see in your head
new LinkedHashMap<>()Create an empty map. Each key will be an Integer id and each value a Course object. <> lets the compiler infer the item types from the declaration.
new Course(nextId, ...)Create a course object with the current id and supplied title.
courses.put(nextId, first)Store the object under that id. put uses the key to add or replace an entry; using the same key twice does not add another entry.
nextId++Increase the local number by one for the next new object. It does not change the id field of an object already created.
for (Course course : courses.values())For each stored object, put its reference into course and run the block. The colon here means “in this collection.”
get(2) / get(999)Retrieve the object for id 2, or null when the key is absent. Test for null before using an object’s fields.
new ArrayList<>() / matches.add(course)Create a separate empty result list, then append matching object references. Adding to matches does not add another course to the map.
contains("Java")Check whether the title contains this exact text; this first exercise is case-sensitive. The larger store later normalises case.
size()Count entries/items: map count and matching-list count answer different questions.

Compile and run, one command at a time:

javac beginner/CourseCollectionPractice.java
java -cp beginner CourseCollectionPractice

The file has no package declaration. -cp beginner asks Java to find its compiled classes in that folder. Expected output:

1: Java Foundations
2: Web Services
Found: Web Services
Course not found.
Stored: 2
Java matches: 1
Next id: 3
  1. Trace nextId on paper: 1 → first object → 2 → second object → 3. Explain why the first object’s id is still 1.
  2. Guided change: after creating the second object and incrementing nextId, add a third Course using nextId and title Java REST Practice. Store it and increment nextId. Predict the extra printed course, stored count 3, Java-match count 2 and next id 4. Save, compile and run again.
  3. Independent change: restore the two-course starting data. Change the search condition to find Web. Predict one match. Then search for Python and predict zero. The stored count must remain 2 in both cases.
  4. Independent lookup: search for id 1, then an absent id. Write one if/else that prints the title when found and Course not found when absent. Do not read title from null. Test both branches.
  5. Close the reference. From your notes, write a small map with two objects, a lookup and a loop collecting matches. Ask your instructor to request a different search value. If you cannot write those steps yet, repeat this lab before the book-store assignment.

Link to the web lesson: CourseStore.add packages object creation, put and nextId++ into a reusable method; find wraps get; list wraps the filtering loop. Jersey receives requests and calls these methods. The collections do not implement HTTP or JSON conversion themselves.

2.3 Prepare the JAX-RS project

Session 1’s simple server used only the JDK. This project needs Jersey and JSON libraries. A dependency is another library our program uses. Maven manages these dependencies and builds all the files together; typing javac HelloResource.java alone does not supply the required JARs.

Step 1 — check the JDK and Maven

Open PowerShell. Type each command separately:

java -version
javac -version
mvn -version

Use JDK 17 or newer and Maven 3.9.x. Maven’s version output also reports the Java version it uses; that must be 17 or newer. On this project’s current machine Maven is already installed. If your own machine says mvn is not recognised, your instructor can help with the one-time setup below.

Windows Maven setup, if it is missing
  1. Install/check the JDK using Session 1’s setup first.
  2. Download the binary ZIP for Maven 3.9.x from Apache Maven. Extract it into a tools folder you own.
  3. Find its bin folder, for example C:\Users\YourName\tools\apache-maven-3.9.x\bin. Replace YourName and the version with the actual folder; this example is not a literal command.
  4. Open Windows Start and search for “Edit environment variables for your account.” Edit your user Path, add the full Maven bin-folder path as a new entry, and save.
  5. Open a new PowerShell window and repeat mvn -version. If it cannot find Java, ask the instructor to check JAVA_HOME points to your JDK folder, not its bin folder.

Do not install random Maven executables from unrelated websites. The official binary ZIP and your JDK are sufficient.

Step 2 — enter the project root

Set-Location "C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service\session2-jaxrs"
Get-ChildItem

You should see pom.xml, src, and the two example JSON files. If you move the repository, use the new path. All build commands and file-body requests below assume this folder. A second terminal for curl must also enter this folder when it reads a JSON file.

session2-jaxrs/
├── pom.xml
├── new-course.json
├── invalid-course.json
└── src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/
    ├── Main.java
    ├── HelloResource.java
    ├── Course.java
    ├── NewCourse.java
    ├── ApiError.java
    ├── CourseStore.java
    └── CourseResource.java

src/main/java is Maven’s normal source root. Java files stay in that tree. The build creates target for output; edit sources, not generated files in target.

Step 3 — understand the build recipe, then build

session2-jaxrs/pom.xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<project xmlns="http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0"
         xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"
         xsi:schemaLocation="http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0 https://maven.apache.org/xsd/maven-4.0.0.xsd">
    <modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>
    <groupId>com.aptech</groupId>
    <artifactId>session2-jaxrs</artifactId>
    <version>1.0</version>

    <properties>
        <maven.compiler.release>17</maven.compiler.release>
        <project.build.sourceEncoding>UTF-8</project.build.sourceEncoding>
        <jersey.version>3.1.5</jersey.version>
    </properties>

    <dependencies>
        <dependency>
            <groupId>org.glassfish.jersey.containers</groupId>
            <artifactId>jersey-container-jdk-http</artifactId>
            <version>${jersey.version}</version>
        </dependency>
        <dependency>
            <groupId>org.glassfish.jersey.inject</groupId>
            <artifactId>jersey-hk2</artifactId>
            <version>${jersey.version}</version>
        </dependency>
        <dependency>
            <groupId>org.glassfish.jersey.media</groupId>
            <artifactId>jersey-media-json-jackson</artifactId>
            <version>${jersey.version}</version>
        </dependency>
    </dependencies>

    <build>
        <plugins>
            <plugin>
                <groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>
                <artifactId>maven-compiler-plugin</artifactId>
                <version>3.13.0</version>
            </plugin>
            <plugin>
                <groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>
                <artifactId>maven-shade-plugin</artifactId>
                <version>3.6.0</version>
                <executions>
                    <execution>
                        <phase>package</phase>
                        <goals><goal>shade</goal></goals>
                        <configuration>
                            <createDependencyReducedPom>false</createDependencyReducedPom>
                            <transformers>
                                <transformer implementation="org.apache.maven.plugins.shade.resource.ServicesResourceTransformer"/>
                                <transformer implementation="org.apache.maven.plugins.shade.resource.ManifestResourceTransformer">
                                    <mainClass>com.aptech.session2.Main</mainClass>
                                </transformer>
                            </transformers>
                        </configuration>
                    </execution>
                </executions>
            </plugin>
        </plugins>
    </build>
</project>
Part of pom.xmlWhy it exists
groupId / artifactId / versionIdentify this project. The artifactId and version determine the normal output filename: session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar.
maven.compiler.release = 17Compile against Java 17’s language/API level, even when the installed JDK is newer.
jersey.version = 3.1.5One shared, pinned Jersey version for all three library entries; chosen to match the family already used in this course project.
jersey-container-jdk-httpConnect Jersey to the JDK HTTP server. It brings related Jakarta REST and Jersey components transitively.
jersey-hk2Provide Jersey’s injection support. You use it through the runtime; you do not write its engine.
jersey-media-json-jacksonAdd JSON conversion; Main registers the Jackson feature.
maven-compiler-pluginCompile the packaged Java sources.
maven-shade-pluginPackage our classes and library classes into a runnable JAR. The services transformer combines library discovery files; the manifest transformer records the Main entry class.

This XML file is a Maven recipe, not Java code. You can inspect it, but you do not need to recreate all of it from memory to learn resource methods. On the first build, Maven needs internet access to download libraries and build plugins. It caches downloads; later builds can reuse them, though a complete cache is needed for offline builds.

mvn package

Wait for BUILD SUCCESS. The output should include target/session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar, containing the app and dependencies. Downloads and overlap warnings may appear; the final build result matters. If the build fails, read the first error and use 2.12 rather than starting an old JAR.

2.4 Guided lab: one greeting resource

Goal: understand one JAX-RS method before using the course API. First read the complete resource:

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/HelloResource.java
package com.aptech.session2;

import jakarta.ws.rs.DefaultValue;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;

@Path("/greeting")
public class HelloResource {
    @GET
    @Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)
    public String greet(@DefaultValue("Student") @QueryParam("name") String name) {
        if (name.isBlank()) {
            name = "Student";
        }
        return "Hello, " + name.trim() + "!";
    }
}
CodeMeaning
@Path("/greeting")This class handles paths beginning at /greeting relative to the application’s /api/ base.
@GETSelect this method for an HTTP GET to the class path.
@Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)The reply is plain text. MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN represents the standard name text/plain.
@QueryParam("name") String nameRead the query parameter called name and supply its value as a String method argument.
@DefaultValue("Student")Use Student when name is omitted. It does not reject an explicitly blank value; our if block handles that.
if (name.isBlank())For an empty/whitespace-only provided name, choose the fallback Student.
return "Hello, " + name.trim() + "!"Return the greeting to Jersey. Jersey writes it as the response body with status 200; it is not just printed in the server terminal.

Read Main: the part that starts the service

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/Main.java
package com.aptech.session2;

import com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer;
import java.net.URI;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jackson.JacksonFeature;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jdkhttp.JdkHttpServerFactory;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ResourceConfig;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ServerProperties;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int port = Integer.getInteger("course.port", 8093);
        String baseUrl = "http://127.0.0.1:" + port + "/api/";
        boolean helloOnly = args.length > 0 && "--hello-only".equals(args[0]);

        ResourceConfig config = new ResourceConfig();
        config.property(ServerProperties.WADL_FEATURE_DISABLE, true);
        config.register(HelloResource.class);
        config.register(JacksonFeature.class);
        if (!helloOnly) {
            config.register(CourseResource.class);
        }

        HttpServer server = JdkHttpServerFactory.createHttpServer(
            URI.create(baseUrl), config);
        Runtime.getRuntime().addShutdownHook(new Thread(() -> server.stop(0)));

        System.out.println("Session 2 JAX-RS service is running.");
        System.out.println("Try: " + baseUrl + "greeting");
        if (!helloOnly) {
            System.out.println("Try: " + baseUrl + "courses");
        }
        System.out.println("Press Ctrl+C in this terminal to stop.");
    }
}

ResourceConfig holds the classes/features Jersey uses. The property line disables optional WADL XML-description generation, which this JSON/text lab does not use. register(HelloResource.class) supplies the class itself; .class is Java’s class-literal syntax, not a request to open a .class file. register(JacksonFeature.class) enables JSON support for the course endpoints. The --hello-only flag skips registering CourseResource so you can practise the first resource in isolation.

Integer.getInteger("course.port", 8093) reads an optional Java system property or uses port 8093. URI.create(baseUrl) builds a URI object from the address. Jersey’s JDK factory creates and starts the HTTP server with that configuration. The shutdown hook supplies a small lambda to stop the server when the JVM shuts down, such as after Ctrl+C. The printed Try address is an instruction in the terminal; the resource’s return value is what reaches the client.

Step 1 — run greeting-only mode in terminal A

java --add-modules jdk.httpserver -jar target/session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar --hello-only
Session 2 JAX-RS service is running.
Try: http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/greeting
Press Ctrl+C in this terminal to stop.

Library logging may appear before these lines. Keep terminal A running. The server waits for requests; its command prompt does not return yet.

Step 2 — request it from your browser or terminal B

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/greeting

Expected: status 200, a plain-text content type, and body Hello, Student!. A browser address-bar visit also works for this GET. Header order/capitalisation may differ. Use curl.exe explicitly on Windows to avoid the PowerShell curl alias.

curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/greeting?name=Amina"

Expected: 200 and Hello, Amina!. The name value goes into the Java parameter; the method does not require a new Java file for each student.

WE DO → YOU CHANGE

Change the returned text so the body begins Welcome, . Predict both the default and named replies. Save; press Ctrl+C in A; run mvn package; start the same greeting-only command again; repeat both requests. Rebuilding a JAR does not update a process already running.

YOU DO • FIRST TRANSFER

In HelloResource, add a second method handling GET at method path /centre. It must produce plain text with your centre name. The complete URL should be /api/greeting/centre. Write the annotations and method yourself, build/restart, and test the new and old greeting URLs.

Hint: combine the two path levels

Keep the existing class path /greeting. Place @GET, @Path("/centre") and a text/plain @Produces on the new method inside that class. The method name can be centre; Jersey selects by the annotations.

2.5 Paths, parameters, headers and bodies

A request has several places for information. Keeping them separate prevents many first-time errors.

GET http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses/1
    host + port              base  class   method path

GET http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses?search=java
                                   query key=value
PartExampleJAX-RS connection
Application basehttp://127.0.0.1:8093/api/Main configures this base; /api is not supplied by @Path automatically.
Class path/courses@Path("/courses") on CourseResource.
Method path/{id}, filled with 1@Path("/{id}") on a method; braces mark a path placeholder.
Path parameter1 in /courses/1@PathParam("id") int id receives it and requests integer conversion.
Query parametersearch=java after ?@QueryParam("search") String search receives optional filter text.
Request headerAccept: application/jsonThe client states what response format it can accept.
Request headerContent-Type: application/jsonThe client states the format of the body it is sending.
Request body{"title":"REST Practice"}JSON data submitted to POST; Jackson converts it to NewCourse.

A path parameter commonly selects one resource; a query commonly filters or changes how a collection is read. /courses/1 and /courses?search=java are different requests. The query parameter name must match the annotation string. ?title=java is not a search in this API and is simply ignored because no parameter reads it.

For a query with spaces, encode the value: ?name=Amina%20Bello. Quotes around the entire URL keep shell characters together, but the URL must still encode spaces. Jersey decodes the value before giving it to the method. Query strings are not where we put the course-creation JSON body.

The request method participates in selection. GET /courses reads; POST /courses creates. These can share a path because their methods differ. @Produces describes available response formats; @Consumes describes accepted request-body formats. Sending JSON does not force every endpoint to return JSON.

PREDICT BEFORE RUNNING

Work out the full URL for class path /greeting and method path /centre under /api/. Decide whether the 1 in /courses/1 is a query or path parameter. Explain why Accept: text/plain may fail for a course method that produces only application/json.

2.6 Java objects, JSON and temporary storage

A representation is how a resource’s data is expressed in a response. Our Course object becomes JSON through Jackson. You do not build the course response by joining quoted strings by hand.

One Course object as JSON:
{"id":1,"title":"Java Foundations"}

A List<Course> as JSON:
[{"id":1,"title":"Java Foundations"},
 {"id":2,"title":"Creating Services for the Web"}]

Braces surround one JSON object; property names are quoted. id is a number; title is text. Square brackets surround an array. Property order and whitespace are not part of the meaning. An empty list is [], a successful empty collection, not necessarily a 404.

Use a separate input class

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/NewCourse.java
package com.aptech.session2;

public class NewCourse {
    public String title;
}

The create request supplies only a title. Jackson can call the implicit no-argument constructor and assign this public field from a JSON property called title. The server chooses the id; the client does not choose it. A data-transfer class like this is not automatically a JPA entity or a saved database row.

Use an object for our readable error bodies

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/ApiError.java
package com.aptech.session2;

public class ApiError {
    public String error;

    public ApiError(String error) {
        this.error = error;
    }
}

new ApiError("Course not found.") becomes {"error":"Course not found."} in our explicit error responses. The constructor stores the supplied message. Framework-generated errors, such as an unmatched route or malformed JSON, may have a different or empty body; assert their status rather than assuming this class handles every error.

Read the temporary store

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/CourseStore.java
package com.aptech.session2;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.LinkedHashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Locale;
import java.util.Map;

public class CourseStore {
    private final Map<Integer, Course> courses = new LinkedHashMap<>();
    private int nextId = 1;

    public CourseStore() {
        add("Java Foundations");
        add("Creating Services for the Web");
    }

    public synchronized List<Course> list(String search) {
        List<Course> result = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Course course : courses.values()) {
            if (search == null || course.title.toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT)
                    .contains(search.trim().toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT))) {
                result.add(course);
            }
        }
        return result;
    }

    public synchronized Course find(int id) {
        return courses.get(id);
    }

    public synchronized Course add(String title) {
        Course course = new Course(nextId, title);
        courses.put(nextId, course);
        nextId++;
        return course;
    }
}
PartWhat it does
private final Map<Integer, Course> coursesKeeps id → Course entries. private hides direct access outside the class; final prevents replacing this map reference, not adding entries to it.
nextId starts at 1; constructor calls add twiceEvery fresh server starts with two sample courses, ids 1 and 2. The next successful add will use id 3.
list(search)Create a result list. If search is omitted (null), include every course. Otherwise include titles containing the trimmed search text, ignoring case with Locale.ROOT.
courses.values() and result.add(course)Loop through the stored objects and collect matches. An unmatched search yields an empty list.
find(id)Ask the map for that key. Return null when the course does not exist.
add(title)Construct the Course, put it under nextId, increment nextId with ++, and return the created object.
synchronized methodsUse a simple Java lock so store operations do not run simultaneously on the same object. This avoids duplicate ids when two requests arrive together. You can use these helper methods without implementing threading yourself.
What survives a restart?

This is an in-memory map. New courses remain available during this server run, but disappear after it stops; the two samples are recreated on startup. It is not a database, a file, or JPA persistence. Stateless HTTP requests can still use stored resource data; stateless does not mean every request must start with an empty map.

WE DO → YOU PREDICT

Assume a fresh run. Write the result of find(1), find(999), list("java") and list("zzzz"). Predict the id of the first valid add. Later, verify all four read cases through HTTP and check the returned id when you create a course.

2.7 Guided lab: list, filter and find courses

First stop greeting-only mode with Ctrl+C. Build successfully if you changed source, then start full mode in terminal A:

mvn package
java --add-modules jdk.httpserver -jar target/session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar

This time Main registers CourseResource as well as HelloResource. The greeting remains available. Read the complete resource once; today we first follow the GET methods, then study POST in 2.8.

session2-jaxrs/src/main/java/com/aptech/session2/CourseResource.java
package com.aptech.session2;

import java.util.List;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Consumes;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.POST;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.PathParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Context;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.UriInfo;

@Path("/courses")
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class CourseResource {
    private static final CourseStore STORE = new CourseStore();

    @GET
    public List<Course> list(@QueryParam("search") String search) {
        return STORE.list(search);
    }

    @GET
    @Path("/{id}")
    public Response get(@PathParam("id") int id) {
        if (id < 1) {
            return Response.status(400)
                .entity(new ApiError("Course id must be positive.")).build();
        }
        Course course = STORE.find(id);
        if (course == null) {
            return Response.status(404)
                .entity(new ApiError("Course not found.")).build();
        }
        return Response.ok(course).build();
    }

    @POST
    @Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
    public Response create(NewCourse input, @Context UriInfo uriInfo) {
        if (input == null || input.title == null || input.title.isBlank()) {
            return Response.status(400)
                .entity(new ApiError("A non-blank title is required.")).build();
        }
        String title = input.title.trim();
        if (title.length() > 80) {
            return Response.status(400)
                .entity(new ApiError("Title must be at most 80 characters.")).build();
        }
        Course saved = STORE.add(title);
        return Response.created(uriInfo.getAbsolutePathBuilder()
            .path(String.valueOf(saved.id)).build()).entity(saved).build();
    }
}

Follow the GET methods

The class-level @Path("/courses") and @Produces(APPLICATION_JSON) apply to its resource methods. The private static final CourseStore STORE is one store reference shared across instances of this class in the running JVM. Jersey commonly creates a resource instance for each request; a new non-static store inside each instance would lose changes from earlier calls. This simple static store is for the local exercise, not a substitute for a production database.

The first @GET has no extra path, so it handles /api/courses. It returns a List and Jersey sends status 200 with its JSON representation. The second @GET adds /{id}. Jersey extracts and converts id, then our method rejects a number below 1 with 400, returns 404 for a missing positive id, or returns 200 with the found object.

Response lets the method describe a status, body and headers instead of returning only data. Response.ok(course).build() constructs a 200 response. Response.status(404).entity(new ApiError(...)).build() supplies a status and error object. The chained calls configure a builder; build() produces the final response.

Run these reads in terminal B

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses

Fresh-run expectation: 200 with the two sample courses. Use the browser for this GET if you prefer, then curl to inspect the status.

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses/1

Expected: 200 with {"id":1,"title":"Java Foundations"}.

curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses?search=java"

Expected: 200 and a one-item array containing Java Foundations on a fresh run.

Request suffix after http://127.0.0.1:8093Expected statusExpected body / meaning
/api/courses?search=JAVA200Same case-insensitive match as java.
/api/courses?search=zzzz200[] when no title matches. The collection exists.
/api/courses/999404Our JSON error: Course not found.
/api/courses/0400Our JSON error: Course id must be positive.
/api/courses/abc404Path parameter cannot convert to int; Jersey rejects it before our get method. Framework body may vary.
/api/missing404No registered matching resource path; framework body may vary.

Run each suffix as a full curl URL and record the actual status/body. Distinguish “there is no such course” from “the collection exists but this search has no matches.” The Java method checks numeric ids, but Jersey performs the text-to-int conversion first.

WE DO → YOU CHANGE

Change the constructor’s first seeded title in CourseStore to a new title containing the word Java. Build/restart and verify /courses/1, the list, and search=java still agree. A change in one source line affects multiple representations of the same object.

YOU DO • NEW READ METHOD

In CourseResource, add GET at method path /count. Return a JSON object with one property count giving the current number of courses. Write the route and method yourself, build/restart, and test /api/courses/count as well as /api/courses/1. On a fresh server count should be 2.

Small hint for the count method

Use @GET and @Path("/count") inside CourseResource. The class already produces JSON. STORE.list(null).size() gives the number of all courses. You may return java.util.Map.of("count", value), which creates a small map Jackson can represent as JSON. The specific static path /count takes precedence over the variable /{id} pattern.

2.8 Guided lab: send JSON and create a course

Goal: trace the difference between reading data and submitting a new resource. GET does not create a course in this API; POST /api/courses does. A browser address-bar visit sends GET and cannot replace the POST command below.

Read the create method in CourseResource

  1. @POST selects POST on the class path /courses. @Consumes(APPLICATION_JSON) accepts a JSON request body.
  2. The unannotated NewCourse input parameter receives the deserialised body. Jackson matches the JSON title property to its public title field.
  3. @Context UriInfo uriInfo asks the runtime to supply request-URI information. This is a different kind of input from a query or body field.
  4. The null/blank checks reject missing usable titles with 400 and an ApiError. The next check rejects a trimmed title longer than 80 with another 400. These returns occur before calling the store, so rejected requests do not consume an id or add a record.
  5. STORE.add(title) creates and keeps the new object in memory. The constructor picks the next id; the input class has no id field.
  6. getAbsolutePathBuilder() starts with the POST address /api/courses. path(String.valueOf(saved.id)) adds the new id as text. Response.created uses this URI for a Location header and status 201 Created; entity(saved) supplies the new JSON object.

Serialise means convert an object to a representation, such as JSON. Deserialise means convert that representation into an object. These are data conversions, not database commits. @Consumes does not validate a title’s meaning; your checks do that.

Step 1 — inspect the request file

session2-jaxrs/new-course.json
{"title":"REST Practice"}

This file contains only the request body. Save it as UTF-8 plain text. It is not a Java source file or a whole HTTP request. We use a file body to avoid fragile nested quotation marks in Windows shell commands.

Step 2 — enter the project folder in terminal B

Set-Location "C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service\session2-jaxrs"

Keep terminal A running in full mode. In B, send the file:

curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@new-course.json"
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
Location: http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses/3
Content-type: application/json
...other headers...

{"id":3,"title":"REST Practice"}

This is a shortened fresh-run example. The id is 3 only if no earlier successful POST has occurred in this run. Header order/capitalisation and JSON property order may differ. Always read the actual returned id and Location. -X POST chooses the method, -H sends a header, --data-binary sends file bytes, and the @ before the filename tells curl to read that file.

Step 3 — prove the created resource is readable

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses/3

Use the actual id from your response. Expect 200 with the created course. GET /courses should now include it. If you wrote /count, verify its count increased. A 201 response alone is not your entire test: read the resulting resource.

Step 4 — test rejected input

session2-jaxrs/invalid-course.json
{"title":"   "}
curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@invalid-course.json"

Expected: 400 and {"error":"A non-blank title is required."}. Verify the course count/list did not change. Then test a file containing {} and a file containing {"title":null}; both should fail with the same validation message.

Step 5 — distinguish routing/format failures

curl.exe -i -X PUT http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses/1

Expected: 405 because no PUT method is defined there. The framework may also support HEAD/OPTIONS for GET routes; “our code defines GET and POST” does not mean all other HTTP methods behave identically.

curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses -H "Content-Type: text/plain" --data-binary "@new-course.json"

Expected: 415 Unsupported Media Type. The body’s actual text looks like JSON, but the header declares text/plain and the method consumes application/json.

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8093/api/courses -H "Accept: text/plain"

Expected: 406 Not Acceptable because the collection produces JSON and the client requested only plain text. These negotiation errors happen in Jersey before your application method returns a normal body.

WE DO → YOU CHANGE

Create your own request file containing a different title with spaces at both ends. POST it, then read its Location. The stored title should be trimmed. POST the same valid file again and observe that another id is created: this sample POST does not prevent duplicates and is not idempotent.

YOU DO • PROVE THE LIMIT

Make one file with a trimmed title exactly 80 characters long and another with 81. Predict and verify 201 versus 400. Record the list/count before and after. Finally restart the server and check that added courses disappeared while the original sample rows returned.

Optional boundary-file helper, after predicting the result
Set-Content -LiteralPath 'title-80.json' -Value ('{"title":"' + ('A' * 80) + '"}') -Encoding ascii
Set-Content -LiteralPath 'title-81.json' -Value ('{"title":"' + ('A' * 81) + '"}') -Encoding ascii

PowerShell’s 'A' * 80 repeats A 80 times. These commands create ASCII-only test files. Use the same POST command, replacing the filename. For malformed-JSON testing, create a separate file with a deliberately missing closing brace and verify 400; its framework-generated body may vary.

2.9 JAX-RS versus JAX-WS, without mixing them up

Both APIs help Java applications expose services, but they organise communication differently. JAX-WS is the familiar acronym associated with Jakarta XML Web Services, commonly used for SOAP services. It is the topic of the next teaching session. JPA from Session 1 serves a different purpose: persistence.

QuestionJAX-RS / Jakarta RESTJAX-WS / Jakarta XML Web Services
How is the interface organised?Resources and HTTP method/path combinations, such as GET /courses/1.Service operations, such as getCourse, communicated through SOAP messages.
Typical data representationsJSON, XML, text and other media types supported by providers.SOAP XML envelopes; Java/XML binding handles supported data types.
Representative annotations@Path, @GET, @POST, @Produces, @Consumes.@WebService, @WebMethod and related SOAP service annotations.
How can the contract be described?Often OpenAPI or documentation of methods, paths, schemas and statuses. OpenAPI is not a JAX-RS requirement.Often WSDL defining operations/messages and bindings.
Example implementationJersey or RESTEasy.Metro or Apache CXF with suitable JAX-WS support.
Possible usesResource APIs used by portals, mobile apps and other services.Systems requiring SOAP contracts or compatible WS-* infrastructure.
Included as a complete working stack in a modern plain JDK?No; add a runtime/libraries or use a suitable application server.No; modern JDKs need external runtime/libraries or an appropriate server.

Choose based on system requirements, compatibility and the agreed interface, not claims that one is always faster or safer. SOAP is a protocol; REST is an architectural style. Neither removes the need for authentication, validation or careful error handling. A REST service can use XML; an XML response alone is not evidence that the service uses SOAP.

YOU EXPLAIN • SCENARIOS

A mobile course app needs a resource API. An existing partner requires a SOAP operation described in WSDL. Choose the likely Java API for each and explain your reasoning. Then identify the API used if the task is “save Student objects to a database.” Use JAX-RS, JAX-WS and JPA accurately.

2.11 Independent practice: build without a finished answer

Keep your reference project as a guide to library syntax, but write your new annotations, branches and explanations yourself. For each task, predict the URL and response before building. Check old routes after adding a method.

TaskYour requirementAcceptance check
P1 — own plain-text resourceCreate HelpResource.java in the same package. Class path /help. GET at its class path returns a help message; GET at /hours returns opening hours. Register it in Main.GET /api/help and /api/help/hours return 200 and different text bodies. Greeting still works.

Hints, without a completed submission

2.12 Find the problem at the right layer

Start by deciding whether the failure is in the build, startup, HTTP routing, body conversion or your application logic. A 404 proves you received an HTTP response; it is not the same as a refused connection.

What you seeLikely layer / causeWhat to do
mvn is not recognisedMaven not on PATH or terminal opened before PATH changed.Follow 2.3, reopen PowerShell and verify mvn -version.
No POM / MissingProjectExceptionBuild command run in the wrong folder.Enter session2-jaxrs; Get-ChildItem must show pom.xml.
package jakarta.ws.rs does not existManual javac invocation lacks dependencies, or Maven failed to resolve them.Build the project with mvn package and inspect dependency/download errors.
Dependency/plugin could not be resolvedNetwork, proxy, repository access or incomplete cache.Check internet/repository access; retry Maven after fixing it. Offline builds require all plugins/dependencies already cached.
release version 17 not supportedMaven is using an older JDK.Check the Java version inside mvn -version and correct JAVA_HOME to a JDK 17+.
Unable to access jarfileWrong folder, unsuccessful build, or filename typo.Find target/session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar and confirm BUILD SUCCESS.
Address already in use / BindExceptionAnother server owns port 8093.Stop your own previous instance with Ctrl+C. For a different application, use java "-Dcourse.port=8094" --add-modules jdk.httpserver -jar target/session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar and use 8094 in every URL.
Connection refusedNo server is listening at the requested host/port.Inspect terminal A for startup errors, keep it running and use its printed address.
404 on /coursesThe application base /api was omitted.Use /api/courses. Base, class path and method path all contribute.
404 on /api/courses in greeting-only modeMain deliberately did not register CourseResource.Stop and restart without --hello-only for full mode.
404 for /api/courses/999 versus /abc999 reaches our missing-record check; abc fails integer conversion earlier.Read status and any body; explain these different causes.
400 for a course POSTBad/malformed JSON or missing/blank/overlong title.Inspect Content-Type and the file. Match field name title; check the validation response. Framework bodies may differ.
405 / 406 / 415Unsupported method / response format / request-body format.Check method annotations, Accept/@Produces and Content-Type/@Consumes respectively.
curl cannot read the fileTerminal B is in the wrong folder or filename is wrong.Enter the project root and use --data-binary "@new-course.json" with the actual file.
Old behaviour after an editThe existing process still uses its old JAR.Save → stop → mvn package successfully → start → request again.
Created course disappeared after restartExpected behaviour of the in-memory store.Use a fresh-run test log and explain why this lab is not database persistence.

Do not mix javax.ws.rs and jakarta.ws.rs imports from different tutorials. Use the dependency/import family in this project. Do not stop an unrelated application to free a port.

2.13 Assignment: a library book API

Scenario: your centre needs a small local API for library books. In a copy of the project, build your own book resource. You may reuse the Maven/server/library setup, but write the data classes, resource methods and store decisions yourself without AI or a pasted finished solution.

Build, register and start your assignment

Use your personal copy of session2-jaxrs. Put new Java classes under src/main/java/com/aptech/session2 and use the matching package. Write your BookResource class and supporting data/store classes first. In Main.java, inside the if (!helloOnly) block, add config.register(BookResource.class);. Registration tells Jersey to discover your resource annotations; creating the file alone does not register it.

Terminal A must be in that copy’s project folder, containing pom.xml. Stop an older server with Ctrl+C before replacing its JAR. Save your source, then run:

mvn package
java "-Dcourse.port=8094" --add-modules jdk.httpserver -jar target/session2-jaxrs-1.0.jar

Only start after BUILD SUCCESS. Do not append --hello-only for this assignment, because it would skip the full-resource registration block. The quoted -Dcourse.port=8094 is a Java startup property; PowerShell needs the quotes around this dotted argument. It selects port 8094, not a book id. Confirm A’s printed base is http://127.0.0.1:8094/api/.

In terminal B, begin with a read:

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8094/api/books

After you have implemented and seeded the collection, expect 200 and an array containing your two books. If it is 404, check the resource’s @Path, registration and full-mode startup before changing the store.

For creation tests, save a request file such as new-book.json containing {"title":"Java Basics","author":"A. Writer"}. In B, enter your copy’s project folder so curl can read that file:

curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8094/api/books -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@new-book.json"

After your POST method is implemented, expect 201; read the actual returned Location and GET that address. This request example is test data, not a completed assignment implementation. Write the methods, validation and storage decisions yourself, then use the full test requirements below.

  1. Use class path /books under the same /api/ application base. Register the new class in Main. Run on port 8094 using the supplied system-property option; confirm the printed address.
  2. A Book has id, title and author. Seed two books. GET /api/books returns their JSON collection.
  3. GET /api/books/{id} returns one book with 200, a clear JSON 404 for an unknown positive id, and 400 for id below 1.
  4. GET /api/books?search=... filters titles case-insensitively. No matches means 200 with []. Omitted search returns all books.
  5. POST /api/books accepts JSON title and author. Both must be non-blank after trimming; each has a maximum of 60. The server generates the id. Rejected data returns 400 and does not change the store.
  6. A valid creation returns 201, the created JSON object, and a Location pointing to the readable book resource.
  7. Add GET /api/books/count with a JSON count property. It must agree with the full collection after a creation or failed request.
  8. Keep storage in memory for this assignment. Stop/restart and explain the reset. Do not claim database persistence.

Your submission must include

  • All new/changed Java sources and your exact build/run commands. Include the updated registration, not only one method pasted into a document.
  • A test log with method, URL, request headers/body when relevant, expected status/body and actual observations. Cover collection, lookup, search match/no-match, nonpositive/missing/non-integer id, valid POST, missing/blank/overlong title and author, 405, 406, 415, count consistency and restart.
  • The returned Location from a successful POST and proof that GET at that address returns the new book. Use observed ids, not a guessed constant.
  • An explanation of @Path, @PathParam, @QueryParam, @Consumes and @Produces; where JSON conversion happens; and why the input and returned data classes can differ.
  • A short comparison of JAX-RS, JAX-WS and JPA, plus one mistake you diagnosed and repaired.
How to record a test without inventing the result

Before running, write “POST /api/books, Content-Type application/json, input file with blank author; expected 400 and no count change.” After running, add the actual status/body and the count you read. Record whether they match. A screenshot is helpful evidence, but it should not replace the commands and explanation.

2.13B Connect your REST knowledge to stored data

Prerequisites: finish the console database lab and Session 2's resource methods, JSON, validation and 201/Location work. Now send a real HTTP request whose data survives stopping the server. Use the supplied session1-jpa-lab project, which contains the same store plus a small Jersey bridge.

Step 1 — trace the code before starting

session1-jpa-lab/src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/StudentResource.java
package com.aptech.persistence;

import java.util.List;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Consumes;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.POST;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.PathParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Context;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.UriInfo;

@Path("/students")
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class StudentResource {
    private final StudentStore store;

    public StudentResource(StudentStore store) {
        this.store = store;
    }

    @GET
    public List<Student> list(@QueryParam("name") String name) {
        return name == null ? store.list() : store.search(name);
    }

    @GET
    @Path("/{id}")
    public Response get(@PathParam("id") long id) {
        if (id < 1) return Response.status(400).entity(new Error("Id must be positive.")).build();
        Student student = store.find(id);
        if (student == null) return Response.status(404).entity(new Error("Student not found.")).build();
        return Response.ok(student).build();
    }

    @POST
    @Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
    public Response create(Input input, @Context UriInfo uriInfo) {
        try {
            Student saved = store.add(input == null ? null : input.name);
            return Response.created(uriInfo.getAbsolutePathBuilder()
                .path(String.valueOf(saved.getId())).build()).entity(saved).build();
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException problem) {
            return Response.status(400).entity(new Error(problem.getMessage())).build();
        }
    }

    public static class Input {
        public String name;
    }

    public static class Error {
        public String message;

        public Error(String message) {
            this.message = message;
        }
    }
}

The nested Input class holds the JSON name. POST calls store.add, which validates and commits before returning. The resource then returns 201 and the new student's Location. IllegalArgumentException becomes a 400 response with a message; unexpected database failures are different server failures. GET uses a new EntityManager through the store. These simple entities have only id/name, so their getters provide the JSON fields after the context closes; more complex entity relationships need a deliberate response model.

GET without a name query lists all students; with a name it performs an exact, case-sensitive search. name == null ? first : second is the conditional expression from 2.2. It selects one call; it does not execute both.

session1-jpa-lab/src/main/java/com/aptech/persistence/PersistentServer.java
package com.aptech.persistence;

import com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer;
import java.net.URI;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;
import jakarta.persistence.EntityManagerFactory;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jackson.JacksonFeature;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jdkhttp.JdkHttpServerFactory;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ResourceConfig;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ServerProperties;

public class PersistentServer {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        EntityManagerFactory factory = Database.open();
        HttpServer server = null;
        ExecutorService requests = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(4);
        try {
            StudentStore store = new StudentStore(factory);
            ResourceConfig config = new ResourceConfig();
            config.property(ServerProperties.WADL_FEATURE_DISABLE, true);
            config.register(new StudentResource(store));
            config.register(JacksonFeature.class);
            int port = Integer.getInteger("lab.port", 8096);
            String base = "http://127.0.0.1:" + port + "/api/";
            server = JdkHttpServerFactory.createHttpServer(URI.create(base), config, false);
            server.setExecutor(requests);
            server.start();
            System.out.println("Persistent service: " + base + "students");
            System.out.println("Leave this terminal open. Press Enter here to stop safely.");
            System.in.read();
        } finally {
            if (server != null) server.stop(0);
            requests.shutdownNow();
            factory.close();
        }
    }
}

new StudentStore(factory) supplies the already-open factory. new StudentResource(store) supplies that store to the resource's constructor; config.register registers this ready-made instance with Jersey. The factory can be shared, but each request opens its own EntityManager through the store. Do not share one EntityManager across concurrent requests.

createHttpServer(..., false) creates the server without starting it, so we can choose its worker threads first. An ExecutorService runs request work; newFixedThreadPool(4) allows up to four workers. After setExecutor, server.start begins listening. On exit, shutdownNow stops these workers so the program can return to the prompt. System.in.read() waits for keyboard input in terminal A. Press Enter there to finish the program; finally stops the server and closes the factory. This lab's stop instruction differs from the earlier services. Stop before rebuilding its JAR or running a console command against the same H2 file.

Step 2 — build and start in terminal A

Set-Location "C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service\session1-jpa-lab"

java --add-modules jdk.httpserver -cp target/session1-jpa-lab-1.0.jar com.aptech.persistence.PersistentServer

Step 4 — prove persistence across a server restart

  1. Record the POST id and Location.
  2. In A press Enter to stop safely. Wait until the prompt returns.
  3. Run the same server-start command again from the same folder. The database path must stay the same.
  4. In B GET the recorded Location again; it must return 200 with the same id/name.
  5. GET the collection. Your new row remains alongside earlier rows. The server did not silently reseed its store.

Explain the difference from Session 2's CourseStore: that Map lived only in the process; this store commits to an H2 file. An HTTP 201 alone did not prove restart persistence; the later read does.

Step 5 — prove a rejected request does not save data

session1-jpa-lab/blank-student.json
{"name":"   "}
curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8096/api/students -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@blank-student.json"

Expect 400 and the name-validation message. Compare GET /students before and after: the rows must be unchanged. Repeat with a 61-character name. A JSON null body must also return 400. A non-JSON media type is a 415; malformed JSON is a 400. Record actual status as well as body.

curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8096/api/students/999999
curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8096/api/students/0
curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8096/api/students?name=Amina"

Expected: 404 for an absent positive id, 400 for zero, 200 with only exact Amina matches. Choose an absent id based on your own list. Two Amina rows are allowed: a name is not a unique primary key.

3.1 What JAX-WS does for you

JAX-WS is the familiar abbreviation for Java API for XML Web Services; its Jakarta successor is Jakarta XML Web Services. It defines Java APIs and rules for exposing and calling services, commonly using SOAP, a protocol with XML message rules. An API describes ways your code can request work. An implementation supplies the working code behind those rules. This project uses Metro 4.0.3 as that implementation.

Imagine the course office: a caller asks “What will two ADSE semesters cost?” The office advertises the task name, required inputs and result. Your Java method does the calculation. Metro receives the SOAP message, converts the inputs into Java values, calls your method and converts the returned value into a SOAP response. You write the business rule; the runtime handles the message machinery.

PartIts job in this project
Java service methodcalculateCourseFees("ADSE", 2) applies your fee rules.
AnnotationsLabels such as @WebService describe how Metro exposes Java code.
Metro runtimePublishes the endpoint, reads/writes SOAP and supplies client support.
SOAP messageCarries the operation, arguments, result or structured error.
WSDLA machine-readable document describing the available operations, message shapes and address.
ClientA program that sends a request and reads the result.
Recall from Session 2Today
JAX-RS: resources and HTTP routes, often Jersey.JAX-WS: service operations and SOAP contracts, here Metro.
GET /courses/1 with a JSON or XML representation.POST /greeting with a SOAP XML operation in the Body.
Annotations include @Path, @GET and @POST.Annotations include @WebService, @WebMethod and @WebParam.

A response containing XML is not automatically SOAP: it must follow SOAP’s envelope rules. JPA stores data; JAX-WS communicates with services. Our examples calculate values in memory and do not use a database. SOAP often fits an integration whose partner already requires WSDL or SOAP standards. The required contract and system compatibility guide the choice of API.

3.2 Java ideas you need before the service

Keep three things separate: an interface says which methods are available; a class implementing it writes their bodies; an object is a particular instance created from that class. A method body is the instructions between { and }. An interface method declaration can end with ; because its implementation will supply the body.

A small Java-only example to read
interface Greeter {
    String greet(String name);
}

class FriendlyGreeter implements Greeter {
    @Override
    public String greet(String name) {
        return "Hello, " + name + "!";
    }
}
Read thisMeaning
String greet(String name);The result is text. The method is greet. It accepts one text input stored as name.
implements GreeterThe class promises to supply the methods declared by Greeter.
@OverrideAsk the compiler to check that this method matches one required/inherited method. It does not publish a web service.
publicOther suitable code may call the method. Interface methods here are implicitly public, so the implementation must be public.
returnSend a value back to the caller; it does not print text by itself.
new FriendlyGreeter()Create an object. The parentheses call its constructor, the setup routine for a new object.
YOU TYPE • JAVA RECAP

Create GreeterDemo.java in your own practice folder. Put the following public class below the two types shown above. Save all three types in that one file; only GreeterDemo is public. Type the file, compile it, then run it.

Add this below Greeter and FriendlyGreeter
public class GreeterDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Greeter greeter = new FriendlyGreeter();
        System.out.println(greeter.greet("Amina"));
    }
}
javac GreeterDemo.java
java GreeterDemo

Expected: Hello, Amina!. Greeter is the variable’s type, greeter its name; it refers to the new object. Change Amina to your name and predict the output first. No web server is involved in this recap.

Package and import: package com.aptech.session3.beginner; groups these service types under a name. Maven expects the matching folder below src/main/java. An import lets you use a type’s short name. Annotation: a label starting with @ supplies metadata, information about your code. The compiler checks Java syntax; Metro reads web-service annotations to build the contract.

Read the punctuation without memorising everything

A dot joins names or selects a member: greeter.greet. Parentheses carry method inputs; quotation marks surround a String literal; + joins text when a String is involved; a comma separates inputs. Braces group instructions; a semicolon ends a statement. Capitalisation matters: String and string are different names. The main method starts a runnable program; service methods are called by Metro when a message arrives.

YOU EXPLAIN • NO CODE OPEN

Explain why an interface has no greeting body and why changing the method name in only the implementation causes a compile error. Then sketch the three parts of today’s service: agreement, working method, startup program.

3.3 Find the files and check the tools

Use JDK 17 or newer and Maven 3.9.x, as in Session 2. Maven is the build tool: it reads pom.xml, downloads required libraries, compiles Java and packages a JAR. A dependency is a library our code needs. A JAR is an archive of compiled Java classes and other files. The supplied Maven configuration also includes Metro’s dependencies in the final JAR.
