        SESSION 6 · JERSEY & GRADLE

        Build a service, one understood step at a time

        6.0 Build a small service and understand the tools

        The August 2024 presenter’s manual maps WEBSRV–TL6 to the book’s mini project chapter: explain Jersey RESTful web services and Gradle (printed as “Gradl” in the supplied text). Teaching Session 7 continues the same chapter with resource methods. This session prepares you for that work by building and testing GET requests in a Gradle project.

        Our original companion project is a fictional campus shop catalogue. First return a greeting; then list three products, filter them and look up one product. You will type Java, build it, send real requests and explain the result. The exact textbook activities still come from your learner book; this supplied presenter’s manual does not reproduce them.

        From earlier sessionsWhat you will use today
        Session 1: Java, requests and JPAVariables, methods, objects and the client/server idea. Our fixed list today is held in memory.
        Session 2: JAX-RS@Path, @GET, parameters, status codes and JSON. We revisit these in smaller steps.
        Session 3: JAX-WSSOAP contracts are a useful comparison; this service uses REST requests.
        Session 4: practiceSave → build → run → test → explain, with evidence.
        Session 5: JAXBJava/XML binding helps explain conversion; Jackson handles our JSON here.
        READINESS • WITH EXAMPLES CLOSED

        Write a method that joins a greeting and a name. Explain a constructor and a getter. Predict what GET /api/products/2 asks for. Explain why an ordinary Java object is different from JSON text. If those steps are difficult, use the Java recap and request routing before starting. A beginner can enter this course, but needs the earlier practice before this lesson.

        Instructor: two-hour meeting and supported follow-up

        Use 10 minutes for readiness, 15 for Jersey’s role, 25 for the wrapper and build tasks, 25 for the greeting and one changed line, 20 for JSON/GET catalogue requests, 15 for independent transfer and 10 for an exit explanation. Prepare downloads before class. The full file typing, boundary tests and assignment need follow-up time; split the meeting if students cannot yet explain their Java. Use the beginner trial to record real confusion, assistance and next-day recall. Existing software checks do not demonstrate learner understanding.

        Each milestone follows predict → type → run → change → explain. For independent tasks use your notes, compiler and instructor feedback without AI-generated code. A check mark records your own claim; your instructor verifies the source, requests and explanation.

        6.1 Who does what: your Java, Jersey and Gradle

        Imagine a campus shop counter. A customer asks for a product; the attendant finds it and gives a reply. In our program the client sends an HTTP request. Its method, such as GET, states the action; its URL selects the resource. Your resource class supplies the Java method for that action.

        JAX-RS, now Jakarta RESTful Web Services, is a specification: agreed interfaces, annotations and behaviour. Jersey is one implementation of that specification. An annotation is metadata attached to Java code, such as @GET. Jersey reads that metadata to choose a Java method, obtain request parameters and create an HTTP response. You still write the greeting, lookup and validation rules yourself.

        PartJob in this projectWhat to inspect
        JDK HTTP serverListen on 127.0.0.1:8098 and exchange HTTP messages.Main starts a Jersey container for this server.
        JerseyRoute requests and connect parameters, Java methods and response providers.The @Path and @GET annotations.
        Jackson JSON providerWrite Java values as JSON for the response.JacksonFeature and jersey-media-json-jackson.
        Your Java classesCreate the greeting and find fictional products.GreetingLogic and ProductCatalog.
        GradleDownload dependencies, compile, assemble and launch.build.gradle and its tasks.

        Gradle is a build tool. It does useful preparation and can launch the Java server. Once Java is running, Jersey handles requests. Maven, used in earlier labs, is another build tool. You can use the same Jersey library from either. A web service is software responding to other software; a build tool prepares that software.

        Our request path is client → JDK HTTP server → Jersey routing → resource method → ordinary Java lookup → Jackson response → client. 127.0.0.1 means this computer. The service is a local learning example with fictional data. It does not include accounts, persistent storage or deployment configuration.

        PREDICT AND EXPLAIN

        Circle the part that compiles your source. Underline the part that reads @GET. Name the part that decides which product matches id 2. Then trace the arrows above aloud. Explain what a library is: reusable code added to your project, rather than a new language you must write.

        6.2 Find each file before writing code

        The new project is session6-jersey-gradle. Keep your completed Sessions 1–5 available for reference. The older folder session6-7-jersey-mini-project contains a combined CRUD example for later review. Use this new folder for the steps below.

        Project folder map
        session6-jersey-gradle/
  build.gradle                         build instructions
  settings.gradle                      project name
  gradlew.bat                          Windows wrapper launcher
  gradlew                              macOS/Linux wrapper launcher
  gradle/wrapper/                       wrapper JAR and configuration
  src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/
    GreetingLogic.java                 plain Java message
    LessonJavaDemo.java                terminal warm-up
    GreetingResource.java              GET greeting
    Main.java                          server startup
    Product.java                       one product's values
    ProductCatalog.java                fixed list and lookup
    ProductResource.java               GET catalogue methods
  build/                               generated after building

        src is source, main is application code and java is the language folder. The package declaration package com.aptech.session6; groups related classes. Its matching folder path is com/aptech/session6. A public class named Main is saved as Main.java. Enable file extensions in File Explorer so it does not become Main.java.txt.

        The project root is the folder containing build.gradle. Type Java in your editor; type commands in PowerShell. Do not paste Java inside build.gradle: that file is a Groovy build script, a different language for describing the build. You only need the small Groovy examples explained below.

        For guided work, read the provided file, then type it in your working copy and explain it. Save a copy of the project or your changed files before experimenting. The build folder contains generated output, so save evidence and your learning log in a separate evidence folder.

        6.3 Start Gradle reliably with the wrapper

        The Gradle Wrapper is a small launcher plus configuration included with a project. It uses the specified Gradle version, even when no global gradle command is installed. This project pins Gradle 9.2.1 and verifies its distribution checksum. Keep the wrapper JAR, properties and both launcher scripts together.

        Use a JDK 17–25 with this pinned Gradle version. This workstation uses JDK 25. A newer JDK needs a compatible Gradle version checked by your instructor. Our compile task produces Java 17 bytecode. Verify both java and javac; a runtime without the compiler is insufficient.

        java -version
        javac -version

        In PowerShell enter the project root. Quotation marks keep the spaces in the file path together. If your copy is elsewhere, replace the path.

        Set-Location "C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service\session6-jersey-gradle"
        Get-Location
        Get-ChildItem
        .\gradlew.bat --version

        . says “run the launcher in this current folder.” --version prints the Gradle and JVM versions. Expect Gradle 9.2.1; details vary by machine. On macOS/Linux use ./gradlew; the commands here are for Windows PowerShell.

        The first wrapper run downloads Gradle. The first compilation also downloads project libraries from Maven Central. Internet access and a writable user cache are needed for that preparation; timings depend on your connection. Downloading is preparation, not the Java server starting. Your instructor should run these steps before class and check a second run.

        .\gradlew.bat tasks --all
        .\gradlew.bat lessonInfo

        A task is a named build action. tasks --all lists tasks, including compileJava, run, installDist and our lessonInfo. lessonInfo prints Session 6: Jersey handles requests; Gradle builds and runs Java. Expect BUILD SUCCESSFUL after a successful completed task. The server run task stays active until you stop it.

        GUIDED CHANGE • BUILD TOOL ONLY

        Find the println sentence in build.gradle. Change its final word to “programs”, save, and run lessonInfo again. Predict the line first. This demonstrates a build-script change; it does not change the HTTP greeting. Restore the reference sentence when done.

        6.4 Read the build script one small block at a time

        build.gradle — Groovy build script
        // Gradle reads this Groovy build script. Java source belongs in src/main/java.
plugins {
    id 'java'         // compileJava, classes and jar tasks
    id 'application'  // run and installDist tasks
}

group = 'com.aptech'
version = '1.0'

repositories {
    mavenCentral() // Repository for dependencies; this does not run Maven.
}

dependencies {
    implementation 'org.glassfish.jersey.containers:jersey-container-jdk-http:3.1.5'
    implementation 'org.glassfish.jersey.inject:jersey-hk2:3.1.5'
    implementation 'org.glassfish.jersey.media:jersey-media-json-jackson:3.1.5'
}

tasks.withType(JavaCompile).configureEach {
    options.release = 17
    options.encoding = 'UTF-8'
}

application {
    mainClass = 'com.aptech.session6.Main'
    applicationDefaultJvmArgs = ['--add-modules=jdk.httpserver']
}

// A small task you can change before working on HTTP requests.
tasks.register('lessonInfo') {
    group = 'learning'
    description = 'Print the purpose of this teaching project.'
    doLast {
        println 'Session 6: Jersey handles requests; Gradle builds and runs Java.'
    }
}

tasks.register('javaBridge', JavaExec) {
    group = 'learning'
    description = 'Run the plain Java warm-up without starting a server.'
    classpath = sourceSets.main.runtimeClasspath
    mainClass = 'com.aptech.session6.LessonJavaDemo'
}
        settings.gradle — Groovy settings script
        rootProject.name = 'session6-jersey-gradle'
        Block or lineMeaningSmall proof
        plugins { id … }Enable Gradle’s Java and application tools. Plugins add tasks and conventions.Find compileJava and run in tasks --all.
        repositories { mavenCentral() }Say where dependency files can be obtained. A repository is a library store.Dependencies below name the libraries to request.
        implementation group:name:versionDeclare code needed when compiling and running this application. Colon-separated coordinates identify the library.Container 3.1.5 integrates Jersey with the JDK server; HK2 supplies injection support; Jackson adds JSON conversion.
        options.release = 17Compile against Java 17 APIs and produce Java 17 class files.The Gradle process can run on this JDK 25 machine.
        mainClassTell the application plugin which class has the starting main method.run launches com.aptech.session6.Main.
        applicationDefaultJvmArgsPass the required JDK HTTP-server module option when launching.You need not type the module option in the run command.
        tasks.register and doLastDefine a named task and the action it performs when executed.lessonInfo prints only when that task runs.
        JavaExec, classpath and javaBridgeLaunch a different Java main using compiled classes and required libraries.javaBridge prints and exits; no HTTP server starts.
        rootProject.nameName the Gradle project and generated installation folder.installDist writes build/install/session6-jersey-gradle.

        A dependency is a library your code relies on. A transitive dependency is another library that it relies on. Gradle resolves these together. Classpath is the set of places Java searches for compiled classes and libraries. The run task assembles it; manually running the small jar without those libraries will fail.

        .\gradlew.bat dependencies --configuration runtimeClasspath
        .\gradlew.bat classes
        .\gradlew.bat build

        The dependencies report is a tree, not an error just because it is long. classes compiles Java and processes resources. build assembles output and runs configured checks. This project has no automated student test task; seeing test NO-SOURCE or BUILD SUCCESSFUL does not prove the endpoints are correct. You must send the requests later in this lesson.

        UP-TO-DATE means Gradle can reuse unchanged output. FROM-CACHE, when present, means cached output was reused. A saved Java change makes the relevant compilation necessary again. clean removes generated build output, so keep your evidence elsewhere. Use --offline only after Gradle and all necessary libraries are cached; it cannot fetch missing files.

        CHECK YOUR UNDERSTANDING

        Explain why mavenCentral() does not mean you are using Maven to build. Identify the library that handles JSON. Explain why Gradle can finish a build successfully even when your greeting says the wrong words.

        6.5 Rehearse ordinary Java before HTTP

        A method is a named group of instructions. String is text; int is a whole number. return hands a result to the caller. static lets us call this helper through its class name. The helper below has no networking.

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/GreetingLogic.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

// Ordinary Java: no Jersey annotation and no HTTP connection here.
public class GreetingLogic {
    public static String message(String name) {
        return "Welcome, " + name + "! You are learning Jersey with Gradle.";
    }
}

        In message(String name), message is the method name, name is the parameter and the String before message is the return type. The + operators join text. GreetingLogic.message("Ada") therefore returns one sentence; it does not print it or send HTTP.

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/Product.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

// A message model, not a JPA entity. These examples only read its values.
public class Product {
    private final int id;
    private final String name;
    private final int priceNgn;
    private final String category;

    public Product(int id, String name, int priceNgn, String category) {
        this.id = id;
        this.name = name;
        this.priceNgn = priceNgn;
        this.category = category;
    }

    public int getId() { return id; }
    public String getName() { return name; }
    public int getPriceNgn() { return priceNgn; }
    public String getCategory() { return category; }
}

        A class describes objects. new Product(1, "Notebook", 1500, "Stationery") creates one object and calls its constructor. this.name = name copies the constructor parameter into the object’s field. private hides fields from direct outside access. final makes each field fixed after construction. A getter returns a field’s value. These products are read-only examples; there are no setters here.

        priceNgn is an illustrative whole-naira amount. It is not a floating point currency calculation; different applications may need integer minor units or BigDecimal with explicit rounding rules. Today 1500 means NGN 1500. The id identifies a product; it is not its list position.

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/LessonJavaDemo.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

public class LessonJavaDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String learner = "Ada";
        System.out.println(GreetingLogic.message(learner));
        Product item = new Product(1, "Notebook", 1500, "Stationery");
        System.out.println(item.getId() + ": " + item.getName()
                + " costs NGN " + item.getPriceNgn());
    }
}
        .\gradlew.bat javaBridge
        Expected application lines, mixed with Gradle task output
        Welcome, Ada! You are learning Jersey with Gradle.
1: Notebook costs NGN 1500

        main starts the warm-up. A local variable named learner holds Ada. The first println prints the returned message. The second uses getters to build a sentence. Printing a Product object directly would not automatically produce JSON.

        Optional: prove the warm-up needs only JDK code after setup
        New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force build/manual-classes
        javac -d build/manual-classes src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/GreetingLogic.java src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/Product.java src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/LessonJavaDemo.java
        java -cp build/manual-classes com.aptech.session6.LessonJavaDemo

        These three classes have no external imports. -d selects the output folder; -cp selects where Java looks for classes. The package means you run the fully qualified class name. This is a useful comparison with Gradle managing a larger classpath.

        TYPE AND CHANGE

        Predict the warm-up for learner “Tunde” and a Notebook price of 1800. Change those two values in LessonJavaDemo, save and run javaBridge. Compare the actual output. Explain why this does not change the separate seeded ProductCatalog. Restore the reference values before the HTTP tests.

        6.6 Write the first resource and follow every line

        A resource method connects an HTTP request to ordinary Java. We use Response so we can choose the status as well as the body. An import tells Java where an external type name comes from; Gradle still has to supply that library.

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/GreetingResource.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

import jakarta.ws.rs.DefaultValue;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;

@Path("/greeting")
@Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)
public class GreetingResource {
    @GET
    public Response greet(@DefaultValue("learner") @QueryParam("name") String name) {
        String cleanedName = name.trim();
        if (cleanedName.isEmpty() || cleanedName.length() > 40) {
            return Response.status(400)
                    .entity("name must contain 1 to 40 characters after trimming.").build();
        }
        return Response.ok(GreetingLogic.message(cleanedName)).build();
    }
}
        CodeMeaning
        @Path("/greeting")Choose the path within the server’s /api/ base. Full URL: http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting.
        @Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)Offer a plain text response. Produces describes the response format.
        @GETUse this method for an HTTP GET request.
        @QueryParam("name")Read name from ?name=Ada in the URL. This is request input.
        @DefaultValue("learner")Use learner when name is absent. An explicitly empty name is still empty.
        name.trim()Remove surrounding ordinary spaces, then check the cleaned result.
        isEmpty() or length() > 40Reject blank or too-long input with HTTP 400. This rule is your Java code.
        Response.status(400).entity(…).build()Choose 400 Bad Request, supply its body and finish the response object.
        Response.ok(GreetingLogic.message(…)).build()Choose 200 OK and put the helper’s returned text in the body.

        An HTTP response contains a status, headers and a body. A 200 means this request succeeded. A 400 means the input is unacceptable. A body explains or supplies data; a header such as Content-Type describes that body. entity is the data you ask Jersey to put in the body.

        The length check here uses Java String.length, which counts UTF-16 code units. For this beginner boundary exercise use ordinary letters/spaces; user-perceived character counting can require different handling. The maximum is 40 after trimming. No request should rely on a UI text box to enforce this server rule.

        PREDICT BEFORE RUNNING

        Write the status and body for: no name; ?name=Ada; ?name=; ?name=%20%20; and 41 repeated A letters. %20 encodes a space in a URL. In the next block prove each result with a real request.

        6.7 Start Java in terminal A; send requests from B

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/Main.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

import java.net.URI;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.concurrent.CountDownLatch;
import com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jdkhttp.JdkHttpServerFactory;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jackson.JacksonFeature;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ResourceConfig;

public class Main {
    public static final String BASE_URI = "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/";

    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        boolean helloOnly = Arrays.asList(args).contains("--hello-only");
        ResourceConfig config = new ResourceConfig().register(GreetingResource.class);
        if (!helloOnly) {
            config.register(ProductResource.class).register(JacksonFeature.class);
        }
        HttpServer server = JdkHttpServerFactory.createHttpServer(URI.create(BASE_URI), config);
        Runtime.getRuntime().addShutdownHook(new Thread(() -> server.stop(0)));
        System.out.println("Session 6 server: " + BASE_URI + "greeting");
        System.out.println(helloOnly ? "Greeting only." : "Products: " + BASE_URI + "products");
        System.out.println("Leave terminal A open. Use terminal B for requests. Ctrl+C stops A.");
        new CountDownLatch(1).await();
    }
}

        main(String[] args) receives launcher arguments. Arrays.asList(args).contains("--hello-only") asks whether the greeting-only switch was supplied. A boolean stores true or false. The if block registers the product resource and JSON provider only in full mode.

        ResourceConfig is Jersey’s list of resources/providers. register(GreetingResource.class) registers a class; .class describes the type, rather than making a product object. JdkHttpServerFactory starts the JDK HTTP server with that configuration and the base URI. URI represents the address. The base ends with /api/; the resource contributes /greeting.

        The final wait keeps the Java process alive for requests. Its shutdown hook stops the HTTP server when you stop the launcher. The CountDownLatch and Thread are startup support; you need not redesign them for the beginner tasks. Java syntax () -> server.stop(0) describes the shutdown action. Focus on the base URL and resource registration.

        Terminal A: launch greeting-only mode

        .\gradlew.bat run --args="--hello-only"

        Expect Session 6 server: http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting, Greeting only. and the instruction to leave A open. It stays busy because the server is running; Gradle’s active-task display is normal. Wait for the server line before sending requests. A separate window or terminal tab is terminal B.

        A warning you may see during startup

        Jersey may print “JAXBContext implementation could not be found. WADL feature is disabled.” WADL means Web Application Description Language, an optional XML description of a REST API. This project does not enable that documentation feature. The warning does not stop our greeting or Jackson JSON responses; verify the server line and your actual request. You do not need to install JAXB to complete this GET lesson.

        Terminal B: send GET requests

        curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting
        curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting?name=Ada"
        curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting?name="
        curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting?name=%20%20"

        Use curl.exe explicitly: older Windows PowerShell uses curl as an alias for a different command. -i includes the response status and headers. The two successful bodies are “Welcome, learner! You are learning Jersey with Gradle.” and “Welcome, Ada! You are learning Jersey with Gradle.” The last two return 400 and “name must contain 1 to 40 characters after trimming.” Header order and dates can differ.

        curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products

        This returns 404 in greeting-only mode because ProductResource was not registered. The framework’s error body may differ. This deliberate check proves which mode is running; it does not mean products are broken.

        GUIDED CHANGE • A REAL RESTART

        Stop A with Ctrl+C. Change “Welcome” to “Hello” in GreetingLogic, save and start greeting-only mode again. Repeat the Ada request in B and record the new body. Explain why refreshing the browser before rebuilding/restarting would show the old code. Restore Welcome and restart for the reference tests. Stop A again before full mode.

        6.8 Turn Java products into JSON and read a list

        A List<Product> is an ordered collection of Product references. The angle brackets state the element type. List.of creates our fixed list. The static field is shared by calls in this Java process. It is not an H2 table: restarting creates the same three fictional examples again.

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/ProductCatalog.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class ProductCatalog {
    // Fixed fictional examples held in memory. No database or write operations.
    private static final List<Product> ITEMS = List.of(
            new Product(1, "Notebook", 1500, "Stationery"),
            new Product(2, "USB Mouse", 6500, "Accessories"),
            new Product(3, "Pen Set", 1000, "Stationery"));

    public static List<Product> findAll(String category) {
        if (category == null || category.trim().isEmpty()) {
            return ITEMS;
        }
        List<Product> matches = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Product item : ITEMS) {
            if (item.getCategory().equalsIgnoreCase(category.trim())) {
                matches.add(item);
            }
        }
        return matches;
    }

    public static Product findById(int id) {
        for (Product item : ITEMS) {
            if (item.getId() == id) {
                return item;
            }
        }
        return null;
    }
}

        findAll returns all products if the category is absent or blank. Otherwise it starts an empty ArrayList, loops over the items and adds matching ones. The enhanced loop for (Product item : ITEMS) means “for each product in ITEMS”. equalsIgnoreCase lets Stationery and stationery match; trim removes surrounding ordinary spaces. No match gives an empty list.

        findById checks each product’s id. == compares these integer values. It returns the matching object or null, meaning no object was found. The resource must handle null before claiming success.

        src/main/java/com/aptech/session6/ProductResource.java
        package com.aptech.session6;

import java.util.Map;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.PathParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;

@Path("/products")
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class ProductResource {
    @GET
    public Response list(@QueryParam("category") String category) {
        return Response.ok(ProductCatalog.findAll(category)).build();
    }

    @GET
    @Path("/{id}")
    public Response find(@PathParam("id") int id) {
        if (id < 1) {
            return Response.status(400).entity(Map.of("error", "id must be positive.")).build();
        }
        Product item = ProductCatalog.findById(id);
        if (item == null) {
            return Response.status(404).entity(Map.of("error", "Product " + id + " was not found.")).build();
        }
        return Response.ok(item).build();
    }
}

        The class path is /products and its response format is JSON. The first @GET method has no extra path, so it handles GET /api/products and its optional category query. The second has @Path("/{id}"): braces mark a variable path segment. Jersey converts that segment to an int for @PathParam.

        find checks positive ids, then looks up the product. An existing id returns 200 and the product. A missing positive id returns 404 with an error. A zero/negative id returns our own 400 error. Map.of("error", "…") makes a small key/value object; Jackson writes it as JSON. This avoids constructing JSON by string concatenation.

        JSON is text representing values. An object has braces and named properties; a list has square brackets. Jackson uses public getters to serialize our products. This output-only model needs no empty constructor, setters or JAXB annotations. Reading a POST body back into a model needs a compatible deserialization design; that is later work. @XmlRootElement alone would not install an XML response provider.

        Terminal A: full mode

        .\gradlew.bat run

        Stop the greeting-only server first. Full mode prints the products URL and registers JacksonFeature. In terminal B:

        curl.exe -i -H "Accept: application/json" http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products
        Expected body: compare values, not property order
        [
  {
    "id": 1,
    "name": "Notebook",
    "priceNgn": 1500,
    "category": "Stationery"
  },
  {
    "id": 2,
    "name": "USB Mouse",
    "priceNgn": 6500,
    "category": "Accessories"
  },
  {
    "id": 3,
    "name": "Pen Set",
    "priceNgn": 1000,
    "category": "Stationery"
  }
]

        Expect 200 and Content-Type application/json. JSON property order and whitespace are not important; ids and values are. Accept requests a response format. This GET has no body, so no Content-Type request header is needed. The product price unit is named explicitly in the property.

        6.9 Test parameters, boundaries and supported formats

        curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products?category=Stationery"
        curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products?category=stationery"
        curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products?category=Unknown"
        curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products/2
        curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products/999
        curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products/0
        Request or boundaryExpected status / resultReason
        category=Stationery or stationery200; ids 1 and 3.Our case-insensitive category rule matches.
        category=Unknown200; [].A successful search can have no matches.
        category absent or category=200; all three.Our chosen rule treats blank as no filter.
        products/2200; USB Mouse, NGN 6500.Path id identifies one existing item.
        products/999404; {"error":"Product 999 was not found."}A positive id with no item.
        products/0 or products/-1400; {"error":"id must be positive."}Our resource checks the numeric boundary.
        products/abc or an int overflow such as products/2147483648404; framework rejection.Jersey cannot convert this path parameter to int; our method is not called.
        Accept: application/xml on products406 Not Acceptable.This resource offers JSON only.
        POST /api/products405 Method Not Allowed.This session registers only GET methods.
        greeting name of 40 ordinary letters200.The maximum length is allowed.
        greeting name of 41 ordinary letters400.One beyond the maximum is rejected.
        curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products/abc
        curl.exe -i -H "Accept: application/xml" http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products
        curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/products

        Framework rejection bodies can be empty or implementation-specific; compare their status and the relevant route rule. Our explicitly coded errors above are JSON. A 404 can therefore mean an unknown path, an unregistered resource, a failed path conversion or an absent item. Inspect the request and source instead of assuming all 404s have one cause.

        PowerShell: form and test the length boundary
        $s6Name40 = 'A' * 40
curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting?name=$s6Name40"
$s6Name41 = 'A' * 41
curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8098/api/greeting?name=$s6Name41"

        The * operator repeats the text in this PowerShell snippet. Do not put it in a Java file. For every test write the prediction, request, actual status, important body values and explanation. A successful curl exit code alone does not prove HTTP 200; curl can receive a 404 successfully.

          6.12 Use the symptom to find the right layer

          SymptomLikely layer and next action
          gradlew.bat not recognizedCheck Get-Location and Get-ChildItem. Use .\gradlew.bat in the folder containing it.
          Could not load GradleWrapperMainCheck gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar and the properties file. Restore the complete instructor-supplied wrapper.
          Download, certificate or proxy failureFirst-time tool/library preparation needs network access. Ask the instructor to use the centre’s approved proxy/certificate setup. --offline works only with a complete cache.
          Unsupported Java version or release 17 not supportedCheck java -version, javac -version and .\gradlew.bat --version. Align JAVA_HOME, PATH and the supported JDK.
          JAVA_HOME is invalidJAVA_HOME must name the JDK folder, not its bin/java.exe. Reopen the terminal after correcting it.
          Cannot find symbol / package does not existRead the first compiler error. Check spelling, imports and declared dependencies. An import alone does not download a library.
          Address already in useStop your earlier Session 6 server in A. If needed choose another unused port in BASE_URI and change every client URL, then rebuild/restart.
          Connection refusedWait for the server line; check A is alive and B uses the same host/port. Opening the HTML lesson does not start Java.
          404 for productsCheck greeting-only versus full mode, /api/products spelling, the id and source registration.
          406 for XMLProducts offer JSON only. Use Accept: application/json; XML support requires a compatible provider and model design.
          405 for POSTGET-only catalogue is expected today. Session 7 adds write methods after review.
          500 or no JSON writerCheck the first server-side exception, Jackson dependency and JacksonFeature registration. A successful compile is only the first check.
          Old response after an editSave, stop A, rebuild/restart and retest. Look for an earlier process still on the port.
          Small jar fails with NoClassDefFoundErrorThe normal jar omits dependency JARs. Use Gradle run or the installed application script below.
          .\gradlew.bat build --stacktrace

          A stack trace shows calls around a failure. Read the first relevant cause and source location; do not edit unrelated files because the output is long. Write a debug note: symptom → prediction → evidence → repair → retest.

          Run the assembled application without Gradle

          Milestone hints after your own plan

          Start with the model and a plain Java print. Next list rooms as JSON. Then add lookup and building filter. Add minSeats last. One option is an int @QueryParam with @DefaultValue("0"); Jersey rejects unconvertible query integers with 400, while your code checks negatives. Combine filters with AND: a room must satisfy both when both are present. Do not use the id as a list index. Return JSON error data through a Map, and record actual boundary statuses.

Document your filter rules before coding. Save source, exact commands, predictions/actual statuses, response samples, one debug note and your downloaded log in evidence. There is no completed Room solution on this page.

          6.15 Find unfamiliar words quickly

          TermPlain meaning / example
          BuildPrepare runnable output from source and dependencies.
          WrapperProject launcher/configuration for a pinned Gradle version.
          TaskNamed action such as classes, lessonInfo or run.
          PluginAdds build behaviour; java and application add standard tasks.
          DependencyLibrary needed by your code; transitive dependencies are its own required libraries.
          RepositoryPlace to obtain dependency files, such as Maven Central.
          ClasspathLocations where Java looks for compiled classes and libraries.
          PackageGroup of related Java classes, such as com.aptech.session6.
          ResourceAn API thing you address, such as products.
          AnnotationMetadata such as @GET that Jersey reads.
          ProviderIntegration that reads/writes message data, such as Jackson JSON support.
          SerializationConvert Java values into message text such as JSON.
          Path parameterVariable part of a URL path: 2 in /products/2.
          Query parameterNamed URL input after ?: category in ?category=Stationery.
          StatusHTTP result code, such as 200 or 404.
          Header / bodyMessage metadata / message content. Accept requests an output format.
          In-memory dataValues in the running Java process; our fixed list is recreated from source on restart.
          Boundary testTest at, just below and just above a rule’s limit.
          Installation distributionLauncher plus application/dependency files produced by installDist.

          Continue to Session 7: POST, PUT and DELETE after your practical demonstration →

          SESSION 7 · JERSEY CRUD

          Create, replace and delete with evidence

          7.0 Give your service carefully controlled write operations

          The supplied August 2024 presenter’s manual maps WEBSRV–TL7 to the book’s same Jersey mini project chapter used in teaching Session 6. It asks you to describe Jersey in a Gradle-managed project and create different HTTP requests in the resource class. We now add POST, PUT and DELETE to the GET catalogue you already understand.

          Our original companion project continues the fictional campus shop story. Read products, create a USB Hub, replace its details and remove it. Prove each change with a following GET, then prove bad requests leave the data unchanged. Use session7-jersey-crud for this lesson; the older combined Sessions 6–7 example is legacy reference. Assign the exact learner-book activities separately, because the coverage manual does not contain their questions.

          READINESS • EXAMPLES CLOSED

          Explain the jobs of Gradle, Jersey and Jackson. Start Session 6 full mode, obtain product 2 and explain its JSON fields. Write a method with a parameter and return value. Explain why the fixed list reappears on restart. If you cannot demonstrate those steps, revisit the Java warm-up, startup and GET tests before adding writes.

          StageYou doEvidence

          Guided writesPOST → GET → PUT → GET → DELETE → GET.Status, Location, body and stored values agree.

          Instructor: two-hour core meeting plus supported follow-up

          Use 10 minutes for readiness, 15 for the HTTP contract, 20 for request models/Java storage, 20 for POST and a confirming GET, 15 for PUT, 10 for DELETE, 15 for rejection tests and 15 for an independent attempt/exit explanation. Prepare dependencies and examples before class. Full source typing, all boundaries and the Room assignment need additional supported/independent time. Split the meeting when a learner cannot yet explain a branch. Use the beginner-trial protocol to record help and next-day recall, rather than treating checkboxes as measured understanding.

          Follow predict → type → run → change → explain. For independent tasks, use your notes, editor, compiler and instructor hints without AI-generated answers or another learner’s completed code. Build-tool configuration and parser support may be reused; write and explain your own application methods.

          7.1 Decide what each request means before coding

          CRUD means Create, Read, Update, Delete, four common data operations. HTTP methods communicate the intended action. Opening a URL in a browser normally sends GET; it does not let you choose a POST body or run a DELETE. Use curl.exe or Postman for the write exercises.

          Method and routeMeaning in this APISuccessful response
          GET /api/productsRead all products, optionally filtered by category.200 JSON array; a search with no matches gives [].
          GET /api/products/{id}Read one existing positive id.200 JSON product.
          POST /api/productsCreate a new product; server assigns id.201 JSON product and its Location header.
          PUT /api/products/{id}Replace all writable fields of an existing product.200 JSON replacement, preserving the path id.
          DELETE /api/products/{id}Remove an existing product.204 No Content; no response body.

          A contract is the agreed request/response behaviour. Our write body has exactly three fields: name, priceNgn and category. The response adds id. An input model with no id prevents a request from choosing the server’s identifier; our parser also rejects unknown properties instead of silently ignoring them.

          Input ruleAccepted exampleRejected example
          name: JSON text, 1–60 code units after trim"USB Hub"null, missing, spaces, 61 ordinary letters, a numeric name
          priceNgn: JSON integer, 0–1000000 inclusive0 or 7500missing, null, -1, 1000001, "7500" or 7500.5
          category: JSON text, 1–30 code units after trim"Accessories"missing, spaces or 31 ordinary letters
          Body: one JSON object with those properties{"name":"Pen","priceNgn":500,"category":"Stationery"}array, malformed JSON, extra field id, or a second trailing object

          These prices are fictional whole-naira amounts, not floating point currency calculations. Zero is allowed for a free item. The text limits use Java String.length, which counts UTF-16 code units; use ordinary letters/spaces for beginner length tests. Trimming removes surrounding ordinary spaces. A field can be present yet invalid; valid JSON syntax alone does not mean the product is acceptable.

          PUT is full replacement here. All three writable fields are required. Sending only a price is rejected, rather than clearing other fields or acting like PATCH. Our PUT does not create an absent id: valid data for an unknown positive id gives 404. Other APIs may choose different contracts, so read their rules.

          PREDICT • RECORD THE CONTRACT

          Write the expected code for creating valid data, replacing product 4, deleting product 4, looking it up afterwards and submitting a blank name. Draw where the id comes from on POST and where it comes from on PUT. Explain why a JSON string "0" is different from the integer 0.

          The plugins, dependency repository and Java release are unchanged ideas from Session 6. mainClass now names com.aptech.session7.Main. The application plugin supplies the classpath and JDK HTTP module option. javaBridge runs LessonJavaDemo; run starts Main. Jersey and Jackson handle messages after Gradle launches Java.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/Main.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

import java.net.URI;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.concurrent.CountDownLatch;
import com.sun.net.httpserver.HttpServer;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jdkhttp.JdkHttpServerFactory;
import org.glassfish.jersey.jackson.JacksonFeature;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ResourceConfig;
import org.glassfish.jersey.server.ServerProperties;

public class Main {
    public static final String BASE_URI = "http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/";
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        boolean readOnly = Arrays.asList(args).contains("--read-only");
        ResourceConfig config = new ResourceConfig()
                .property(ServerProperties.WADL_FEATURE_DISABLE, true)
                .register(JacksonFeature.class).register(JsonSupport.class);
        if (readOnly) { config.register(ReadOnlyProductsResource.class); }
        else { config.register(ProductResource.class); }
        HttpServer server = JdkHttpServerFactory.createHttpServer(URI.create(BASE_URI), config);
        Runtime.getRuntime().addShutdownHook(new Thread(() -> server.stop(0)));
        System.out.println("Session 7 server: " + BASE_URI + "products");
        System.out.println(readOnly ? "Read-only mode." : "CRUD mode: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE.");
        System.out.println("Leave A running; send requests from B. Ctrl+C stops A.");
        new CountDownLatch(1).await();
    }
}

          ResourceConfig registers JacksonFeature and our JsonSupport, then exactly one resource class for the chosen mode. WADL, optional XML API documentation, is disabled because this JSON lesson does not use it. The URI ends /api/ and the resource adds /products. The server listens only on this computer at 127.0.0.1:8099, a separate port from Session 6.

          Terminal A: start the baseline

          .\gradlew.bat run --args="--read-only"

          Wait for Session 7 server: http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products and Read-only mode. Leave A running. In a separate terminal B:

          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products
          curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products

          Expect 200 JSON for GET and 405 Method Not Allowed for POST because write methods are not registered. The list contains Notebook id 1/1500, USB Mouse id 2/6500 and Pen Set id 3/1000, the same fictional values as Session 6. Stop A with Ctrl+C before launching another mode; answer a batch-job prompt if your terminal asks.

          7.3 Understand missing values and practise changes in Java

          Session 6’s Product is immutable and output-only. It still works as our stored/response data. To deserialize means convert incoming JSON into a Java object. We now use a separate ProductInput with a no-argument constructor and setters, a simple mapping shape Jackson can populate.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/Product.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

// Immutable response data: the stored values do not change through setters.
public class Product {
    private final int id;
    private final String name;
    private final int priceNgn;
    private final String category;

    public Product(int id, String name, int priceNgn, String category) {
        this.id = id;
        this.name = name;
        this.priceNgn = priceNgn;
        this.category = category;
    }
    public int getId() { return id; }
    public String getName() { return name; }
    public int getPriceNgn() { return priceNgn; }
    public String getCategory() { return category; }
}
          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/ProductInput.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

// Incoming JSON has these three fields. The server chooses id separately.
public class ProductInput {
    private String name;
    private Integer priceNgn;
    private String category;

    public ProductInput() { }
    public String getName() { return name; }
    public void setName(String name) { this.name = name; }
    public Integer getPriceNgn() { return priceNgn; }
    public void setPriceNgn(Integer priceNgn) { this.priceNgn = priceNgn; }
    public String getCategory() { return category; }
    public void setCategory(String category) { this.category = category; }
}

          A getter returns a field’s value. A setter assigns a supplied value to a field. In setName(String name), the right name is a parameter; this.name is the object’s field. Jackson can call the constructor, then set the matching JSON properties. The private fields protect direct access; setters do not automatically validate your business rules.

          Integer is the object wrapper for primitive int. It can be null, meaning no value. A primitive int defaults to zero, which would confuse a missing price with an explicitly free product. ProductInput keeps priceNgn nullable until validation; Product stores a definite int afterwards. Checking for null before comparison avoids a NullPointerException.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/ProductRules.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

public class ProductRules {
    // A null return means valid; a String explains the first invalid rule.
    public static String validate(ProductInput input) {
        if (input == null) { return "A JSON product object is required."; }
        if (input.getName() == null || input.getName().trim().isEmpty()
                || input.getName().trim().length() > 60) {
            return "name must contain 1 to 60 characters after trimming.";
        }
        if (input.getPriceNgn() == null || input.getPriceNgn() < 0
                || input.getPriceNgn() > 1_000_000) {
            return "priceNgn must be a whole number from 0 to 1000000.";
        }
        if (input.getCategory() == null || input.getCategory().trim().isEmpty()
                || input.getCategory().trim().length() > 30) {
            return "category must contain 1 to 30 characters after trimming.";
        }
        return null;
    }
}

          The validate method returns the first problem as text, or null when all rules pass. || means OR and short-circuits: later tests are skipped once an earlier one is true. Thus input == null is checked before calling getters, and a null name is checked before trim. 1_000_000 is the integer 1000000; underscores improve readability, not the value.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/LessonJavaDemo.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

public class LessonJavaDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Initial count: " + ProductStore.findAll(null).size());
        ProductInput input = new ProductInput();
        input.setName(" USB Hub ");
        input.setPriceNgn(7500);
        input.setCategory(" Accessories ");
        String problem = ProductRules.validate(input);
        if (problem != null) { throw new IllegalArgumentException(problem); }
        Product saved = ProductStore.create(input);
        System.out.println("Created: " + saved.getId() + " " + saved.getName());
        input.setPriceNgn(8500);
        Product replacement = ProductStore.replace(saved.getId(), input);
        System.out.println("Replaced price: " + replacement.getPriceNgn());
        System.out.println("Deleted: " + ProductStore.delete(saved.getId()));
        System.out.println("Final count: " + ProductStore.findAll(null).size());
        input.setName("   ");
        System.out.println("Rejected: " + ProductRules.validate(input));
    }
}
          .\gradlew.bat javaBridge
          Expected application lines
          Initial count: 3
Created: 4 USB Hub
Replaced price: 8500
Deleted: true
Final count: 3
Rejected: name must contain 1 to 60 characters after trimming.

          The demo constructs an input, sets its fields and validates before creating. IllegalArgumentException stops this warm-up if its hardcoded input is invalid. The returned Product shows normalized text and the server-chosen id. Replacing creates a new immutable Product with the same id. Deletion removes the Map entry. No HTTP happens in this warm-up; each run is a new Java process with a new seeded store.

          GUIDED CHANGE • BEFORE NETWORKING

          Predict the output if you change the created name to “Desk Lamp” and the replacement price to 9000. Change those values in LessonJavaDemo, save and run javaBridge. Then set the initial price to -1: explain why validation stops before insertion. Restore the reference values. Explain why this Java demo does not alter the separate running HTTP process.

          7.4 Separate JSON reading from application validation

          The client sends JSON text in an HTTP body. Jackson maps it to ProductInput before the Java resource method can use it. Your resource then calls ProductRules. These are separate checks: parsing/mapping first, business validation second. If JSON is malformed or a property has the wrong type, mapping fails before your create/replace code runs.

          examples/new-product.json
          {"name":"USB Hub","priceNgn":7500,"category":"Accessories"}

          Property names match ProductInput: name, priceNgn and category. Strings use double quotes; the integer has none. Save the file as ordinary UTF-8 text. When typing it yourself, use plain straight quotes, not word-processor quotation marks. The body does not contain id.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/JsonSupport.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.DeserializationFeature;
import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.MapperFeature;
import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.ObjectMapper;
import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.json.JsonMapper;
import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.cfg.CoercionAction;
import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.cfg.CoercionInputShape;
import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.type.LogicalType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.ext.ContextResolver;
import jakarta.ws.rs.ext.Provider;

// Reusable parser configuration: do not silently accept misspelled fields or prices.
@Provider
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class JsonSupport implements ContextResolver<ObjectMapper> {
    private final ObjectMapper mapper = JsonMapper.builder()
            .enable(DeserializationFeature.FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES)
            .enable(DeserializationFeature.FAIL_ON_TRAILING_TOKENS)
            .disable(DeserializationFeature.ACCEPT_FLOAT_AS_INT)
            .disable(MapperFeature.ALLOW_COERCION_OF_SCALARS)
            .build();

    public JsonSupport() {
        mapper.coercionConfigFor(LogicalType.Textual)
                .setCoercion(CoercionInputShape.Integer, CoercionAction.Fail)
                .setCoercion(CoercionInputShape.Float, CoercionAction.Fail)
                .setCoercion(CoercionInputShape.Boolean, CoercionAction.Fail);
    }

    @Override
    public ObjectMapper getContext(Class<?> type) { return mapper; }
}

          This is reusable parser configuration, registered in Main. A provider integrates framework behaviour. ContextResolver<ObjectMapper> supplies Jackson’s configured mapper; @Provider marks the helper and @Produces selects JSON. @Override says getContext implements the promised interface method. You may retain this provided helper when writing independent application code; explain its role instead of memorizing every library constant.

          SettingReason
          FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIESReject misspelled/extra fields, including a client-supplied id.
          FAIL_ON_TRAILING_TOKENSReject a second value after the intended JSON object.
          Disable ACCEPT_FLOAT_AS_INTReject a decimal token rather than truncating a price.
          Disable ALLOW_COERCION_OF_SCALARSReject a quoted or boolean price instead of silently converting it to an integer.
          Textual coercion Fail settingsReject numeric/boolean name or category values instead of turning them into text.

          After mapping, valid text can still be blank and a valid integer can still be negative; ProductRules must reject those. Binding metadata and parser settings do not replace application rules. Our explicit application errors return JSON with an error property. Jersey/Jackson mapping or media-type rejection bodies may differ; verify their HTTP status and that storage remains unchanged.

          CLASSIFY THE FAILURE

          For each input decide whether parsing/mapping or ProductRules rejects it: malformed syntax; priceNgn "7500"; priceNgn -1; name " "; missing priceNgn; unknown property priceNGN. Explain why a correctly parsed object is not automatically accepted.

          7.5 Use a Map to create, replace and remove entries

          A Map<Integer, Product> connects an id key to a Product value. ITEMS.get(4) looks up key 4; it does not mean the fourth list position. The final variable points to the same Map, while its entries can change. LinkedHashMap keeps insertion order so our teaching list is predictable.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/ProductStore.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.LinkedHashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;

public class ProductStore {
    private static final Map<Integer, Product> ITEMS = new LinkedHashMap<>();
    private static int nextId = 4;
    static {
        ITEMS.put(1, new Product(1, "Notebook", 1500, "Stationery"));
        ITEMS.put(2, new Product(2, "USB Mouse", 6500, "Accessories"));
        ITEMS.put(3, new Product(3, "Pen Set", 1000, "Stationery"));
    }

    public static synchronized List<Product> findAll(String category) {
        List<Product> matches = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Product item : ITEMS.values()) {
            if (category == null || category.trim().isEmpty()
                    || item.getCategory().equalsIgnoreCase(category.trim())) {
                matches.add(item);
            }
        }
        return matches;
    }

    public static synchronized Product findById(int id) { return ITEMS.get(id); }

    // Resource methods validate first. The same lock covers id selection and insert.
    public static synchronized Product create(ProductInput input) {
        Product saved = fromInput(nextId, input);
        ITEMS.put(nextId, saved);
        nextId++;
        return saved;
    }

    public static synchronized Product replace(int id, ProductInput input) {
        if (!ITEMS.containsKey(id)) { return null; }
        Product replacement = fromInput(id, input);
        ITEMS.put(id, replacement);
        return replacement;
    }

    public static synchronized boolean delete(int id) { return ITEMS.remove(id) != null; }

    private static Product fromInput(int id, ProductInput input) {
        return new Product(id, input.getName().trim(), input.getPriceNgn(),
                input.getCategory().trim());
    }
}
          Method or lineWhat it does
          static initializerSeed the three fictional products when the class is first used in this process.
          findAllCopy matching immutable products into a new list; absent/blank category means all.
          nextId = 4The next accepted POST receives id 4 in a fresh process.
          createBuild normalized Product data, store it at nextId and increment the counter.
          replaceCheck that the key exists; put a new Product at that same key. An absent key returns null.
          deleteRemove the key; return true if there was a value, false if absent.
          synchronizedProtect each shared store operation from overlapping threads; id selection/insertion are one operation.
          fromInputTrim accepted text, convert validated Integer price to int and construct immutable data.

          Resource methods validate before calling create/replace. An invalid request never reaches insertion or the counter increment. Deleted ids are not reused within this process. A replacement preserves the path id and list count. The check and replacement share the same lock, so a concurrent deletion cannot slip between them and recreate an entry.

          In memory means these values live in this running Java process. This Map is neither a JPA entity manager nor an H2 database. Restarting loses HTTP-created/changed/deleted data and reseeds ids 1–3. Session 4’s database restart proof has different behaviour. This local reference does not provide accounts, durable storage or production deployment.

          TRACE THE STATE ON PAPER

          Starting with keys 1, 2 and 3 and nextId 4: accept one POST, reject a blank-name POST, replace key 4, delete key 4, accept another POST. Write the keys and nextId after each step. The next created id is 5, not 4. Now restart and explain why id 4 is available again in a fresh process.

          7.6 Match method, route, body and response

          The baseline resource repeats Session 6’s read methods. Its @Path selects /products; @Produces offers JSON. @PathParam reads a path segment and @QueryParam reads a named query input. The read-only mode deliberately registers only this class:

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/ReadOnlyProductsResource.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

import java.util.Map;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.PathParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;

// A separate starting mode: no write methods are registered.
@Path("/products")
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class ReadOnlyProductsResource {
    @GET
    public Response list(@QueryParam("category") String category) {
        return Response.ok(ProductStore.findAll(category)).build();
    }
    @GET
    @Path("/{id}")
    public Response find(@PathParam("id") int id) {
        if (id < 1) {
            return Response.status(400).entity(Map.of("error", "id must be positive.")).build();
        }
        Product item = ProductStore.findById(id);
        if (item == null) {
            return Response.status(404).entity(Map.of("error", "Product " + id + " was not found.")).build();
        }
        return Response.ok(item).build();
    }
}

          The final resource keeps those GETs and adds three writes. Read the whole file once to see its shape, then study create, replace and delete in the next three blocks. Keep the imports and closing braces when typing it in your guided copy.

          src/main/java/com/aptech/session7/ProductResource.java
          package com.aptech.session7;

import java.net.URI;
import java.util.Map;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Consumes;
import jakarta.ws.rs.DELETE;
import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;
import jakarta.ws.rs.POST;
import jakarta.ws.rs.PUT;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;
import jakarta.ws.rs.PathParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;
import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Context;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;
import jakarta.ws.rs.core.UriInfo;

@Path("/products")
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class ProductResource {
    @GET
    public Response list(@QueryParam("category") String category) {
        return Response.ok(ProductStore.findAll(category)).build();
    }

    @GET
    @Path("/{id}")
    public Response find(@PathParam("id") int id) {
        if (id < 1) { return error(400, "id must be positive."); }
        Product item = ProductStore.findById(id);
        if (item == null) { return error(404, "Product " + id + " was not found."); }
        return Response.ok(item).build();
    }

    @POST
    @Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
    public Response create(ProductInput input, @Context UriInfo uriInfo) {
        String problem = ProductRules.validate(input);
        if (problem != null) { return error(400, problem); }
        Product saved = ProductStore.create(input);
        URI location = uriInfo.getAbsolutePathBuilder()
                .path(String.valueOf(saved.getId())).build();
        return Response.created(location).entity(saved).build();
    }

    @PUT
    @Path("/{id}")
    @Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
    public Response replace(@PathParam("id") int id, ProductInput input) {
        if (id < 1) { return error(400, "id must be positive."); }
        String problem = ProductRules.validate(input);
        if (problem != null) { return error(400, problem); }
        Product replacement = ProductStore.replace(id, input);
        if (replacement == null) { return error(404, "Product " + id + " was not found."); }
        return Response.ok(replacement).build();
    }

    @DELETE
    @Path("/{id}")
    public Response delete(@PathParam("id") int id) {
        if (id < 1) { return error(400, "id must be positive."); }
        if (!ProductStore.delete(id)) { return error(404, "Product " + id + " was not found."); }
        return Response.noContent().build();
    }

    private static Response error(int status, String message) {
        return Response.status(status).entity(Map.of("error", message)).build();
    }
}
          Annotation or helperJob
          @POSTMatch creation requests at the class’s /products path.
          @PUT plus @Path("/{id}")Match replacement requests with a target id.
          @DELETE plus @Path("/{id}")Match removal requests with a target id.
          @Consumes(APPLICATION_JSON)For POST/PUT, accept the JSON request-body format.
          @Produces(APPLICATION_JSON)Offer JSON responses, except a successful 204 has no body.
          ProductInput inputReceive the request body as mapped Java data.
          @Context UriInfoAsk Jersey for the current request address; use it to build Location.
          error(status, message)Build a status and structured Map error body once, then reuse it.

          Consumes describes the request body; Produces describes the response. The client’s Content-Type identifies what it sent; Accept asks for a response format. Headers do not create Java validation automatically. Our resource does not accept XML bodies, and asking for XML output gives 406.

          A helper method avoids repeating the same response-building code. error returns a Response and takes two parameters: an int status and String message. The Map’s error property is serialized by Jackson; it is not a handwritten JSON string. The resource handles HTTP concerns while ProductRules and ProductStore have separate jobs.

          TRACE BEFORE RUNNING

          For PUT /api/products/4 with a JSON body, circle the method annotation, underline the path parameter and point to the body parameter. Identify the rule check and the storage call. Explain why @PUT alone does not change a product until your Java method does the work.

          7.7 Create a product and follow its Location

          POST creates a new entry in this API. create first validates the mapped ProductInput. A problem returns 400 immediately; the store is not called. Otherwise the store assigns the next id, trims text and returns the immutable saved Product.

          UriInfo.getAbsolutePathBuilder() starts with this request’s collection address. Appending the saved id builds its item URL. Response.created(location) chooses 201 Created and sets the Location header. entity(saved) supplies the created product body. The Location tells the client where to GET this newly created item.

          Terminal A: switch to the full reference

          Stop read-only mode first, then start a fresh full server:

          .\gradlew.bat run

          Expect CRUD mode: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE. The reference contains all final methods, although you study them one at a time. If you edit source, save, stop A, rebuild/restart and remember that restart resets the Map.

          Terminal B: enter the project and inspect your file

          Set-Location "C:\Projects\Aptech\creating service\session7-jersey-crud"
          Get-Content examples/new-product.json
          examples/new-product.json
          {"name":"USB Hub","priceNgn":7500,"category":"Accessories"}
          curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products -H "Content-Type: application/json" -H "Accept: application/json" --data-binary "@examples/new-product.json"

          Use curl.exe explicitly on Windows, because older PowerShell aliases curl to a different command. -X POST selects the method; -H adds a header. --data-binary @file reads the file’s bytes as the request body. The @ here belongs to curl’s file syntax, not a Java annotation. Your current folder must contain examples/new-product.json.

          Expected important response on a fresh server
          HTTP/1.1 201 Created
Location: http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4
Content-Type: application/json

{"id":4,"name":"USB Hub","priceNgn":7500,"category":"Accessories"}

          Header order, case, date and JSON property order can differ. On a fresh full server the first accepted POST is id 4. If you already created entries, use the actual id and Location returned, rather than assuming 4. Sending the same POST again creates another product; this API does not deduplicate names.

          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4
          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products

          Use the returned Location when your id differs. Expect the new product in both GETs. A 201 body alone is weaker evidence than a subsequent read: checking the stored values catches a handler that returned data without saving it.

          GUIDED VARIATION

          Copy new-product.json to your own file such as examples/my-product.json. Type a fictional new name, a valid whole-naira price and category. Predict 201, send the file and GET its actual Location. Explain why you did not choose its id. Record the new id and use it in later commands if practising on that item.

          7.8 Replace an existing item without changing its id

          PUT selects the item through its URL. Our replace method checks positive id, validates all three incoming fields, and asks the store to replace an existing entry. A valid unknown positive id gives 404; the method does not create it. The body must describe all writable fields, not just whichever field you want to change.

          examples/updated-product.json
          {"name":"USB Hub Plus","priceNgn":8500,"category":"Accessories"}
          curl.exe -i -X PUT http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4 -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@examples/updated-product.json"

          This assumes id 4 was created in the preceding POST on the same running server. If you restarted, create it again first; if your chosen item has a different id, use it. Expect 200, id 4, name USB Hub Plus, priceNgn 8500 and category Accessories.

          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4
          curl.exe -i "http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products?category=Accessories"

          The id and collection size remain the same. The resource returns a new Product at the old key, so an immutable Product did not need setters. If you change category during replacement, the old-category filter should no longer include that item and the new-category filter should.

          REPEAT AND EXPLAIN

          Send the identical PUT twice and follow with GET. It should not add duplicates. A method is idempotent when repeating it has the same intended state effect as doing it once. Identical replacements leave the same item values. This is about state; status/body do not have to be identical for every possible repeated method.

          The Java method validates the mapped body before checking whether the positive id exists. Therefore valid data for 999 gives 404, while invalid data for 999 gives 400. Malformed JSON is rejected during mapping before the method runs. Document this order so you can explain a response rather than guessing.

          GUIDED VARIATION • FULL REPLACEMENT

          Create a request file with all three fields but a changed category such as Devices. Replace your created item, test the old and new category filters and prove the id is stable. Then try a body containing only priceNgn: predict 400 and verify that the earlier complete values remain. Restore the updated-product.json values for the deletion exercise.

          7.9 Delete an entry and interpret an empty response

          DELETE selects an id in the path and needs no request body. delete validates the numeric id, calls ProductStore.delete and distinguishes found versus absent. A successful removal returns Response.noContent().build(), which means 204 No Content. There must be no success JSON body attached to that 204.

          curl.exe -i -X DELETE http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4
          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4
          curl.exe -i -X DELETE http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/4
          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products

          Expect 204 with empty body, then 404 JSON from GET, then 404 JSON from the repeated DELETE. The final list no longer includes id 4. Our second DELETE reports the item was already absent, while the intended state remains “item absent”. This is consistent with DELETE’s idempotent state effect.

          Do not treat a blank 204 body as a server failure, and do not try to parse it as JSON. A 404 from GET after deletion is the expected proof. An id of 0/-1 returns our 400 rule; unconvertible path text is rejected by Jersey with 404 before the Java method runs.

          GUIDED CHANGE • CHECK THE CORRECT ITEM

          Create a second fictional product and note its Location. Delete only that id. Prove another product still exists, then explain why DELETE /products/2 and DELETE /products/4 have different targets even if list positions have changed. Do not invent an “array index” for an id.

          7.10 Reject bad input and prove the data did not change

          Use a fresh full server for a predictable test log. GET the list before a rejected POST; GET the target before a rejected PUT; capture the status/body; GET again. Compare ids, count and every relevant property. Test ordering matters: a request after restart sees a fresh Map, not the state before restart.

          examples/invalid-name.json
          {"name":"   ","priceNgn":7500,"category":"Accessories"}
          examples/invalid-price.json
          {"name":"USB Hub","priceNgn":-1,"category":"Accessories"}
          examples/missing-price.json
          {"name":"USB Hub","category":"Accessories"}
          examples/malformed.json
          {"name":"USB Hub","priceNgn":7500,"category":
          curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@examples/invalid-name.json"
          curl.exe -i -X PUT http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/2 -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@examples/invalid-price.json"
          curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@examples/malformed.json"
          curl.exe -i http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/2

          All three bad writes return 400. The blank name and negative price reach our application rules; malformed.json does not map into ProductInput. Product 2 should remain USB Mouse/6500/Accessories. Correct malformed.json in a new personal file, then prove valid data succeeds.

          Case to testExpected statusState evidence
          Valid POST, followed by GET Location201 then 200New positive id and normalized values appear exactly once.
          Valid PUT to existing item, repeated200 each timeSame id/count and final fields.
          Valid PUT to absent positive id404No new id is created.
          Invalid complete body for absent positive id400Validation rejects before the store lookup; no new item.
          Name lengths 1 and 60; category 1 and 30201/200 when other fields validThe allowed boundaries are accepted.
          Name 61 or category 31; blank/missing/null required text400No create/update occurs.
          Price 0 and 1000000201/200 when text validBoth inclusive endpoints are accepted.
          Price -1, 1000001, missing or null400No entry/counter change on POST; prior item intact on PUT.
          Quoted price, decimal price, numeric name or unknown id field400Mapping rejects before mutation.
          Malformed JSON, array, or second trailing JSON value400No mutation; framework error body may vary.
          POST/PUT with Content-Type: text/plain415 Unsupported Media TypeBody is not accepted as JSON.
          Accept: application/xml406 Not AcceptableThe request cannot use this JSON representation. Verify no mutation.
          Path id 0/-1400Explicit positive-id rule; no unwanted deletion/update.
          Path id abc or int overflow404Jersey path conversion fails; resource method not called.
          DELETE present, then GET and repeated DELETE204 then 404 then 404Body empty on success; item absent.
          PATCH at an item route405 Method Not AllowedNo PATCH method is registered.
          curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products -H "Content-Type: text/plain" --data-binary "@examples/new-product.json"
          curl.exe -i -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products -H "Content-Type: application/json" -H "Accept: application/xml" --data-binary "@examples/new-product.json"
          curl.exe -i -X PUT http://127.0.0.1:8099/api/products/999 -H "Content-Type: application/json" --data-binary "@examples/updated-product.json"

          Do not assume a framework rejection has our JSON error shape. Record the actual code and enough of its clue, then prove the state stayed unchanged. curl.exe can successfully receive HTTP 400/404; its own process exit code is not the HTTP code. Use -i to see the response.

          RESTART PROOF • MEMORY HAS LIMITS

          On a fresh server create id 4 and replace product 2 with visibly different values. Prove both changes. Stop A, start a fresh full server and GET again: product 4 gives 404 and product 2 returns the original USB Mouse/6500 data. Explain that HTTP mutation worked while the process lived, yet was not durable persistence. Save this evidence outside build.

          7.11 Trace a write request before revealing its result

          This browser aid describes the Java path. It does not run Java, send HTTP or modify the server’s Map. Predict each step; reveal it; locate the corresponding source; confirm with a real request.

            EXPLAIN WITHOUT THE TRACE

            Name the part that reads JSON, the method that validates it, the operation that changes storage and the line that selects the status. Explain why an invalid PUT must not alter the old Product. Then explain why DELETE has no ProductInput parameter.

            7.12 Try changed requirements without the completed solution

            Close the final resource while attempting each challenge. Write a plan and predicted tests first. Use a hint after a real attempt, record the help and repeat a changed version independently. Retest existing routes after a new route or rule.

            7.13 Find the layer that rejected or mishandled the request

            SymptomNext useful check
